MRPBA Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Neither scan gives an ionising dose, since fields, pulses and sound cannot ionise.): Ionising radiation must carry enough energy per photon or particle to remove electrons from atoms. Magnetic fields, radiofrequency pulses and sound waves lack that energy, so neither booked scan gives an ionising dose. The radiofrequency option is tempting because MRI uses pulses, the ultrasound option because both are imaging, and the low dose option because patients often assume all scans irradiate. Other MRI safety rules still apply.
  2. The correct answer is C (Outer electrons filling inner shell vacancies in tungsten release photons of one fixed, characteristic energy.): Characteristic radiation arises when an incoming electron removes an inner shell electron and an outer electron drops in, releasing a photon whose energy is typical of the element. Bremsstrahlung is the tempting answer but it gives the smooth continuous part of the spectrum. Filtration only removes low energy photons, and pair production needs photons above 1.022 MeV, far above 100 kVp.
  3. The correct answer is D (About 99 per cent becomes heat in the anode, and about 1 per cent becomes x-rays.): Only about 1 per cent of the electron energy becomes x-ray photons and about 99 per cent becomes heat, which is why anodes need a large heat capacity and exposure times are limited. The ten per cent figure sounds like a modest correction but overstates x-ray efficiency by an order of magnitude. A 60 per cent heat figure and the reversed bremsstrahlung option both overstate x-ray production, which is not what happens at diagnostic energies.
  4. The correct answer is A (Contrast improves as photoelectric absorption rises, but dose rises unless the mAs is cut.): Photoelectric absorption rises steeply as photon energy falls and with atomic number, so a lower kVp gives more difference between bone and soft tissue. The same lower energy beam is absorbed more in the patient, so dose rises unless the mAs is cut, which matters for a small child. The option claiming both better contrast and lower dose ignores that trade off. Compton scatter does not fall with energy as the claim about scatter suggests, and it does not drive contrast.
  5. The correct answer is C (Scatter from the patient, mostly Compton interactions, as the patient becomes the source of scatter.): In soft tissue across the diagnostic range the main interaction is Compton scatter, and the scattered photons leave the patient in many directions. That makes the patient the main source of staff exposure, which is why distance and shielding are used. Tube leakage is limited by housing design. Photoelectric absorption removes the photon rather than sending it onwards. The primary beam is collimated towards the receptor and away from the operator.
  6. The correct answer is D (Above 1.022 MeV, which is twice the 0.511 MeV rest energy of an electron.): Pair production turns a photon into an electron and a positron, so it needs twice the electron rest energy, 1.022 MeV. The 0.511 MeV value is what each annihilation photon carries afterwards, which makes it tempting. The 140 keV and 100 keV values sit far below the threshold, so pair production is relevant to megavoltage therapy beams and not to diagnostic imaging.
  7. The correct answer is B (30 µGy per hour, worked out as 240 × 0.5 × 0.5 × 0.5): The thickness of 1.5 mm is three times the 0.5 mm half-value layer, so the intensity is halved three times. That gives 240 → 120 → 60 → 30 µGy per hour, which equals 240 × 0.125. Dividing by 3 is tempting because the layers are treated as a simple divisor, and 120 or 60 come from stopping after one or two halvings.
  8. The correct answer is B (0.67 mGy per hour, worked out as 6 mGy per hour divided by 3 squared): For a point source the inverse square law applies, so the rate at 3 m is 6 × (1 ÷ 3)² = 6 ÷ 9, which is about 0.67 mGy per hour. Dividing by 3 is tempting because distance has tripled, but that treats the fall as linear. Dividing by 27 applies a cube that does not describe a point source. Multiplying by 3 sends the rate in the wrong direction as the nurse moves away.
  9. The correct answer is A (About 850 MBq, worked out as 1200 × 0.5^0.5): Three hours is 0.5 of the 6 hour half-life, so the remaining fraction is 0.5^0.5 = 0.7071 and 1200 × 0.7071 = 849 MBq, about 850 MBq. A value of 900 is tempting because it treats decay as a straight line, but decay is exponential. The 600 figure would need a full 6 hours to pass. Dividing by three has no physical basis for radioactive decay.
  10. The correct answer is D (At 06:00 the next morning, which is 18 hours or three half-lives later.): Going from 800 to 100 MBq needs three halvings: 800 to 400, 200 and 100, which is 3 × 6 = 18 hours after noon, so 06:00 next morning. Two half-lives leave 200 MBq, four leave 50 MBq and one leaves 400 MBq. Each option names the wrong number of halvings.
  11. The correct answer is D (Alpha particles are stopped by skin, but they ionise densely and harm tissue if taken inside.): An alpha particle is a heavy, doubly charged helium nucleus that is stopped by paper or the dead outer layer of skin, so the probe reads little and external exposure is minor. If the material is inhaled, ingested or absorbed, the particle deposits its energy over a very short path and ionises densely, so tissue damage is severe. Long range in air and deep penetration describe gamma rays or beta particles, not alpha. Thermal injury is not the mechanism, because the harm comes from ionisation.
  12. The correct answer is C (It is a metastable state that decays to technetium-99 by isomeric transition, releasing a gamma photon.): The m stands for metastable, a long lived excited nuclear state that drops to technetium-99 by isomeric transition and emits the 140 keV gamma photon. The link to molybdenum-99 is real because the generator produces it, but the m does not mean mother. Electron capture and beta-plus emission change the proton number, whereas isomeric transition does not. Two 511 keV photons are the signature of positron annihilation in PET agents.
  13. The correct answer is A (40 mSv, worked out as 2 mGy multiplied by the weighting factor of 20): Equivalent dose is absorbed dose multiplied by the radiation weighting factor, so 2 mGy × 20 = 40 mSv. A result of 0.1 mSv is tempting if the operation is reversed and 2 mGy is divided by 20. A result of 2 mSv comes from treating the weighting factor as 1, which is only true for photons and electrons. A result of 20 mSv confuses the factor itself with the dose.
  14. The correct answer is B (About 3.3 mSv, the sum of each tissue dose multiplied by its own weighting factor.): For photons wR is 1, so mGy equals mSv. Lung 20 × 0.12 = 2.4, breast 4 × 0.12 = 0.48 and thyroid 10 × 0.04 = 0.4. The sum is 2.4 + 0.48 + 0.4 = 3.28, about 3.3 mSv. Adding raw doses gives 34. Using 0.12 for all three tissues gives 4.08. Dropping the breast gives 2.4 + 0.4 = 2.8, which skips a tissue that carries weight.
  15. The correct answer is C (Kerma area product, which relates better to the total energy delivered to the patient.): Kerma area product is air kerma multiplied by beam area and relates to the total energy delivered to the patient, so it is recorded for audit and optimisation. Effective dose is not read from a machine display and is not an exact risk for one person. Equivalent dose uses the radiation weighting factor, not tissue weighting. Absorbed dose is per kilogram and does not include beam area.
  16. The correct answer is B (No, dose limits do not apply to patients; justification and optimisation govern a justified examination.): Dose limits protect workers and members of the public from exposures that bring no personal benefit. A patient having a justified examination is protected by the principles of justification, where benefit outweighs risk, and optimisation, where dose is as low as reasonably achievable for the clinical question. The 20 mSv average belongs to occupational limits, and 50 mSv is the occupational single year figure.
  17. The correct answer is D (Effective dose, which suits comparison but is not her exact risk.): Effective dose sums equivalent dose multiplied by tissue weighting factors across tissues, which lets different procedures be compared in rough terms for stochastic risk. It is not intended to give one person's exact risk. Absorbed dose is physical and ignores tissue sensitivity. Air kerma is a measure in air. A weighting factor is a multiplier, not a quantity of dose.
  18. The correct answer is A (Alpha particles ionise densely, so they cause more biological damage per gray.): Linear energy transfer is the energy left per unit path length. Alpha particles ionise densely, so for the same absorbed dose they do more biological harm, which is why wR is 20. The question already fixes the absorbed dose as equal. Alpha particles are stopped by skin, so they are less penetrating. Dense ionisation makes direct action more common for alpha, not indirect.
  19. The correct answer is A (About two thirds, because radiation ionises water and the resulting free radicals attack the DNA.): For x-rays and gamma rays, about two thirds of DNA damage is thought to be indirect: radiation ionises water, making free radicals that attack DNA. Direct action is more common for densely ionising radiation such as alpha particles, which makes the claim of one third tempting. Direct hits on DNA do occur for photons, so it is not true that indirect action is the only route.
  20. The correct answer is C (Bone marrow stem cells, because they divide quickly and are unspecialised.): The law of Bergonié and Tribondeau says cells are more radiosensitive when they divide rapidly and are unspecialised, and marrow stem cells fit both. The thyroid is sensitive to radioiodine because of uptake, but that is a different mechanism from this law. Nerve cells are specialised and rarely divide, which makes them more resistant. Muscle is active but divides little, so activity and mitochondria do not predict sensitivity.
  21. The correct answer is C (Well oxygenated cells are more radiosensitive, so low oxygen regions are more resistant to radiation therapy.): Oxygen fixes radiation induced DNA damage, so well oxygenated cells are more sensitive and the hypoxic core of a poorly perfused tumour is more resistant. Poor perfusion limits drug delivery, but photons reach the tumour regardless of its blood supply. Late S phase is actually the most resistant part of the cycle, so that reversal fails. Faster repair in resting cells is not the principle, because the oxygen effect is about damage fixation rather than repair speed.
  22. The correct answer is B (Fractions give normal tissue time to repair between treatments, sparing it more than the tumour.): Radiation therapy exploits differences between tumour and normal tissue by giving many small fractions, which lets normal tissue repair between them. Reoxygenation may help, but it does not remove the need for the dose. The total dose is usually not lowered by fractionation. Late S phase is the most resistant phase of the cycle, not the most sensitive.
  23. The correct answer is A (A deterministic effect with a threshold: severity rises with dose, far above diagnostic levels here.): Skin reddening is a tissue reaction, which is a deterministic effect. It has a threshold in the order of grays, and above it the severity rises with dose, so long repeated fluoroscopy can produce it. Stochastic effects such as cancer have probability, not severity, rising with dose and are assumed to have no threshold. Ordinary radiographs are far below the skin threshold.
  24. The correct answer is D (Stochastic effects are assumed to have no threshold, so risk rises in proportion to dose.): Radiation protection uses the linear no threshold model, in which the probability of stochastic effects such as cancer is assumed to rise in proportion to dose with no safe level. That is why dose is kept as low as reasonably achievable even far below any tissue reaction threshold. Severity of a stochastic effect does not grow with dose, only its probability does. Medical exposures are exempt from limits, but that is why optimisation matters, and it is not restricted to doses above a threshold. Tissue reactions have thresholds that a single low dose does not approach.
  25. The correct answer is A (Withhold the exposure and tell the referring practitioner or radiologist, who judges whether it is justified.): For an examination that exposes the pelvis, a possible pregnancy means the radiographer pauses and informs the referring practitioner or radiologist, who decides whether to proceed, delay or use a method without ionising radiation such as ultrasound. An apron on the abdomen does not help when the primary beam includes the pelvis. Staff do not cancel alone, and consent does not replace the justification decision.
  26. The correct answer is C (It is far below the 100 mGy malformation threshold, so dose alone does not justify termination.): Malformation is a deterministic effect with a threshold commonly cited as about 100 mGy to the foetus, so 20 mGy is well below it, and dose below that is not by itself a reason to recommend termination. A small added risk of childhood cancer is a stochastic effect assumed to have no threshold, so it cannot be called zero. The radiographer gives calm information per policy and directs her to the referrer.
  27. The correct answer is B (Late G2 and mitosis are most sensitive, and late S phase is most resistant.): Cells are most sensitive in late G2 and mitosis and most resistant in late S phase, where repair by homologous recombination is most active. Reversing the two ends of the cycle is the usual slip. Naming early G1 as the most sensitive phase has no support, and treating G2 as resistant ignores that it is next to mitosis and sensitive. This phase dependence is one reason fractionated therapy redistributes tumour cells into sensitive phases between doses.
  28. The correct answer is D (Within the limit, worked out as 80 mSv ÷ 5 years = 16 mSv): The occupational limit is an effective dose of 20 mSv a year averaged over five years, with no more than 50 mSv in any single year. The five doses add to 12 + 15 + 22 + 18 + 13 = 80 mSv, and 80 ÷ 5 = 16 mSv, which is below 20 mSv, while the highest year of 22 mSv is well under 50 mSv. Comparing the 80 mSv total with 50 mSv mixes a cumulative figure with a single year ceiling. Using 22 mSv against 20 mSv ignores averaging, and the 1 mSv figure belongs to members of the public.
  29. The correct answer is B (The photoelectric effect, because absorption rises steeply with atomic number and barium absorbs many diagnostic photons.): The photoelectric effect depends roughly on the cube of the atomic number, so barium absorbs far more than soft tissue and appears bright. Compton scatter depends on electron density, not atomic number, and its slow fall with energy does not create the contrast. Pair production needs more than 1.022 MeV and is not relevant to diagnostic imaging.
  30. The correct answer is B (Origin only: x-rays come from the electron cloud or slowing electrons, gamma rays from the nucleus.): X-rays and gamma rays are the same kind of photon and differ only in where they come from, since x-rays arise in the electron cloud or from fast electrons slowing down and gamma rays arise in the nucleus. Penetration and energy both depend on the photon energy, and the ranges overlap, so neither defines the type. Many gamma emitters are made artificially in reactors and generators, so the natural versus machine split fails.
  31. The correct answer is A (Limits cover workers and the public, while patient doses are controlled by justification and optimisation.): A limit caps the risk for people who gain no direct benefit from an exposure, so it applies to workers and members of the public. A patient receives a clinical benefit, so the dose is managed by justifying the examination and optimising the technique. A limit that lifts when a doctor refers sounds practical, but there is no patient limit to lift. Applying a 1 mSv figure to a patient would make most examinations unlawful. The public is also covered by a limit, with shielding supporting that limit rather than replacing it.
  32. The correct answer is D (Ask the referrer or radiologist for the missing clinical information first.): Justification begins with adequate clinical information, and the practitioner's role is to notice a gap and ask. Performing the series and noting it afterwards delivers the dose before the question is answered. Refusing outright is too blunt because the request may be entirely appropriate once clarified. Trimming views on personal judgement risks a non-diagnostic study and a repeat.
  33. The correct answer is C (Choose the lowest dose protocol that still confirms alignment in a small patient.): Optimisation means using the lowest dose that still answers the clinical question, here a bony match in a small patient, so the protocol is chosen to suit the patient. Keeping a high dose protocol for image quality gives more than the task needs. Skipping imaging cuts dose but gives up the check that protects the treatment. Narrowing the field helps, but leaving settings for a larger patient still delivers more dose than needed.
  34. The correct answer is B (13.3 µGy per hour, worked out as 120 µGy per hour ÷ 3²): Moving from 1 m to 3 m triples the distance, so the dose rate falls by the square of 3, which is 9. The working is 120 ÷ 9 = 13.3 µGy per hour. Dividing by 3 is tempting because it treats the fall as linear, and dividing by 4 borrows the factor that belongs to doubling the distance. Multiplying moves the rate in the wrong direction.
  35. The correct answer is C (10 µSv per hour, worked out as 160 ÷ 2⁴ for four layers): Four half value layers reduce the rate by a factor of 2⁴, which is 16. The working is 160 → 80 → 40 → 20 → 10 µSv per hour, and 160 ÷ 16 = 10 confirms it. Dividing by 4 is tempting because the number of layers is mistaken for the attenuation factor, while 20 and 80 come from stopping too early.
  36. The correct answer is A (Acrylic first, because lead would produce extra X-rays with beta particles.): Beta particles produce bremsstrahlung when they strike a high atomic number material, so the inner layer should be low atomic number, such as acrylic, with lead outside to absorb any x-rays that do form. Putting lead first creates the secondary x-rays the senior technologist wants to avoid. Calling bremsstrahlung negligible ignores that yttrium-90 betas are energetic. Thin foil is too thin to stop them, so the dose to hands stays high.
  37. The correct answer is D (Yes, limit met, worked out as 100 mSv divided by 5 years equals 20 mSv): The five year sum is 12 + 28 + 16 + 20 + 24 = 100 mSv, and 100 ÷ 5 = 20 mSv per year, which meets the averaging requirement. The highest single year is 28 mSv, well below the 50 mSv cap. Comparing the total of 100 mSv with 20 mSv mixes a five year quantity with an annual one. Treating 20 mSv as a yearly ceiling overlooks that the framework averages over five years. Checking only the 50 mSv cap is also tempting, but the average must hold as well.
  38. The correct answer is C (A dose constraint set for one source or task): A dose constraint is a planning value below the limit, applied to one source or task so that added contributions from several sources still keep a worker under the limit. An investigation level is also set below a limit, but it triggers a review after doses are recorded rather than shaping the design beforehand. A diagnostic reference level compares patient doses in imaging. The public limit is a legal cap for other people, not a target chosen for one source.
  39. The correct answer is A (Each state and territory has its own regulator, so she must learn local licence requirements.): ARPANSA publishes national codes and standards, but licensing, notification and enforcement sit with each state and territory regulator, so requirements differ and she must learn the Queensland ones. National registration through AHPRA is real but covers professional registration, not radiation use licences. Regulators adopt the national codes with local variation, so the claim of no variation fails. Treating premises and people as separate in this way is also wrong.
  40. The correct answer is D (The badge may record dose from the unit that she never received.): A dosimeter records whatever radiation reaches it, so leaving it beside the unit adds dose that she never received to her record. Badges belong in a low radiation area when off duty. Saying it only needs wearing during procedures is partly true but ignores where it is kept. A dosimeter does not record only while worn, and storage near equipment does not void calibration, since the issue is added dose.
  41. The correct answer is B (Report the result and circumstances now so the cause can be checked.): An unexpected high reading is reported promptly, because the cause may be a real exposure, a badge left in a beam or contamination, and only a check can decide. Waiting hides a possible overexposure and the person may not be the one best placed to assess it. Stopping work assumes the reading is real without evidence. Adjusting her own record is not her role and breaks the integrity of the dose record.
  42. The correct answer is A (Tell the responsible medical practitioner and let them decide the next step.): The radiographer neither proceeds alone nor simply refuses; the possibility of pregnancy returns the justification question to the responsible medical practitioner. They can choose to scan, defer, or use ultrasound or MRI. A reduced dose may spoil the study and is not the radiographer's decision to make alone. Sending the patient away may delay urgent care. A shield in the beam does not remove the scatter and is not current practice.
  43. The correct answer is D (Her duties are reviewed so the foetal dose is controlled, without an automatic ban.): A declared pregnancy triggers a review by the employer so the foetal dose stays low under local legislation and policy, without an automatic ban. Stopping all radiation work for the whole pregnancy is the usual overstatement. Working unchanged until a doctor certifies a problem skips the employer's duty to review. Staying below the worker limit is not enough, because the foetus has a lower dose limit than the worker.
  44. The correct answer is C (Explain the risk simply, provide an apron and stand them away from the primary beam.): A comforter helps knowingly and willingly, so the radiographer explains the risk, offers protective clothing and keeps the person out of the primary beam. Aprons and gloves do not make the beam safe to stand in. Staff should not routinely hold patients when a willing carer is available. Skipping the apron ignores scatter, which still reaches a person close to the patient.
  45. The correct answer is A (Use a size-based protocol, the smallest field covering the anatomy, and good immobilisation.): Children are more radiosensitive and have more years for late effects, so settings follow patient size and the field is limited to the anatomy of interest. Good immobilisation prevents motion blur and the repeat that would double the dose. A wide field adds dose to tissue that is not needed, and leaving a child unrestrained risks a repeat. Adult settings with a shorter time are not an equivalent size based protocol.
  46. The correct answer is B (A review benchmark for typical doses, not a limit for any individual patient.): A diagnostic reference level comes from national survey data and is compared with a department's typical doses for a standard sized patient. Larger patients may need more dose, so it cannot be a ceiling. It is not a target either, because dose follows patient size and clinical need. It has nothing to do with occupational limits that apply to staff.
  47. The correct answer is D (Review the protocol, since a dose far below the benchmark may give poor images.): Optimisation means low enough while still answering the clinical question. Poor images can lead to repeats and missed findings, so a very low dose also calls for review. Lowering further would worsen the problem. Ignoring below-benchmark results misses half of the purpose of the tool. Setting all patients to one value ignores size, so the benchmark is not applied as a target.
  48. The correct answer is C (Pulsed mode at a low frame rate, with last image hold instead of live screening.): Pulsed mode at a low frame rate reduces the number of pulses, and last image hold lets the operator review without further exposure. The detector should sit close to the patient and the tube far from the patient to reduce skin dose. Moving the detector away increases dose even in pulsed mode. A tube close to the patient raises skin dose, and continuous live screening raises time and scatter even with tight collimation.
  49. The correct answer is B (Adapt kV and tube current to the child's size and scan only the range needed.): Optimisation in CT means tailoring kV and tube current to the child's size and limiting the range and phases to what the clinical question needs. Reducing tube current alone while scanning the full adult range keeps unneeded dose. A second phase adds dose without answering a new question. Raising tube current to cut noise increases dose when a size adjusted protocol already gives adequate quality.
  50. The correct answer is A (Use an activity based on weight or guideline tables, not a flat adult amount.): Activity for a child is scaled to body weight or taken from recognised guideline tables, and kept as low as reasonably achievable for diagnostic quality. A flat fraction of the adult amount ignores the real size of this 22 kg child. Copying the amount given to another child ignores differences in weight. Accepting a prescribed amount unchecked is tempting but the practitioner must verify it against guidance.
  51. The correct answer is D (Explain that routine shielding rarely lowers dose and can cause repeats.): A calm, evidence based explanation respects the parent's concern while following current department policy. Routine shielding rarely lowers dose meaningfully and a shield can hide anatomy or disturb automatic exposure control, causing repeats. Describing shielding as a legal duty is incorrect. Brushing the parent off gives a true point but offers no real communication, and placing the shield just to please her ignores the policy.
  52. The correct answer is C (Report the near miss through the incident system, since it shows where a check failed.): Reporting a near miss through the facility incident system is expected practice because it shows where a check failed before harm occurred. Staff learn from the report and the system can be improved. A verbal mention leaves no trace for review. Writing it in the patient's record misplaces it, and staying silent because nothing was exposed loses the learning.
  53. The correct answer is A (Make the patient safe, tell the practitioner and safety officer, then record.): The patient comes first, then the responsible practitioner and radiation safety officer are told promptly and the event is recorded honestly. The facility then reports externally where required. Going to the regulator first reverses the order and leaves the local team uninformed. Telling only the patient omits the people who must act, and waiting for symptoms delays action on a known dose error.
  54. The correct answer is C (The patient is told promptly and openly what happened, with an apology.): Open disclosure means the patient hears promptly, honestly and in person what happened, with an expression of regret and a plan for what follows. Waiting until every fact is checked delays something the patient is entitled to hear early, and a written summary can follow. A legal letter replaces the human conversation. Limiting the apology to serious harm departs from the framework, which includes regret for any incident.
  55. The correct answer is B (Warn others, then contain the spill with absorbent material from the edges.): The first steps are to warn others and contain the spill, working inwards from the edges so the contaminated area does not grow. Decontamination and monitoring follow, and the radiation safety officer is notified. Wiping at once without warning others leaves people exposed. Dealing with the glove alone leaves the spill spreading, and working from the centre outward widens it.
  56. The correct answer is D (About 75 MBq, worked out as 1200 ÷ 2^4 (16)): Twenty four hours divided by a 6 hour half-life gives 4 half-lives. The activity is halved four times, 1200 → 600 → 300 → 150 → 75 MBq, which matches 1200 ÷ 16. Each of the other values comes from counting one, two or three halvings, which is easy to do when the steps are tallied quickly. The disposal decision still needs monitoring and a record under the licence conditions.
  57. The correct answer is B (Give written advice on time and distance from children and pregnant people.): A patient still holding radioactivity needs clear written instructions on time and distance from children and pregnant people for the period set by the department. Limiting advice to the partner leaves the vulnerable household members out. Verbal advice relayed by a tired patient is easily lost. Holding the patient until all activity has gone is impractical and not required.
  58. The correct answer is A (Limits do not apply to patients; a justified treatment's benefit is large.): Occupational and public limits protect people who gain no direct benefit from exposure, so they do not apply to patients receiving justified treatment. Patient dose is managed by justification and optimisation. Averaging a limit over a course, applying the public limit or splitting the body into tumour and non tumour tissue all wrongly treat the limit as relevant to the prescription.
  59. The correct answer is C (Stand back behind a barrier, keep exposure short, and wear an apron if in the room.): Staff protection uses time, distance and shielding together, and removing the need to be close where possible. Standing back behind a barrier, using a short exposure and wearing an apron when in the room applies all three. An apron reduces dose but does not remove it, so staying close is poor practice. Wearing it only for long exposures ignores that scatter occurs with every exposure, and the foot of the bed remains close to the scatter source.
  60. The correct answer is C (A lead apron suits diagnostic X-ray energies, not source shielding here.): Lead aprons are made for diagnostic X-ray energies and do not stop the more penetrating photons from many radionuclides. Proper source shielding, distance and time are the main controls in the injection area. Plastic is indeed used for beta emitters, but that does not make an apron suitable for gamma emitters. Short use does not change what the apron material can absorb.
  61. The correct answer is B (It removes the need for staff to be near the source during treatment.): Remote loading removes the need for staff to be near the source during treatment, which is the strongest form of control. The source stays radioactive, so the room still needs full shielding. Staff may still be monitored, as the device does not replace personal dosimetry. It works by distance, not by speeding up manual loading.
  62. The correct answer is D (About 5 µSv per hour, worked out as 5000 divided by 10 cubed): Each tenth value layer cuts the dose rate to one tenth, so three layers give a factor of 10 × 10 × 10 = 1000. The expected rate is 5000 ÷ 1000 = 5 µSv per hour. Dividing by 3 is tempting because it uses the layer count as the attenuation factor, giving 1667. Dividing by 10 or 100 stops after one or two layers and leaves the rate too high.
  63. The correct answer is A (Ask her to say her own full name and date of birth, then check the request.): She is hard of hearing and may agree to anything said to her, so the nod proves nothing. Asking her to state her own full name and date of birth gives a genuine check that can be matched against the request. The clerk's check-in and the daughter's word are tempting shortcuts, but identification must be done by the person delivering the procedure, directly with the patient.
  64. The correct answer is D (Ask her privately about her last period and possible pregnancy, and record the answer.): The person performing the examination asks every time for anyone who could be pregnant, privately and in plain words, and records the answer. Early pregnancy cannot be seen and the woman may not know. Asking in the waiting room breaks privacy. A silent request form or a wall notice is not a substitute for a direct question.
  65. The correct answer is D (Pause the scan and put the question to the justifying practitioner for review of alternatives.): Pregnancy is possible and the scan is not urgent, so the justification must be reviewed by the justifying practitioner, who may choose ultrasound or MRI instead. Shielding and lower settings do not replace that decision, and a waiver does not make an unjustified exposure acceptable. The practitioner at the bench is the one who noticed, so the duty to escalate falls to you.
  66. The correct answer is B (Voluntary, informed, specific to the procedure, and given by a person who has capacity.): Valid consent is voluntary, informed, specific to the procedure and given by a person with capacity. Written forms and witnesses are useful records but do not make consent valid. A relative cannot consent for a capable patient. A single signature before the course cannot cover changes that were not discussed, since consent is specific and can be withdrawn.
  67. The correct answer is C (Cooperation may imply consent for the radiograph, but the contrast injection needs expressed consent.): Routine imaging with a clear explanation can rest on implied consent shown by cooperation. An injection of contrast carries real risk, so consent is expressed and often written, after the material risks are explained. The referrer's request and the patient's attendance do not stand in for his own agreement.
  68. The correct answer is A (Stop and raise the capacity concern with the treating team so valid consent can be arranged.): Capacity is presumed, but his confusion about where he is gives a real reason to doubt it. When capacity is in doubt, the practitioner stops and raises it with the treating team, because consent must come from him if he has capacity, or from a person authorised under the law of the state or territory if he does not. A daughter's assurance or a signature from a person who does not understand is not valid consent.
  69. The correct answer is D (Arrange a qualified interpreter, face to face, by phone or by video, and document its use.): A relative may leave things out, add personal views or hesitate to translate bad news, so a qualified interpreter is the safe choice for consent discussions. Services such as TIS National and state health interpreter services can be used by phone or video, and the use is documented. An untrained bystander or an unexplained handout carries the same risks.
  70. The correct answer is A (Use the phone interpreter, accepting the delay, and do not use the child to interpret.): A child must not be used as an interpreter for a consent discussion, even for part of it, because the content is distressing and the child may soften or omit it. An adult relative is also not a safe substitute. The delay of fifteen minutes is a minor cost compared with consent that is not informed.
  71. The correct answer is B (Follow local policy and escalate to the supervisor or referrer before the injection.): An older child who understands the procedure may be able to consent for herself, but the rules differ between states and territories. When unsure, the practitioner follows the local policy and escalates rather than guessing. Her understanding is relevant, yet it does not settle the legal question for you.
  72. The correct answer is C (Proceed under the emergency exception to save life, and document the reasons.): When a patient cannot consent and no one can be asked, treatment necessary to save life or prevent serious harm may go ahead under the emergency exception. Waiting for relatives or recovery would put him at risk of the very harm the scan is meant to find. The reasons for proceeding are recorded.
  73. The correct answer is D (A risk that a reasonable person in the patient's position would want to know about.): A risk is material when a reasonable person in the patient's position would want to know about it, even if it is uncommon. The test looks to the patient, not to the practitioner's own opinion. Severity is relevant but a milder, more likely risk can still be material. Frequency alone fails because a rare risk can matter greatly.
  74. The correct answer is B (He may be deteriorating, as the beta blocker can hide a fast pulse, so escalate.): A beta blocker can stop the heart rate rising, so a normal pulse is falsely reassuring here. The raised respiratory rate, low saturation, soft blood pressure and his statement that he feels strange together point to deterioration. The pattern, compared with his baseline, calls for escalation rather than waiting or treating one number.
  75. The correct answer is A (A rising respiratory rate, which is easy to miss because it is rarely counted.): A rising respiratory rate is one of the earliest and most reliable signs and is also the one most often not counted. Heart rate and blood pressure can be masked by medicines or held up by compensation. Counting breaths takes about a minute and should not be skipped.
  76. The correct answer is C (Stay with her and call for help using the local escalation pathway.): Drowsiness with fast breathing signals deterioration, so the first step is to stay with her and call for help through the local escalation pathway. Finishing the scan keeps her on the table while she worsens. Calling the ward nurse and carrying on positioning splits attention. Stopping and recording first is sensible but reporting afterwards still delays help.
  77. The correct answer is D (Call a rapid response or medical emergency review, because serious worry is itself a valid trigger.): Services allow a rapid response call when criteria are met or when anyone is seriously worried. His appearance and statement are enough, and waiting to see if things settle risks missing a sudden decline. Starting with a call to the nurse in charge is reasonable only if it is immediate, not deferred to a convenient time.
  78. The correct answer is B (Give intramuscular adrenaline into the mid-thigh, stop the injection and call for help.): Her skin, airway, breathing and circulation are all involved, which is anaphylaxis, and the first treatment is intramuscular adrenaline in the outer mid-thigh. Antihistamines and steroids are not a substitute, and delay in giving adrenaline is the most common serious error. The trigger is stopped and help is called alongside the injection.
  79. The correct answer is A (0.24 mg, which is 0.24 mL of the 1 mg per mL solution.): Dose = 0.01 mg per kg × 24 kg = 0.24 mg. The solution contains 1 mg in each mL, so 0.24 mg ÷ 1 mg per mL = 0.24 mL. A check by multiplying back, 0.24 mL × 1 mg per mL = 0.24 mg, confirms it. The adult dose of 0.5 mg is too large for this child, and the other two answers are decimal slips.
  80. The correct answer is C (Lie her on her left side while treatment, including adrenaline, is given as protocol allows.): A person with breathing difficulty may sit up, but a pregnant patient is placed on her left side so the uterus does not compress the large veins and lower her blood pressure further. Lying flat on her back is the standard position for others but not for her. Standing or walking a person with anaphylaxis can cause cardiac arrest.
  81. The correct answer is A (Send for help by calling the emergency number or the hospital code blue team.): The sequence is Danger, Response, Send for help, Airway, Breathing, CPR, Defibrillation. Having checked danger and response, the next step is to send for help so that more people and equipment are on the way. The other options are correct steps but come later in the sequence.
  82. The correct answer is C (Thirty compressions to two breaths, at 100 to 120 compressions per minute.): ANZCOR guidance for adult CPR is 30 compressions followed by two breaths, delivered at 100 to 120 compressions per minute to about one third of the chest depth. Interruptions are kept as short as possible. The other options each change one of the two numbers.
  83. The correct answer is B (Attach it as soon as it arrives and follow its prompts, pausing compressions briefly.): Early defibrillation improves survival, so the AED is attached as soon as it arrives. It analyses the rhythm and instructs rescuers, so no doctor or pulse check is needed to start. Compressions are interrupted only for pad placement and analysis.
  84. The correct answer is A (Wear a P2 or N95 respirator and use a negative pressure room if one is available.): Tuberculosis stays in the air, so airborne precautions are added: a P2 or N95 respirator and a negative pressure room where available. A surgical mask suits droplet infections such as influenza, and gown and gloves suit contact infections such as MRSA. Airborne organisms can be inhaled without any physical contact.
  85. The correct answer is D (Wash your hands with soap and water for about 40 to 60 seconds.): Soap and water is used after contact with C. difficile, even when hands look clean, because the spores resist alcohol. Alcohol rub is the preferred method only for other situations where hands are not visibly soiled. Gloves reduce contamination but do not replace hand hygiene.
  86. The correct answer is D (Before putting on gloves, because connecting to a cannula is a clean or aseptic procedure.): The 5 Moments include before a clean or aseptic procedure, and connecting to a cannula is one. Hands are cleaned with alcohol rub before gloving, since you have already touched surfaces around the patient. Gloves do not replace hand hygiene, and cleaning only afterwards leaves the patient exposed.
  87. The correct answer is D (Guide him gently to the floor, protect his head and call for help.): Practitioners do not attempt to catch a falling patient, because this injures both the person and the staff member. The safe action is to guide him down, protect his head and call for help. Holding him upright or moving a table toward him can cause a harder fall.
  88. The correct answer is B (Lower the table, lock the wheels, and let her sit at the edge first.): Dizziness from sedatives or low blood pressure on standing is a known fall factor. Lowering the table, locking the wheels and allowing a minute seated at the edge reduces that risk. An at-risk patient should not be left alone on a high table.
  89. The correct answer is B (Keep her still until she is assessed for injury, then report the event.): After any fall the patient is not moved until she has been assessed for injury, and hip pain raises the possibility of a fracture. Her wish to continue does not change this. The event is reported, and the wet floor is made safe.
  90. The correct answer is A (Pause, ask the nurse or doctor about analgesia within protocol, and support the limb.): Severe pain is the cause of the movement, so the right move is to stop, involve the nurse or doctor about analgesia under local protocol, and support the limb meanwhile. Pillows and a sling help comfort but do not treat pain, so blur may persist. A shorter exposure reduces blur only partly and ignores the patient's distress. Naming a specific drug is outside the radiographer's role.
  91. The correct answer is C (Report the sudden change to the nurse or doctor as possible delirium before continuing.): Dementia is a slow decline, whereas delirium is a sudden change over hours to days, often with infection or pain. A person with dementia can still develop delirium, so a change from usual behaviour must be reported. Restraint is a last resort that must be authorised and documented.
  92. The correct answer is D (Call for medical help urgently and do not increase the flow rate.): Some patients with long term lung disease depend on a lower oxygen target. Unusual drowsiness after oxygen is a signal to call for medical help, not to turn the flow up. Oxygen is a medicine given under an order, so adjusting it is not the practitioner's decision.
  93. The correct answer is C (Stop, do not inject contrast, and report the possible cannula problem to the clinician.): A line that will not flush easily, or that causes pain, risks extravasation, which is leakage of contrast into the surrounding tissue. Injection is stopped and the problem is reported. Cannulation is done only by practitioners trained and authorised under their employer's scope of practice.
  94. The correct answer is C (Time analgesia before the scan, position him comfortably, and allow family nearby.): Because the result will change his pain care, the scan has a clear purpose. Comfort and dignity guide how it is done: pain relief timed before the procedure, comfortable positioning, the shortest approach that meets the aim and a family member close by. The advance care directive is respected throughout.
  95. The correct answer is B (Withhold the injection and report the missing eGFR result to the radiologist.): The protocol requires a recent eGFR, so the missing result is the stem detail that decides this case and the gap must be raised before any injection. Injecting as booked is tempting because of the scheduling pressure, but a booking does not replace a safety check. Stopping metformin or cutting the volume feels protective, yet both are clinical decisions that belong to the radiologist under local protocol. Escalating first lets the clinician choose to check kidney function, change the study or defer it.
  96. The correct answer is B (They do not split into charged particles in solution, so side effects are less likely.): Non-ionic agents stay whole in solution instead of splitting into charged particles, which is why they cause fewer side effects and are the standard for intravascular use. The idea that non-ionic means more iodine is tempting because the word sounds like a strength, but concentration is written separately in mgI/mL. Higher osmolality actually causes more pain, warmth and blood pressure change, so lower osmolality products are preferred. Contrast is cleared by the kidneys, so kidney checks remain necessary.
  97. The correct answer is A (The injection delivers 25.6 g of iodine in total.): Total iodine is concentration multiplied by volume: 320 mg/mL × 80 mL = 25,600 mg. Dividing by 1,000 to convert to grams gives 25.6 g, and the check 320 × 8 = 2,560, then × 10 = 25,600, agrees. The values 256 g and 2.56 g come from a decimal slip in the conversion. The value 0.25 g comes from dividing 80 by 320, which has no meaning here.
  98. The correct answer is D (40 seconds, worked out as 120 mL divided by 3 mL/s): The weight based volume is 1.5 mL/kg × 85 kg = 127.5 mL, which exceeds the 120 mL maximum, so 120 mL is the planned dose. Injection time is volume ÷ rate = 120 mL ÷ 3 mL/s = 40 s, and 3 mL/s × 40 s = 120 mL confirms it. The 42.5 s value is tempting because it keeps the uncapped volume, but that breaks the protocol limit. Dividing the weight by the rate has no physical meaning, and a 4 mL/s rate is not part of the stated protocol.
  99. The correct answer is C (Keep her lying flat with legs raised, monitor observations, and tell the radiologist.): A slow pulse with pale, sweaty skin and fainting, without hives or breathing difficulty, fits a vasovagal episode, and the stem's slow pulse is what separates it from anaphylaxis. Lying her flat with legs raised restores blood flow to the brain, while observations and a report to the radiologist keep the response safe. Adrenaline is for anaphylaxis, which shows a fast weak pulse, hives, swelling and breathing difficulty. Sitting her up or sending her to walk could cause collapse.
  100. The correct answer is C (Stop the injector at once and inform the radiologist or responsible clinician.): Burning and swelling during an injection suggest extravasation, so the injector is stopped immediately and the radiologist or responsible clinician is told so they can assess the arm. A flush through a leaking cannula pushes more fluid into the tissue. Removing the cannula and treating before the clinician is informed puts the assessment in the wrong order. Documentation is needed, but it follows the clinical response, and the decision to continue belongs to the clinician.
  101. The correct answer is A (Arrange urgent medical review, because these are warning signs of serious tissue injury.): Severe or increasing pain, blistering, colour change, numbness or a cold limb are the named signs of serious injury and need urgent medical review. The earlier steps of elevation and a cold pack were right at the start, but this new finding changes the priority from routine care to escalation. Documenting and re-checking later is a delay when circulation may be threatened. Reassuring him treats a warning sign as an expected effect.
  102. The correct answer is D (From about an hour to several days afterwards, usually as a rash or flu-like symptoms.): Delayed reactions to iodinated contrast appear from about an hour to several days later, most often as a rash or flu-like illness. Patients therefore need aftercare advice before leaving. Treating the first hour as the whole risk period ignores delayed cases. Onset after ten days or more is later than the usual pattern, and reactions only on the table describe immediate reactions.
  103. The correct answer is B (Give adrenaline into the outer thigh, lay her flat, and activate the emergency response.): Hives, hoarse voice, wheeze and falling blood pressure together mark anaphylaxis, and the fast weak pulse separates it from a faint. First-line treatment is adrenaline into the outer middle thigh, with the patient flat, oxygen if available and an emergency call. An antihistamine tablet does not reverse airway swelling or low blood pressure, so waiting on it wastes time. Sitting her up risks collapse when blood pressure is falling.
  104. The correct answer is A (0.2 mL, which delivers 0.2 mg of adrenaline.): The dose is 0.01 mg/kg × 20 kg = 0.2 mg. The ampoule holds 1 mg in 1 mL, so 0.2 mg equals 0.2 mL, and the check 0.2 mL × 1 mg/mL = 0.2 mg agrees. A tenfold decimal slip upward or downward gives 2 mL or 0.02 mL. The 0.5 mL answer is the adult dose, tempting because it is the figure most often practised.
  105. The correct answer is D (Record the history and tell the radiologist, who decides whether to go ahead.): A shellfish or iodine allergy does not on its own make contrast unsafe, because the allergy is not to the iodine atom. Even so, the history is recorded and reported, and the decision belongs to the clinician rather than the practitioner acting alone. Cancelling rests on the myth, and giving an antihistamine is a medicine decision outside the practitioner's role. Calling the history irrelevant leaves the radiologist without information.
  106. The correct answer is B (Record the previous reaction and pass it to the radiologist before the injection.): A prior reaction to contrast raises risk, and it is passed to the radiologist or referring doctor, who decides whether to proceed, change the product or give premedication under local protocol. The stem's mild reaction is the tempting detail, but severity is not the practitioner's call to make. Cancelling or changing the study overreaches the role. Premedication is a clinician's decision and cannot be started by the practitioner alone.
  107. The correct answer is C (Water-soluble iodinated oral contrast, because leakage into the abdomen is not harmful.): Water-soluble iodinated oral contrast is used when a leak or perforation is suspected because it is not harmful in the abdominal cavity. Barium that escapes can cause serious inflammation, and dilution does not make it a safe choice. Withholding contrast is also wrong because the radiologist has requested it and water-soluble products can show a leak. The practitioner confirms the request and reads the label.
  108. The correct answer is B (Severe kidney impairment combined with older, less stable gadolinium agents.): Nephrogenic systemic fibrosis is linked with severe kidney impairment and older, less stable gadolinium agents. Modern group II agents carry a far lower risk, though kidney function is still checked by local protocol. Liver impairment is not the risk factor even with older agents. Pregnancy is a separate screening topic and is not the cause of this condition.
  109. The correct answer is A (Stop scanning, check airway and breathing, give support as trained, and call for help early.): Slow breathing and falling saturation in a sedated child are warning signs, and the stem's trend is what makes this urgent rather than routine. The practitioner's role is to watch airway, breathing and consciousness, give trained support and call for help early. Finishing the sequence trades safety for images. Watching or recording the trend without acting wastes the time that matters.
  110. The correct answer is D (Do not use it; obtain a freshly drawn labelled product and report the unlabelled syringe.): A product from an unlabelled syringe is never used, and the contents of a clear liquid cannot be verified by looking at it. A verbal assurance is tempting under time pressure but does not replace the label. Labelling it yourself would put your name to contents you did not prepare. Drawing up fresh and reporting the problem keeps the patient safe and fixes the system.
  111. The correct answer is C (Make the patient safe, tell the radiologist and the patient, then report it.): After a wrong dose the response is to make the patient safe, tell the patient and the responsible clinician, and report through the incident system. Feeling well now does not remove the need to disclose, because the clinician decides on monitoring. Changing the record to hide the error is dishonest. Waiting until shift end or until asked delays care and reporting.
  112. The correct answer is B (Very little reaches milk, so stopping is not usually needed, and her choice stands.): The amount of iodinated contrast that passes into milk is very small and the baby absorbs very little, so current guidance does not usually require stopping. The mother's choice and local policy are still respected. The 48 hour discard rule, permanent stopping and the idea of concentration in milk all overstate the risk.
  113. The correct answer is A (Pause the scan and report the possible pregnancy to the responsible clinician.): An uncertain or positive answer about pregnancy before any study using radiation is reported to the responsible clinician, who decides how to proceed. The late period is the stem fact that makes the answer uncertain rather than negative. A lead apron does not remove exposure risk when the abdomen is in the beam, and assuming no pregnancy is unsafe. Cancelling alone is a decision that belongs to the clinician.
  114. The correct answer is C (Decline, explain it is outside protocol, and request a valid order from an authorised person.): A medicine is given or supervised only if the law, the employer's protocol and the practitioner's training all allow it and a valid order exists. Here the protocol lists contrast and saline only and no order exists, so the safe step is to decline and ask. A half dose does not create authority, and preparing the drug without an order breaks the same rule. The patient's discomfort is real but does not change scope.
  115. The correct answer is D (Stop, do not connect the injector, and report the problem to the radiologist.): A test flush must go in easily without pain or swelling, and this one failed on all three counts, so the cannula cannot be trusted for a power injection. Forcing the flush or lowering the rate does not fix a misplaced line and may cause extravasation. Watching during the run is the right step in the wrong order, because the problem has already been shown before contrast is given. Stopping and reporting allows the cannula to be re-sited.
  116. The correct answer is B (Moderate reaction: call for medical help and monitor his observations closely.): Widespread hives and persistent vomiting are moderate features, and the normal blood pressure with no wheeze or throat symptoms keeps this below anaphylaxis. The right response is medical help and close monitoring, because it could progress. Calling it mild underestimates the reaction, and a few hives would be the mild example. Ten minutes is inside the first hour, so it is an immediate reaction, not a delayed one.
  117. The correct answer is D (Drink more fluid than usual, because barium can cause constipation afterwards.): Barium can cause constipation afterwards, so patients are advised to drink more fluid than usual. Pale stools for a day or two are expected, so a week is wrong. Fibre without extra fluid can worsen constipation. Sipping only small amounts of water offers too little fluid and the dilution reason has no basis.
  118. The correct answer is B (Report the low eGFR to the radiologist, who chooses the agent and the next step.): Severe kidney impairment is the stem detail that matters, since gadolinium agents are screened for kidney disease and the radiologist chooses the agent. Group II agents carry much lower risk, but lower is not none, and the choice is not the practitioner's. Iodinated contrast also depends on kidney function. Urgency raises the need to report quickly, not to skip the check.
  119. The correct answer is C (Tell her not to drive, and arrange a responsible adult and written advice.): A patient who has had sedation needs a responsible adult and clear instructions about driving and recovery. Feeling well does not show that judgement and reflexes have returned. A short rest or a plan to drive slowly does not remove the risk. The practitioner explains the rule and works with the clinician to arrange safe transport.
  120. The correct answer is A (Repeat adrenaline into the outer thigh now, keep her flat, and continue monitoring.): Adrenaline is repeated after about 5 minutes if there is no improvement, and this patient still has wheeze and falling blood pressure. The repeat goes into the outer thigh with the patient kept flat. Waiting for the team delays treatment, and sitting her up risks collapse. An antihistamine tablet is not a substitute for adrenaline.
  121. The correct answer is D (Respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, heart rate, blood pressure, temperature and level of consciousness.): The Board names respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, heart rate, blood pressure, temperature and level of consciousness. Each wrong set is close to this one with a single substitution, which is why it is tempting. Blood glucose, pain score and ECG rhythm are useful in some settings but are not part of the named six.
  122. The correct answer is A (Use the large forearm vein, confirm the saline test flush goes in easily, and secure it.): A large, straight forearm vein suits power injection, and a saline test flush must go in easily without pain or swelling before contrast is given. Small wrist or hand veins raise the extravasation risk. The injector cannot make a fragile vein safe. A contrast flush wastes agent and risks leaking contrast, whereas saline checks the line without that risk.
  123. The correct answer is A (Axial slices are viewed from the feet, so the patient's right is on screen left.): Axial CT and MRI images are displayed as if the viewer stands at the patient's feet looking towards the head, so the patient's right is on the screen's left. The liver on screen left is therefore exactly what normal anatomy looks like here. Reversing the image would create a laterality error, and situs inversus would be a rare finding that needs far more evidence. Viewing from the head would put the liver on the screen right, which contradicts the convention.
  124. The correct answer is C (Upright, facing forward, arms by the sides, palms forward, with left and right from the patient.): Anatomical position is an upright person facing the viewer with arms at the sides and palms forward. Every direction is described from the patient's own side, so a left forearm lesion is always the patient's left. The supine and seated postures are positions used for imaging but are not the reference. Taking left and right from the observer is the classic cause of wrong-side errors.
  125. The correct answer is B (The coronal plane, which separates front from back.): The coronal plane runs from side to side and separates the anterior half from the posterior half. The sagittal plane would separate left from right, which does not compare sternum and spine in one slice. The axial plane separates upper from lower. An oblique cut can be reconstructed but is not one of the three standard planes.
  126. The correct answer is C (5.6 litres per minute, worked out as 80 × 70 mL = 5,600 mL): Cardiac output is heart rate × stroke volume, so 80 × 70 mL = 5,600 mL per minute, which is 5.6 litres per minute after dividing by 1,000. Adding the two numbers is tempting but has no physiological meaning, and dividing gives a ratio rather than a flow. Using 100 instead of 1,000 for the conversion gives 56 litres per minute, ten times too large for a resting adult.
  127. The correct answer is D (Alert the medical team at once about a suspected tension pneumothorax.): Falling blood pressure, severe breathlessness and a shifted heart point to a tension pneumothorax, where trapped air presses on the heart and great vessels. The practitioner's role is immediate escalation rather than waiting for a formal report. An expiratory image is useful for small stable pneumothoraces but delays care here. Lying him flat could worsen his breathing, and a routine report arrives far too late.
  128. The correct answer is B (Fluid in the pleural space, settling at the lung base.): A blunted costophrenic angle with a curved fluid line on an erect image is the classic appearance of a pleural effusion. Fluid settles at the lowest point of the pleural space, which is the base of the lung when upright. Air would show a visible lung edge with no markings beyond it. Poor inspiration can mimic blunting, but a curved line up the chest wall points to fluid.
  129. The correct answer is A (A non-contrast CT brain, to exclude bleeding before treatment decisions.): A suspected stroke goes straight to a non-contrast CT brain, because treatment differs sharply depending on whether the cause is bleeding or a blocked artery. Angiography often follows, but not before bleeding is excluded. MRI is sensitive but slower and less available, and time matters because brain cells are lost every minute. A chest X-ray does not answer the immediate question.
  130. The correct answer is D (Tell the clinician the history suggests a possible clot and request urgent review.): Sudden breathlessness and chest pain after a long flight with a swollen calf point to a clot travelling to the lungs, and a chest X-ray is often normal in this condition. The practitioner notices the pattern and escalates instead of treating the request as routine. An extra view will not change that. Waiting unobserved for a report could delay life-saving testing such as CT pulmonary angiography.
  131. The correct answer is A (A larger local tumour with no lymph node involvement and no distant spread.): In the TNM system T describes the size and local extent of the tumour, N the lymph nodes and M distant metastasis. T2 N0 M0 therefore means a larger local tumour, no nodes involved and no distant spread. M0 means no metastasis, not one. Grade describes how abnormal cells look and is recorded separately from this stage.
  132. The correct answer is C (High grade but early stage, because grade and stage measure different things.): Grade describes how abnormal the cells look under the microscope, while stage describes how far the disease has spread. This patient has abnormal cells (high grade) but a small tumour without nodes or metastases (early stage). Both are recorded because they guide treatment in different ways. Size cannot predict how abnormal the cells appear.
  133. The correct answer is B (The gut lining divides rapidly, so it shows effects sooner than slowly dividing tissue.): Radiosensitivity is higher in cells that divide often and are less specialised, which is why the bowel lining and bone marrow show the earliest effects. Muscle cells divide slowly, so changes appear later or not at all. Depth does not explain the difference when both sit in the treated region. High specialisation would make a tissue less sensitive, not more.
  134. The correct answer is A (Palliative: it aims to relieve pain, usually with a lower total dose.): Pain relief from bone metastases is the classic palliative aim, and palliative courses usually use a lower total dose in few fractions. A curative or radical plan uses higher total doses over weeks. Adjuvant treatment follows surgery, and none is mentioned here. The single treatment and the symptom focus point clearly to palliation.
  135. The correct answer is C (The liver, which receives blood draining from the bowel.): Blood from the bowel drains through the portal vein to the liver first, so the liver is the most common distant site for bowel cancer. Lung spread does occur, particularly from the lower rectum, but it is less common overall. Bone and adrenal spread are typical patterns for prostate and lung cancer, which is why they tempt but do not fit here.
  136. The correct answer is D (Hold the injection and refer the result to the radiologist or prescriber.): Iodinated and gadolinium contrast are both cleared by the kidneys, so a low eGFR needs a decision from the radiologist or prescriber. The radiographer's role is to follow the protocol, check the result and refer it before giving contrast. Halving the volume is a clinical decision that is not hers to make. Hydration may be part of the plan, but not as a replacement for referral.
  137. The correct answer is D (Withhold the injection and report the high glucose to the team.): High blood glucose competes with FDG for uptake and lowers tumour uptake, so the study may be unreliable. The glucose is measured before injection precisely so a high value can be reported to the team, who decide what happens next. Injecting regardless wastes tracer and dose. Reducing the activity does not remove the competition, and a longer wait does not fix it.
  138. The correct answer is A (Stop the scan, tilt her onto her left side with support, and call for help.): Late in pregnancy the uterus can press on the inferior vena cava when she lies flat, reducing blood returning to the heart and causing faintness. Tilting to the left lifts the weight off that vein, and the scan stops while she is checked. Raising the legs does not remove the compression. The right side would not relieve it.
  139. The correct answer is B (The first trimester, when organs form and the brain begins developing.): Organ formation and early brain development occur mostly in the first trimester, which makes the foetus most sensitive to radiation then. The first two weeks before implantation settle at loss or normal development, so they do not match the stem. Fast growth later in pregnancy and larger size do not raise sensitivity in the same way. Pregnancy is checked first and dose kept as low as reasonably achievable.
  140. The correct answer is D (Offer a further projection and tell the doctor, rather than accept one negative image.): Growth plates are weaker than the bone beside them, and a strongly suspected injury that is not obvious needs another projection and a word with the doctor. Comparison views have a place, but calling a difference an old injury closes the question without evidence. Repeating the same view at higher dose adds exposure without new information. An unfused plate does look like a lucent line, which is exactly why it can hide a real injury.
  141. The correct answer is A (Explain to the child, immobilise comfortably, collimate tightly and use the lowest adequate dose.): Explaining to the child, comfortable immobilisation, tight collimation and the lowest adequate dose together reduce both movement and dose. Shortening exposure time helps but does not replace calming and holding the leg. Parent help is reasonable, yet opening the field adds exposure to a child whose cells divide rapidly. Repeating until one is sharp multiplies dose.
  142. The correct answer is C (Keep the infant warm and minimise the time spent undressed.): Infants lose heat fast and have little reserve, so they are kept warm and undressed only for the exposure itself. Fast breathing rate is normal at this age, and feeding well means no emergency call. Putting the baby on a cold surface adds heat loss for a flatter chest. Leaving the baby undressed through set-up trades warmth for avoiding clothing artefacts.
  143. The correct answer is B (Dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry, using two X-ray energies to estimate mineral density.): Dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry compares attenuation at two energies to calculate mineral density, and it is the standard for assessing osteoporosis. Heel ultrasound is used for screening in some settings but does not replace it. Bone scans shows metabolic activity, and magnetic resonance signal does not give a mineral density value. These are real modalities, which is why they tempt.
  144. The correct answer is C (Move her slowly with a slide sheet, support her limbs and avoid pulling the skin.): Slow movement with a slide sheet, supported limbs and no pulling on the skin suits thin skin, warfarin and confusion. Lifting under the arms or gripping the forearms risks skin tears and bleeding. Climbing unaided risks a fall for a confused patient. Rigid boards with tight straps raise pressure injury risk in frail skin and suit a different patient group.
  145. The correct answer is D (Stop the injection, call for help through the emergency response, and keep her airway open.): Hives, a swollen lip, wheeze and falling blood pressure after contrast point to anaphylaxis. The first actions are to stop the injection, call for help through the emergency response and keep the airway open. Waiting to see whether symptoms settle wastes time when airway and circulation are at risk. Positioning her is useful, but finishing the scan first delays help, and an oral tablet is too slow.
  146. The correct answer is B (Stay with him, call for help through the emergency response, and state facts.): A pale, sweaty, drowsy patient with a falling level of consciousness cannot wait for the routine list. The pattern is to stay, observe, call for help, state plain facts and document afterwards. Leaving him alone or sending him away unaccompanied delays recognition of deterioration. Waiting to see if it passes is the wrong choice in this scenario.
  147. The correct answer is B (Open, because the wound connects to the break and infection risk is high.): A fracture is open when a wound connects to the break, even if no bone is visible, and the infection risk is high. Wound size and the shape of the fracture line do not decide this. Pathological describes a break through diseased bone, which says nothing about the skin and is not suggested by a cycling injury in a healthy adult. Treating it as closed would delay urgent care.
  148. The correct answer is A (Behind the peritoneal sac, in the retroperitoneum, on either side of the spine.): The kidneys lie either side of the spine at the back of the upper abdomen, outside the peritoneal sac, in the retroperitoneum. Organs held on a mesentery sit inside the sac, and the kidneys are not among them. The pelvis is the wrong level for an upper abdominal slice. The liver, stomach and bowel are the structures that lie within the sac.
  149. The correct answer is A (Get specific advice from the nuclear medicine team before injecting and relay it.): Breastfeeding after some radiopharmaceuticals needs specific advice from the nuclear medicine team, and the advice differs between agents. The technologist raises it before injection while the choice of timing or interruption is still open. A blanket yes or a blanket permanent stop would both be wrong. Injecting first removes the chance to plan feeding around the dose.
  150. The correct answer is D (Minimise movement, use slide sheets and extra staff, and image her as she lies.): A shortened, outwardly rotated leg after a fall suggests a hip fracture in a frail, confused patient. Minimal movement, slide sheets and extra staff, imaging the pelvis as she lies, keeps pain and displacement low. Lifting the pelvis or rotating the leg moves the injury, and a quick roll causes pain. True standard position is not worth risking further damage.
  151. The correct answer is C (Pause, follow the local pregnancy procedure, and refer to the radiologist for a decision.): Pregnancy is asked about before radiation to the lower trunk, and a possible pregnancy changes the risk-benefit decision. The decision to proceed belongs to the referring clinician and radiologist after weighing risk and benefit, so the practitioner pauses and follows the local procedure. A lead apron does not stop the primary beam in a pelvic CT. Cancelling permanently or proceeding unchanged are both unilateral choices.
  152. The correct answer is D (Sarcoma, a malignant tumour of bone or connective tissue.): Tumours are named by the tissue they come from, and malignant tumours of bone or connective tissue are sarcomas. Carcinomas arise from cells lining surfaces such as skin, gut, breast and lung. Lymphomas come from lymph tissue and gliomas from supporting cells of the brain. A tumour arising within bone fits only the sarcoma group.
  153. The correct answer is C (The Board sets registration standards, and Ahpra runs the registers on its behalf.): The National Board for each profession sets the registration standards, while Ahpra handles applications and keeps the public register on the Board's behalf. Reversing the roles is tempting because Ahpra is the name candidates deal with directly. Giving the register to the Board or to a ministerial council misplaces a function that Ahpra performs for all the Boards.
  154. The correct answer is A (Each jurisdiction enacted its own version, so the rules are close to identical.): The National Law was enacted in a version by every state and territory, so the rules are close to identical and one registration covers work in Perth. A single Commonwealth Act sounds neat but is not how the scheme was legislated. Separate state boards and a special Western Australian registration are tempting because other laws, such as radiation licensing, do differ by state, yet registration standards are national.
  155. The correct answer is C (Using the title without registration in that division is an offence, qualifications aside.): Under the National Law the title diagnostic radiographer is protected for people registered in that division, so using it before registration is an offence whatever his degree. Supervision and employer checks feel responsible but neither can grant a protected title. The belief that only some divisions' titles are protected is tempting, yet all three are covered.
  156. The correct answer is D (She cannot declare compliance yet, since renewal declares every standard is currently met.): The renewal declaration states that the standards are met now, and continuing professional development is one of them, so she has to finish the hours before she can truthfully declare. A written plan or a supervisor's co-signature shows good intent but does not make the declaration true today. Audit checks happen later and do not change what the declaration requires when she submits it.
  157. The correct answer is B (Criminal history, English language skills, professional indemnity insurance, continuing professional development and recency of practice.): The registration standards cover criminal history, English language skills, professional indemnity insurance, continuing professional development and recency of practice. The other lists mix in items that sound plausible, such as vaccination or association membership, which employers or associations may ask for but the Board does not require. Qualifications matter for registration, yet they are not the only item checked.
  158. The correct answer is A (Agree a supervised re-entry plan with her manager before she treats independently again.): After five years away her current competence is not established, and recency of practice is a registration standard, so a supervised re-entry plan agreed with her manager protects patients while she rebuilds skill. Starting with simple cases still has her treating independently with no check on her competence. A refresher module is useful but does not replace supervised practice, and the Board does not clear individuals for local rosters.
  159. The correct answer is C (Call the on-call senior to supervise, since working outside a condition is a breach.): A condition must be followed exactly, and the on-call senior can be reached, so calling her to supervise keeps him within the condition and the patient imaged promptly. A radiologist is not the senior radiographer the condition names, and a nurse unit manager has no power to vary a Board condition. Transferring a trauma patient adds risk and delay when compliant supervision is available by phone.
  160. The correct answer is B (Conditions can be imposed at the start or later, for example after a notification.): Conditions limit how a practitioner works and can be imposed when registration is granted or added later, often after a notification. The Board can act without waiting for a tribunal or for a finding of misconduct when the public needs protection. Limiting conditions to first registration would leave later concerns with only cancellation as a response. Employers have no power to add them.
  161. The correct answer is C (Decline the paediatric list and tell the manager about her condition.): A condition must be followed exactly, whoever rosters her and whatever the staffing pressure. Parent agreement or a supervisor present does not lift a condition placed by the Board, and telling the Board afterwards does not undo a breach. She should decline the paediatric list and let the manager know the limit so the roster can be corrected.
  162. The correct answer is B (Decline to scan alone and ask for training, supervision or another care pathway.): Scope depends on being educated or trained, competent now and permitted, and she has no ultrasound training, so she should decline to scan alone and seek training or supervision. Policy permission covers only the third part of the test. Having a radiologist report the scan ignores that acquiring the images is also a skilled task, and watching two scans is not training.
  163.
