The correct answer is A (Scope can differ between people with the same title, depending on education, competence and authorisation.): Scope belongs to the individual and depends on education, competence and authorisation, so two therapists with the same title can differ. The registration division sets the title but not the tasks each person may perform. A role description and years since graduation are relevant inputs, yet neither alone defines what a particular practitioner can safely do.
  164. The correct answer is D (The senior remains accountable for choosing that trainee and for the level of supervision she provided.): Handing over a task does not hand over accountability for the decision to delegate and for the supervision provided, so the senior remains accountable for both. Sharing accountability by blame or tying it to a signature sounds fair but is not how delegation works. Accountability also does not move to the trainee when she accepts the task.
  165. The correct answer is C (Decline to delegate that sign-off, because the student is not yet competent to complete it safely.): Delegation is appropriate only when the person is competent, the task is suitable and supervision is adequate, and a first-year student is not yet competent to sign off a plan check. Induction training and manager approval are real parts of the process but do not establish competence for this task. Cosigning afterwards keeps the therapist accountable but does not make the delegation safe.
  166. The correct answer is A (Do the pregnancy check herself, because it needs clinical judgement a porter lacks.): Delegation needs a competent person and a task suitable to delegate. A pregnancy check before radiation involves judgement and questions beyond a form, so a porter without training is not the right person. Checking forms later is not supervision of the conversation itself.
  167. The correct answer is C (Perform it herself or directly supervise him until his competence is shown.): Delegation requires competence for that specific task and supervision at a suitable level. He has watched this view once, so he is not yet shown to be competent. Reviewing the image afterwards cannot prevent a repeat exposure, and confidence is not competence.
  168. The correct answer is A (Delegation hands over a task but keeps accountability; supervision is watching over and guiding that work.): Delegation hands a task to another person while the delegator stays accountable for the decision to delegate. Supervision is overseeing and guiding the work, and the two duties travel together. Swapping the definitions is a common slip. Accountability does not pass to the person who accepts the task, and neither term is limited to a particular group of staff.
  169. The correct answer is D (It is not law itself, but a disciplinary matter can use it as evidence.): The Code is not legislation itself, but a disciplinary matter can use it as evidence of expected conduct, so ignoring parts of it does carry consequences. Describing it as a regulation overstates its legal form, and limiting it to applications understates how the Board uses it. It applies to every registered practitioner, not only association members.
  170. The correct answer is D (The Code covers advertising and social media, and no post may identify a patient.): The Code covers advertising and social media, and a post must not identify a patient wherever it appears. Blurred faces and first names can still identify someone through context, so partial anonymising does not make a post acceptable. The duty also does not depend on naming an employer or on how many followers can see a private profile.
  171. The correct answer is B (Decline the offer, declare it to the panel chair and manage it openly.): A conflict of interest is an interest that could influence judgement, such as supplier payment while choosing products. The Code asks practitioners to decline valuable gifts, declare the interest and manage it openly. Abstaining or staying silent leaves the panel unaware of the approach.
  172. The correct answer is B (Good care, culturally safe practice, ethical conduct, clear communication, minimising risk and secure records.): The Code describes expected behaviour: good care, cultural safety, ethical conduct, communication, minimising risk and keeping records secure. Dose limits and shielding sit in radiation protection law, program accreditation and language skills sit in registration standards, and examination protocols are workplace guidance. Each other list starts with real Code themes but ends with items that belong to a different document.
  173. The correct answer is C (State and territory radiation laws are a separate system, so licensing still applies to premises.): Registration with the Board concerns the person and their fitness to practise, while licensing concerns radiation sources and where they are used. Private clinics are licensed under state and territory radiation laws, so staff registration cannot stand in for the licence. A notification to a regulator, a council approval or a national agency may sound like a substitute, but none of them replaces the state licence that this clinic still needs.
  174. The correct answer is A (The Board writes the Code and guidelines, while Ahpra works with it administering the scheme.): The Board is the decision maker for the profession and writes its Code of conduct and guidelines. Ahpra supports the Board by running the registers and managing notifications, so the friend's reversed view is tempting but wrong. Health ministers approve certain registration standards and associations may offer advice, yet neither is the author of the Code.
  175. The correct answer is B (Intoxication at work, sexual misconduct, substantial risk from impairment, and a significant departure from standards.): The four mandatory grounds are practising while intoxicated, sexual misconduct, placing the public at risk of substantial harm because of impairment, and placing the public at risk through a significant departure from accepted standards. Insurance breaches and criminal charges are real regulatory matters, but they are not among the four grounds for notifying about another practitioner. A diagnosed impairment alone is also not enough, because the ground depends on risk of substantial harm.
  176. The correct answer is C (Notify, since a reasonable belief on reasonable grounds triggers the duty without proof.): The duty to notify is triggered by forming a reasonable belief on reasonable grounds that sexual misconduct occurred, and a detailed, distressed account from the patient can supply those grounds. The radiographer does not need to have witnessed it or to hold proof. The duty belongs to her personally, so it does not depend on the patient lodging a complaint, on an internal finding, or on a manager choosing to act.
  177. The correct answer is D (Notify now on her reasonable belief and leave the investigating to Ahpra and the Board.): A mandatory notification rests on a reasonable belief, not on certainty or collected evidence, and the notifier leaves investigation to Ahpra and the Board. With children among the rostered patients, delay leaves the public exposed. Offering the colleague time to self-notify, waiting for a test, or handing the matter to a safety officer may feel measured, but each postpones or transfers a duty that belongs to her.
  178. The correct answer is D (A voluntary notification is possible, because the concern does not meet any mandatory ground.): Voluntary notifications cover concerns that do not meet a mandatory ground, and anyone may make one. Curt communication with no risk of harm is not intoxication, sexual misconduct, impairment or a significant departure placing the public at risk. A Code breach alone does not make a notification mandatory, and the employer's process does not remove the option of notifying Ahpra.
  179. The correct answer is A (People who notify honestly and in good faith are protected from liability under the National Law.): The National Law protects anyone who notifies honestly and in good faith, whether the notification is voluntary or mandatory. The student's status does not matter, and the notifier need not prove the concern. Protection also does not depend on Ahpra upholding the concern, since honesty and good faith are the test. Each of the other statements adds a condition the law does not contain.
  180. The correct answer is B (No, impairment alone is not grounds; the duty arises when it risks substantial harm.): Many practitioners live with health conditions and practise safely. The ground is an impairment that places the public at risk of substantial harm, and a managed condition with good insight and no errors does not meet it. A diagnosis alone does not make a notification mandatory, although health can still be notified if risk emerges. Confidentiality of the disclosure is not the reason, because the scheme does reach health matters when risk exists.
  181. The correct answer is D (Notify, because her continued work while unwell risks substantial harm to the public.): Impairment alone is not grounds, but it becomes mandatory when it places the public at risk of substantial harm. Repeated dose errors, an untreated condition and refusal of help show that risk is present now. The test is risk, not injury already caused. Employer processes and encouraging self-notification may help, but neither discharges the coworker's own duty while patients are still being treated.
  182. The correct answer is B (Keep the colleague out of patient care and tell the senior person.): The first step is immediate patient safety: keep the colleague out of patient care and tell the senior person in charge, who has authority over the list. Notification to Ahpra follows once a reasonable belief has formed, but it does not protect the patients waiting at 7 am. Testing the colleague or reallocating duties herself goes beyond her role and bypasses the person responsible for the department.
  183. The correct answer is C (Make a mandatory notification, having formed a reasonable belief that he practised while intoxicated.): Practising while intoxicated is a mandatory ground, and the duty falls on any registered practitioner who forms a reasonable belief. Clear slurred speech and the smell of alcohol provide that basis, so no test result is needed. A senior person's report or an employer investigation does not discharge her own duty, and removing the colleague from duty does not turn a mandatory ground into a voluntary one.
  184. The correct answer is B (Placing the public at risk of harm through a significant departure from accepted professional standards.): Repeatedly skipping identification and pregnancy checks after correction is a significant departure from accepted standards that places patients at risk. Nothing in the stem points to a health condition or substance use, so inferring impairment or intoxication adds facts that are not there. A breach of a registration standard is a regulatory matter, but it is not a mandatory ground for notifying about another practitioner.
  185. The correct answer is A (Ahpra investigates on the Board's behalf, and the Board decides the action.): Ahpra receives and assesses notifications and investigates on behalf of the Board, and the Board decides what action to take. The notifier is not expected to gather further proof, and the employer does not run the regulatory investigation. Reversing the roles of Ahpra and the Board is a common slip, and publication on the register is an outcome of Board decisions, not a first step.
  186. The correct answer is D (Seek advice from her supervisor or Ahpra, and consider a voluntary notification if the concern remains.): When no mandatory ground is clearly met, the sensible path is to seek advice from a supervisor or Ahpra and consider a voluntary notification if the concern remains. Doubt alone does not create a mandatory duty, so an immediate mandatory notification overreaches. Waiting for months or leaving the decision to human resources avoids using the advice and voluntary pathways that exist for exactly this situation.
  187. The correct answer is A (A duty to take reasonable care to avoid harming people you could foreseeably affect.): Duty of care is the legal obligation to take reasonable care not to harm people you can foresee being affected by your acts or omissions. It asks for reasonable care rather than protection from every outcome. Policies and referrals inform the standard but do not define it. The employer carries its own responsibilities, and the radiographer also owes the duty personally.
  188. The correct answer is C (Duty of care owed, standard not met, breach caused the harm, and loss was real.): Negligence needs a duty of care, a failure to meet the standard, causation linking the breach to harm, and real loss. A complaint, fault in general terms or poor supervision is not a required element. Foreseeability and carelessness are relevant to the standard, but without causation and actual loss the claim is incomplete.
  189. The correct answer is D (It is what a reasonable practitioner with the same training would have done.): The standard of care is what a reasonable practitioner of the same kind, with the same training, would have done. A new graduate is not measured against a practitioner with a decade of experience. Department protocols and patient expectations are evidence about what is reasonable, but neither one sets the legal standard on its own.
  190. The correct answer is A (A prompt, honest, compassionate conversation covering regret, what is known, prevention steps and support offered.): Open disclosure is a prompt, honest and compassionate conversation that includes regret, the facts known, the steps to prevent recurrence and the support on offer. A single apology is part of it but leaves out the explanation and follow up. A written statement after the review is too late and impersonal, and an internal committee report is quality work rather than a conversation with the patient.
  191. The correct answer is B (An apology is not an admission of legal liability in most jurisdictions.): Apology laws in most jurisdictions mean that saying sorry is not in itself an admission of legal liability. Open disclosure also expects the practitioner to explain what is known, so protection does not require a vague apology. The protection is not limited to employers and does not wait on insurer approval. Silence may feel safer but conflicts with honest disclosure and does not protect her.
  192. The correct answer is C (Tell the supervisor, report the incident, and take part in open disclosure.): After an error the practitioner tells the supervisor, reports the incident through the incident system, and joins open disclosure so the patient hears honestly what happened and what follows. Repeating the examination first, or without the supervisor, adds exposure before anyone has judged whether it is justified. Asking the referring doctor to explain also passes on a conversation that the radiographer should take part in.
  193. The correct answer is A (A senior clinician may lead, but the radiographer involved should still take part honestly.): Open disclosure can be led by a senior clinician, yet the practitioner involved still takes part honestly, because openness is expected of everyone connected with the event. An incident report is also needed, but it does not replace the conversation with the patient. Having a union representative speak for her removes her own honest account. She also does not need to lead, because the department head can carry that role.
  194. The correct answer is B (He cannot use that title without registration in that division.): Protected titles belong only to practitioners registered in that division, so a diagnostic radiographer cannot use the nuclear medicine title yet. A course certificate shows learning but is not registration. A clinic or its governing body has no power to confer the title, and limiting the advertisement to one venue does not make the use lawful. Holding one division gives no right to another division's title.
  195. The correct answer is C (No, the Board decides what happens to registration, and Ahpra administers the process on its behalf.): An employer's warning is an employment matter and does not itself decide registration. The Board makes the decisions about conduct and health, while Ahpra administers the process for it. Giving Ahpra the decision role confuses administration with decision making. A health complaints commissioner may handle complaints, but it does not administer the registration process, and the Board never simply adopts an employer's finding.
  196. The correct answer is D (Tell the manager and renew with an honest declaration before practising under the protected title.): Practising under a protected title needs current registration, and renewal is itself a declaration that standards are met, so she should be open with the manager and renew honestly first. Supervision does not stand in for registration. Beginning work while payment clears still means practising before registration is confirmed. Calling the lapse a short administrative delay does not change the offence risk.
  197. The correct answer is D (Protect the patient, tell the senior person on duty, and notify about sexual misconduct.): Sexual misconduct is a mandatory notification ground, and her own observation gives her reasonable grounds, so she must notify personally as well as protect the patient and inform the senior person. Leaving the notification to someone else does not discharge her duty. Waiting for the sedated patient to confirm makes the duty depend on a person who cannot yet speak up. Delaying until recovery leaves the patient unprotected now.
  198. The correct answer is B (Capacity, voluntary agreement, adequate information on risks and alternatives, and agreement to that procedure): Valid consent needs capacity, voluntariness, adequate information including alternatives, and agreement to the specific procedure, all together. A signature records consent but does not create it, and the information must include alternatives. The referring doctor's earlier agreement cannot replace the patient's own. A set that leaves out capacity is incomplete, since agreement from someone unable to decide is not valid.
  199. The correct answer is D (Presume capacity, record his understanding and take his own consent): Capacity is presumed, and a disability does not remove it, especially when he has shown he understands, weighs and decides. Recording that understanding and taking his own consent respects him. A co-signing sibling adds a requirement the law does not impose. Staff doubt that his own demonstration answers should not hand the decision to his sibling, and a formal assessment is reserved for real doubt about capacity.
  200. The correct answer is C (Reassess capacity for this decision now, explain the study and seek her own consent): Capacity is decision and time specific, so now that she is lucid and the study is non-urgent, the radiographer should reassess and seek her own consent. The nurse's report is useful but does not replace the assessment by the person taking consent. Her son has no role while she can decide. Handing the consent task to the nurse leaves the radiographer without an informed, current decision from her.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Respect his informed refusal, document it and tell the referrer): A capable adult may refuse after being informed, so the radiographer respects the choice, documents it and tells the referrer so alternatives can be discussed. His wife cannot sign for him while he has capacity. Changing to an unenhanced scan and only noting it on a form does not give the referrer the clear message. Sending in a second radiographer to repeat the benefits turns an informed refusal into pressure.
  2. The correct answer is A (A valid, applicable directive must be followed even if relatives disagree): A valid, current directive that clearly applies to his present condition takes effect precisely when he cannot decide, so it must be followed even if relatives disagree. Urgency does not let the registrar override a clear refusal. The partner who delivers it does not decide whether it binds. The family's agreement is not a condition either, although the team should check validity and ask a senior clinician if real doubt remains.
  3. The correct answer is A (Raise it with the senior clinician on duty, who can check the record and seek advice): A directive must be valid, current and applicable, so an unverified claim is neither proof nor something to dismiss. The senior clinician on duty can search the record and seek advice within the hour. Phoning the GP herself puts a verification task on someone without that role. Treating first risks acting against a real refusal, while withholding treatment on the nephew's word could deny care the patient wants.
  4. The correct answer is C (Follow policy and ask the clinical team to confirm the legally recognised decision-maker): Who may consent for a person without capacity is set by state or territory law, so the radiographer follows policy and asks the clinical team to confirm the recognised decision-maker. A signed statement of relationship does not create authority. A radiologist's best-interests note does not replace consent for a planned scan. No rule ranks relatives by how quickly they can be reached, and an estranged brother may rank below others.
  5. The correct answer is C (Decline to give details by phone and refer the request to the person releasing records): Disclosure without consent is allowed only where the law requires or permits it, and that is decided through the workplace process, so the request goes to the person who releases records. A spoken badge number proves nothing by phone. Verifying the caller is sensible, but a radiographer releasing the report directly bypasses that process. Confirming attendance is itself disclosure of health information.
  6. The correct answer is B (A privacy breach, because access was not for his care even though nothing was disclosed): Health information may be used for the purpose it was collected, which is the patient's care, and curiosity is not care. The access is therefore a breach even without disclosure. Department staff are given system access for work purposes, not for browsing any booked patient. Privacy law covers use and access as well as disclosure, and the breach does not depend on later talking about it.
  7. The correct answer is D (Where the law requires or permits it, such as a court order or mandatory report): Apart from consent, health information may be disclosed where the law requires or permits it, for example under a court order or a mandatory report. An employer has no entitlement to confirm attendance or clinical detail without the patient's agreement. Using images for staff training is a different purpose from care and needs consent or de-identification. A caring family member still needs the patient's agreement before results are shared.
  8. The correct answer is D (Report the threat urgently to the senior clinician, sharing only what is needed): Confidentiality yields to a serious and imminent threat, so the concern goes promptly to the senior clinician, sharing only what is needed. Seeking consent is courteous, but his refusal cannot block disclosure of a serious threat. Warning the neighbour personally bypasses the people equipped to assess and act. Reporting at the end of the shift is too slow when he plans to act tonight.
  9. The correct answer is C (Correct it with a dated addendum naming her, leaving the original entry visible): Records must be accurate and traceable, so the person who made the error adds a dated addendum and leaves the original readable. A correction made under the manager's login misstates who did what. An incident report may be useful, but it leaves the clinical record inaccurate for later readers. Deleting and re-entering hides what was first recorded and removes the trail.
  10. The correct answer is B (She generally has a right of access to her own health information, with limited exceptions): Privacy law and state health records law give people a general right of access to their own health information, with limited exceptions such as serious risk to another person. Images form part of that information, so they need no separate radiologist approval. The referring doctor does not set a waiting period. A formal freedom of information application is not the usual route, since requests go through the service's records process.
  11. The correct answer is A (It breaches policy and privacy duties, so she should report the lapse): Patient information must travel through approved, secure channels, and a photo on a personal phone falls outside the workplace's controls. Dates, side or injury can still identify a patient, so a missing name does not remove privacy duties. A good clinical purpose does not make the channel acceptable. Asking the friend to delete the photo does not tell the service, which needs to know so it can manage the risk.
  12. The correct answer is A (Duty of care owed, standard breached, that breach caused the harm, and real loss resulted): Negligence needs a duty of care, a breach of the standard, causation of the harm by that breach, and real loss. Disciplinary findings belong to regulation, not to a civil claim. Failure to disclose may matter elsewhere, but it is not an element of negligence. Leaving out breach is a gap, and the patient not contributing relates to a defence, not to what the patient must prove.
  13. The correct answer is B (Against what a reasonable radiographer with similar training and role would have done): Negligence is judged objectively, by asking what a reasonable practitioner with similar training and in a similar role would have done in the circumstances. The standard is not lifted to that of a senior colleague, because a three year radiographer is not expected to perform at that level. It is not set by her own sincere belief either, since an honest view can still fall short. Local custom is relevant but does not decide the matter when it departs from a standard procedure designed to protect patients.
  14. The correct answer is B (Stay with the patient, call for medical help and begin monitoring): A duty of care is owed to a patient the practitioner could foreseeably harm, and it does not end at the shift boundary when she is aware of a developing emergency. Pallor and breathlessness soon after contrast may signal a serious reaction, so immediate help and monitoring come first. Asking others to assess later, or arranging a call tomorrow, sounds courteous but leaves the patient without timely care. Telephoning the general practitioner is a sensible communication step but cannot replace emergency assistance on site.
  15. The correct answer is C (A prompt, honest talk with the patient and family covering events, regret and prevention): Open disclosure is a prompt, honest and compassionate conversation with the patient and family about what happened, an expression of regret, what is known and what will be done to prevent recurrence. It sits alongside incident reporting to the service and regulator rather than replacing it. A written explanation after the review finishes is too late to meet the timeliness the framework expects. A briefing only for the family after discharge leaves out the patient who was affected.
  16. The correct answer is D (Tell the patient honestly, inform the supervisor and report the incident): After an error the practitioner discloses openly to the patient, informs the supervisor and reports the incident so the service can learn and put things right. A small dose does not remove any of those steps. Apologising and logging the exposure is honest but bypasses the supervisor and the incident system. Disclosure belongs to the practitioner involved, not a radiologist who may only see the images later, and a low dose still needs reporting.
  17. The correct answer is A (Take part in open disclosure with regret expressed, since apology laws generally protect it): Open disclosure expects a prompt conversation that includes an expression of regret, an explanation of what is known and what will happen next. In most jurisdictions apology laws mean that saying sorry is not an admission of legal liability. Holding the apology until a review finishes delays what the patient is entitled to hear. A complaints officer is not the right person to disclose a clinical incident, and a hurried personal apology without the supervisor misses the supported process.
  18. The correct answer is B (Autonomy and beneficence conflict; respect her informed refusal and document it): Her refusal sets autonomy, her right to choose, against beneficence, the therapist's wish to benefit her. A capable adult may refuse even a treatment that seems wise, so the role is to confirm the choice is informed, document it and leave the door open to later care. Naming justice misreads the conflict, which is about her benefit and not fair allocation. Enlisting family to persuade her or seeking committee approval both treat a capable decision as something others must authorise.
  19. The correct answer is D (Justice, treating people fairly and distributing resources equitably): Justice is the principle about fair treatment and the fair distribution of limited resources, which is why it applies to scanner time and waiting lists. Beneficence is about acting for a patient's good and can be mistaken for fairness when many patients are waiting. Autonomy concerns the right to choose one's own care, not a say in allocation. Non-maleficence concerns avoiding harm, even though delay can feel harmful, but it is not the principle of fair sharing.
  20. The correct answer is D (Allocate the slot by clinical urgency, explain this to the outpatient and offer the next time): Justice means fair treatment according to need, and a worsening neurological deficit is more urgent than a routine follow-up. Fairness also includes explaining the decision to the outpatient and offering the next available time. Giving the right patient the scan but leaving the outpatient to sort it out alone gets the priority right and fails the communication owed. Waiting time is not a measure of clinical need, and splitting one slot between two patients would compromise both examinations.
  21. The correct answer is C (Name the ethical issue, gather the relevant facts and consider the patient's wishes and the law): A reasonable approach is to name the issue, gather the facts, consider the patient's wishes and the law, consult the team, decide and review. Here the facts include that she is competent and has clearly asked for the finding not to be shared. Consulting others before understanding her wishes inverts the order. Deciding first and reviewing afterwards, or listing options before the problem is defined, skips the early analysis that makes later steps sound.
  22. The correct answer is B (The practitioner, because the relationship is unequal and agreement does not remove the duty): A therapeutic relationship is unequal, since the patient is often unwell, anxious and dependent. For that reason the practitioner carries the responsibility for the boundary, and the patient's agreement does not remove it. Shared responsibility ignores the power imbalance. The employer sets policy and supports staff, but the personal duty to hold the boundary sits with the practitioner. The patient raising the idea does not shift the duty to her.
  23. The correct answer is A (Decline the number kindly, keep contact at work, and declare or return the gift under policy): Boundary problems grow in small steps such as sharing a private number, favouring one patient and accepting gifts. The sound response keeps contact within the workplace and handles the gift openly under the gift policy. Accepting a modest gift without declaring it leaves a possible conflict hidden. A private number stays a private channel even when its use is limited. Reassigning a long-term patient is out of proportion and still leaves the gift unaddressed.
  24. The correct answer is C (Weigh time passed and the person's vulnerability, and never use knowledge gained during care): The Code treats a relationship with a former patient as a serious concern when knowledge of the person's vulnerabilities could be used, and asks the practitioner to weigh the time passed and how vulnerable the person was. Anxiety and recent bereavement are relevant facts here. A closed record or the woman's consent does not settle the matter, because vulnerability can persist and consent may be shaped by it. An employer's waiting period is not the professional test.
  25. The correct answer is B (Declare the offer, decline the trip and manage the decision openly): A conflict of interest is a personal, professional or financial interest that could influence judgement, and the response is to declare it and manage it openly. Declining the valuable trip removes the inducement. Accepting the trip while stepping back from one vote still takes a benefit from a bidder. Declining without declaring leaves the panel unaware, and asking the supplier to withdraw is not her decision. Telling only the chair without declining the trip leaves the inducement in place.
  26. The correct answer is C (The radiographer, who remains accountable for choosing the student and the supervision): Delegation hands over a task but not accountability for the decision to delegate. The radiographer chose the student, judged the task suitable and supplied the supervision, so she answers for those choices. The university certified competence in education, but its role ends there. A manager approving a placement does not make the manager answerable for one delegation. The student remains responsible for her own conduct, but that does not remove the radiographer's accountability for delegating.
  27. The correct answer is A (Decline to delegate the injection, explain it is outside the assistant's competence and role, and escalate): Delegation is appropriate only when the person is competent, the task is suitable and the right supervision can be provided. Here the department does not permit assistants to inject and no supervisor can stay. A single practice attempt or a watching colleague does not create competence or extend the role. A manager's written instruction cannot move an assistant beyond what the department allows. The sound step is to decline and escalate the workload problem.
  28. The correct answer is D (The person is competent, the task is suitable, and appropriate supervision can be given): Delegation requires a person who is competent for the task, a task that is suitable to hand over, and the ability to give the right level of supervision. Willingness, routine work and informing the patient do not replace supervision. Registration or a paper record does not show competence in this particular task. A busy list and urgency explain why delegation is tempting but do not make it safe.
  29. The correct answer is A (A privacy risk; she should remove the post and report it under policy): Anything on social media that could identify a patient is a privacy breach, and a friend recognising someone shows identification was possible. Removing the post stops further exposure, and reporting it under policy lets the service manage the breach and inform the patient if needed. Contacting the patient herself bypasses that process. Editing the photo leaves a version already seen, and tighter audience settings do not undo a disclosure that has already happened.
  30. The correct answer is B (Decline politely, say you keep personal accounts apart from patients, and offer workplace channels): A friend request from a current patient crosses a professional boundary, and the practitioner carries the responsibility for holding it. A short, kind explanation and a workplace channel maintain trust without creating a personal link. Accepting with restrictions still creates one. Passing the reply to a manager is unnecessary and leaves the patient without a kind personal answer. Promising to accept later ignores that boundaries also apply to former patients.
  31. The correct answer is A (A confidentiality breach, since the details could identify him): Information is identifying when the combination of details lets others work out who the person is, and a rare tumour with age, sex, place and timing can do that in a small town. The duty of confidentiality does not depend on whether the patient complains or on the poster's good intentions. Leaving out a name does not make the description de-identified. The practitioner should delete the post and report it.
  32. The correct answer is B (Follow the child protection procedure and report the concern to the right person): A reasonable concern about a child's safety is enough to follow the workplace child protection procedure and report, and proof is not needed. The duty belongs to the practitioner who holds the concern, so it cannot be handed to the referrer, who may not know about the bruising and behaviour. Hearing from the parents first, or questioning the child, risks her safety and may compromise any later inquiry.
  33. The correct answer is D (They differ by state and territory, including who must report, so check local requirements): Child protection and mandatory reporting are set by state and territory legislation, so who must report and what must be reported varies. A graduate changing states should check the requirements that apply where she now works. The National Law regulates practitioner registration rather than child protection, and her Victorian knowledge does not transfer automatically. Employer policy supports the law but cannot define the legal duty.
  34. The correct answer is C (Create privacy if possible, offer support, follow the service procedure and keep factual notes): Creating privacy, offering support, following the service procedure and keeping factual notes protects her safety and her choices. Asking about violence in front of her partner can raise her risk, however gently it is done. A capable adult is generally not reported to police without her consent, so immediate notification oversteps. Leaving the matter to a follow-up visit misses the only contact the service may have with her.
  35. The correct answer is B (Use the accredited interpreter, because family interpreting risks errors and compromised voluntariness): An accredited interpreter gives an accurate, impartial explanation and lets her ask questions or decline without family present. A teach-back after the grandson has interpreted comes too late, because errors or omissions have already shaped what she heard. A bilingual staff member is not trained or accredited for interpreting and has other duties. A translated sheet with the grandson answering questions still leaves a relative controlling the conversation.
  36. The correct answer is D (Allow time, use plain language, ask who he wants present, and offer a liaison officer): Cultural safety is shaped by the patient, so the technologist allows time, uses plain language, asks who he wants present and offers a liaison officer. Family often share in decisions, but they should not answer for him unless he asks. Quietness and averted eye contact can reflect many things, so an interpreter should be offered only if a language need is found. Asking family to leave by default replaces his choice with the staff member's assumption.
  37. The correct answer is C (Offer a female practitioner where possible, explain the short wait, and respect her modesty): Respecting modesty means offering a same gender practitioner where possible and explaining any short wait, which is easy when a female radiographer is already on duty. A chaperone with a male radiographer does not meet the stated request when the preferred option is available within minutes. Insisting on a day long delay or dismissing the request on the grounds of equal quality also fails to respect her needs.
  38. The correct answer is C (Stop the examination, since consent can be withdrawn at any time, and tell the radiologist): A lucid patient can withdraw consent at any moment, so the study stops and the radiologist is told. A signature records earlier agreement but does not bind him. Explaining the value of the last image and asking again turns a clear request into pressure. The radiologist can discuss options with him, but the decision to stop is his, and a lower dose does not remove his right to refuse.
  39. The correct answer is C (Assess her maturity and understanding for this decision, then proceed on her consent if competent): A young person with enough maturity and understanding of the nature and consequences of the decision can give valid consent, and the practitioner doing the procedure assesses this. No single age automatically confers or removes that ability. The GP's referral shows clinical need and cannot replace the assessment at the point of care. Telling her parents afterwards, when she has asked for privacy, is a confidentiality breach unless serious risk is identified.
  40. The correct answer is D (Proceed on the urgent request, as emergency care to prevent serious harm needs no consent): Emergency care needed to prevent serious harm to a person who cannot consent is lawful without consent, so the urgent scan goes ahead and the emergency basis is documented. A signature by a team leader or administrator does not create consent, and neither person is a legal substitute decision-maker. Identification matters for records but does not need to precede a time-critical scan. The key principle is necessity, not paperwork.
  41. The correct answer is A (The Privacy Act 1988 and Australian Privacy Principles): The Privacy Act 1988 and the Australian Privacy Principles are the federal framework for many health providers. State and territory health records laws sit alongside it, but each applies in only one jurisdiction. The My Health Records Act covers a particular national record system, not all patient information. The Ahpra Code sets professional expectations on confidentiality and is not a statute.
  42. The correct answer is D (Neither confirm nor deny attendance, take her details, and pass the message to the senior clinician): Even confirming attendance discloses health information, and a caller's identity and relationship cannot be verified by phone. Details such as a referring doctor's name can be known by others, and limiting what is revealed still reveals that a cardiac scan occurred. Concern about a missing person does not by itself justify disclosure. Taking her details and passing the message to the senior clinician lets the right person decide, for example whether to contact police.
  43. The correct answer is B (Ask him to explain in his own words what he will do about fluids at home): Asking him to explain in his own words what he will do shows whether he can act on the advice, and any gap points to your explanation. Word-for-word repetition can be recalled without understanding. A chart helps later but does not test comprehension now, and a repeated summary followed by an open invitation tends to get a polite nod. He is tired, so a specific request about his own plan is more reliable.
  44. The correct answer is A (Use short plain sentences, one idea at a time, and invite her questions after each step): Short plain sentences, one idea at a time, with questions invited after each step, suit a person with limited schooling and low stress tolerance. Handing the leaflet to a relative leaves her without the explanation she is entitled to hear.
  45. Reading the form aloud quickly or asking him to read it does not give him a real chance to question or refuse. A short explanation when he is already positioned on the table puts pressure on him to continue. The running late list is a pressure but it cannot override valid consent.
  46. The correct answer is B (Pause the treatment, go into the room and talk with her about what she needs): Her request to stop is a withdrawal of consent, so the therapist pauses the treatment and goes in to talk with her about what she needs. A nod on a monitor is not a clear, informed decision to continue.
  47. The correct answer is B (Politely decline his help and arrange a qualified interpreter by phone or video): A child should not interpret, and a qualified interpreter can be arranged by phone or video without delay. Allowing him to help with greetings keeps a child in an adult clinical conversation and may blur boundaries. A cleaner is an adult but is untrained and not bound to confidentiality.   48. The correct answer is A (Book a qualified interpreter and ask the pregnancy questions privately through the interpreter): A qualified interpreter used privately gives accurate answers on pregnancy and contrast reactions, and she can speak freely.   49. State services cover their own jurisdiction only, so they do not fit a national need. NAATI certifies interpreters but does not dispatch them to departments. A paid commercial agency can work, but it does not meet the free national requirement in the question.
  50. The correct answer is C (Use the best available means to communicate, proceed with the urgent scan and document it): Where no interpreter is available and delay risks harm, the team uses the best available means to communicate, scans, and documents what was done. A signature from a deteriorating patient on a translated form is neither practical nor necessary in an emergency. Explaining only afterwards omits the dignity and cooperation that some communication can offer. A radiologist can justify the urgency but does not stand in for the patient's consent.
  51. The correct answer is D (Acknowledge his worry, say who will give the results and when, and offer support): A caring reply that acknowledges his worry and names who will give the results and when stays within scope and supports him. Sending him to his GP is accurate but leaves his fear unaddressed. Telling him the scan looks clear is an unreported interpretation that could be wrong. General reassurance about most scans may prove false for him and so should be avoided.
  52. The correct answer is A (Arrange a female practitioner for the examination, with a gown and drape for her dignity): Female practitioners are rostered, so her preference can be met with a gown and drape for dignity. A chaperone does not change who performs the examination, which is what she asked for. She does not need to justify her preference in writing. Moving her to another site or time makes her pay for a request that the department can meet on the day.
  53. The correct answer is B (Health literacy, meaning the ability to find, understand and use health information): The term describes the person's own ability to find, understand and use information when deciding about care, which is what the educator names. The online-only version is tempting because many people search the internet, but it covers only one source. Speaking up for oneself is a related skill, yet it does not define understanding information. Adapting information to a person's background is what the practitioner does, not the patient's ability.
  54. The correct answer is D (A voluntary decision made with knowledge and understanding of benefits and risks): Consent is a voluntary decision based on real knowledge of the benefits and risks, and that is the definition the Board expects. A note that an explanation occurred shows information was given, not that the choice was free or understood. A one-off agreement is tempting for efficiency but consent must be revisited and can be withdrawn. A signed form is evidence, yet without understanding it does not make the decision valid.
  55. Pausing and then pressing him to try again shows the stop is not real and cuts across his request. Calling the referrer moves the decision away from the patient and delays the conversation he needs now. Rebooking straight away is considerate but settles the outcome before his concerns are heard.
  56. The correct answer is C (Thank them, use the name and pronouns given, and correct yourself if you slip): Inclusive practice means thanking the person, using the name and pronouns they have just given, and correcting yourself calmly if you slip. Explaining system limits is accurate but turns their disclosure into an administrative problem. Asking for pronouns they have already stated makes them repeat themselves. Asking them to explain the change is curiosity, not clinical need, and adds discomfort.
  57.   58.   59.   60.   61.   62. The correct answer is C (Safety, trust, choice, collaboration and empowerment, applied in every step of the care): Trauma-informed care is built on safety, trust, choice, collaboration and empowerment, which aim to avoid repeating harm. The patient rights charter is a real framework but describes entitlements, not trauma principles. The cultural continuum terms belong to cultural safety teaching.   63.   64.   67.   89. The correct answer is C (Welcome the relatives he names, explain to the group, then confirm with him that he agrees): Family involvement is central for many Torres Strait Islander people, and he has asked for his relatives, so welcoming them and then confirming his agreement keeps consent with him. A separate shorter summary treats his request as an afterthought. Directing the explanation to his uncle assumes a decision role he has not been given. Limiting the group to one relative ignores that the room can hold them and that he has chosen who he wants.
  90. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge the burden, ask what would help, and involve liaison and social work supports): Being far from Country and family is a real burden, so the therapist acknowledges it, asks what would help and connects her with liaison and social work supports. Stressing the importance of finishing treatment risks sounding like pressure before she has been heard. A break from treatment may be considered later but is premature before asking her. A social work referral alone skips acknowledgement and cultural liaison support.
  91. The correct answer is D (Acknowledge his concern, explain each step and offer liaison support): His mistrust has roots in past harm, so acknowledging it, explaining each step and offering liaison support addresses the barrier at the point of care. Explaining and recording his concern is professional but offers no support for how he feels. Acknowledging and then hurrying may reassure little and shorten the time he needs to feel safe. Correcting the triage label is worthwhile later but delays the care he is here for.
  92. The correct answer is A (Cultural sensitivity, which involves respecting the differences between cultures): The continuum runs from awareness, knowing cultures differ, to sensitivity, respecting those differences, to competence, applying cultural knowledge, to safety, judged by the patient and involving reflection on power. The description in the stem is respect for difference without necessarily applying detailed knowledge, which is sensitivity. Safety goes further and is defined by the patient's experience. Awareness stops at recognising difference, and competence requires applying knowledge in practice.
  93. Doing this privately protects his dignity and the safety check together. Confirming identifiers aloud at the desk uses the correct name but exposes private details in a full room. Relying on the wristband alone skips active confirmation, and checking the booking system first delays respectful care without confirming identity with him.
  94. Relying on recorded sex can miss a patient who could be pregnant. Asking quietly in the waiting area keeps the wording consistent but is still not private enough with others nearby. Having the partner complete the form with him shifts a personal history question to the wrong person and removes his own control of the answer.

  96.   97. The correct answer is D (Allergies, medicines, cannulas, infection status, mobility and language needs relevant to the patient's care): Background carries the relevant history the receiver needs, such as allergies, medicines, cannulas, infection status, mobility and language needs. The next action, urgency and timing belong to the Request. The reason for the call, the scan and the finding belong to the Situation. Current vital signs and the practitioner's view of the problem belong to the Assessment, so each of those is a true ISBAR item placed in the wrong part.
  98. The correct answer is C (I am worried he is deteriorating and I think this is urgent.): The Assessment is the speaker's own judgement of the problem and its urgency. The timing of the breathlessness is a description of what is happening, so it belongs to the Situation. Asking for a review within ten minutes is the Request. The allergy and cannula are history, which makes them Background. Each of these is correct information said at the wrong point of the call.
  99. The medication standard touches allergy recording but centres on the medication process. Clinical governance covers leadership, risk and workforce competence rather than handover and identification.   122. The correct answer is D (Decline, and ask the colleague to complete and sign the entries by late documentation): Only the person who delivered a treatment can truthfully sign for it, and a late entry by that person is the proper way to complete the record.   123. The correct answer is C (Of those who truly had the condition, 90% were correctly identified as positive.): Sensitivity starts from the people who truly have the condition and asks what share the test picked up, so 90% means 90 of every 100 affected people tested positive. Overall agreement with the reference method mixes diseased and healthy people. Correct negatives among healthy people describe specificity instead. The share of positive results that were real describes predictive value, which depends on how common the condition is.
  124. The correct answer is A (The systematic review, because it combines several well-designed randomised trials of the same question.): To decide whether a treatment works, the strongest evidence pools several well-designed randomised trials, which is what a systematic review does. A large cohort study is appealing, but patients were not randomly allocated, so other differences may explain the result. A laboratory study shows how a product might act but not whether patients benefit. A case series is detailed but has no comparison group.
  125. The correct answer is C (A clinical audit, which measures current practice against an existing standard.): Checking current practice against a standard the service already has is a clinical audit. Comparing groups of records is not a randomised trial because nothing is allocated by chance. Research needing prior ethics approval would aim to create new generalisable knowledge, which an internal quality check does not. Critical appraisal applies to a published paper, and none is being assessed here.
  126.   127. The correct answer is A (Relative risk 0.6, with 6 fewer severe reactions in every 100 patients treated.): Risk with the new routine is 9 out of 100 and with standard care 15 out of 100, so relative risk is 0.09 divided by 0.15, which is 0.6. The absolute difference is 0.15 minus 0.09, or 6 fewer reactions per 100. Inverting the ratio gives 1.67. Using 0.4 reports the relative reduction instead, and 9 per 100 is the new risk rather than the difference.
  128. The correct answer is D (The interval includes 1, so the study has not shown a clear reduction in repeat imaging): A confidence interval that includes 1 means no difference remains a plausible true value, so a clear reduction has not been shown. The point estimate of 0.80 looks encouraging but ignores the uncertainty around it. A wide interval means the estimate is imprecise, not that the study is invalid. The upper limit above 1 shows harm cannot be excluded, not that harm was demonstrated.
  129. The correct answer is B (A result this large would be unlikely if the aid had no real effect.): A p-value tells how surprising a result this large would be if the aid truly had no effect, so a small value makes chance an unlikely explanation. It is not the chance that the aid works. It does not give the size of the benefit or the share of patients helped. A small p-value alone cannot prove effectiveness.

  133.   139. The correct answer is C (Seek ethics review first, because testing something new for publication counts as research.): Trying a new technique on patients and planning to publish the findings is research, which needs review by a Human Research Ethics Committee before any data are collected.   140. The correct answer is A (Test B, because few healthy people would be treated by mistake.): Before a harmful treatment, a confirmatory test must produce few false positives, which means high specificity. Test B falsely flags only 2% of healthy people. Test A suits screening, where missing cases is the main worry, but a positive result from it would often be wrong here. Lower sensitivity is not a feature that suits screening.
  141. The correct answer is B (85%, worked out as 102 true negatives ÷ 120 people): Specificity uses only the people without disease, so it is 102 true negatives ÷ 120 = 0.85, or 85%. As a check, 102 + 18 = 120. The 15% figure is the false positive rate, which is the complement of specificity. Dividing by 200 uses the wrong group, and 90% is the sensitivity from the diseased group.
  142. The correct answer is D (Joining the best available research with clinical expertise and the patient's values and preferences.): Evidence-based practice joins the best available research, the practitioner's expertise and the patient's values and preferences. Informing the patient after the decision leaves their values out. Protocols are useful but are not the definition. Treating expertise as a fallback drops one of the three parts.
  143. The correct answer is C (Discuss the options with her and use the supine technique that suits her situation.): Evidence-based practice weighs the research, her situation and her preferences, and a workable supine technique exists.   144.   145. The correct answer is B (It spreads known and unknown differences evenly between groups, limiting confounders.): Random allocation gives each patient the same chance of either routine, which spreads age, health and unmeasured differences evenly and limits confounders. Hiding the allocation is blinding, a separate protection. Equal group sizes are a matter of design, not of randomisation itself. Representing the wider population relates to how patients are selected, not how they are allocated.
  146.   147. The correct answer is A (Ask what she was thinking and what influenced her decisions.): After describing the event in facts, the next step is to examine what she was thinking and what influenced her decisions.   148.   149.   150. The correct answer is C (Supervised hands-on practice, because adults learn best from immediate use of the skill.): Adults learn best when they see why learning matters, can use it soon and receive feedback while the skill is new. Supervised hands-on practice meets all three. A webinar and quiz test theory but not use. A month of observing delays application past next week.   151. The correct answer is C (Competence is ability to the required standard; performance is actual daily practice.): Competence is the ability to do a task to the required standard, while performance is what a person actually does in daily practice. One good assessment shows competence on that occasion but not daily performance. Reversing the definitions or treating competence as a registration status changes what the terms mean.
  152. The correct answer is A (Use de-identified data on approved systems, since audit still needs careful handling of patient information.): An audit still requires careful handling of patient information, so de-identified data on approved systems is the best route.
