A manager's assistant is not the person with authority to approve removing identifiable records.
  153. The correct answer is D (Recognising and Responding to Acute Deterioration, because worsening signs were not noticed or acted on promptly): The failure described is that observations were not reviewed after injection, so early signs of deterioration were missed for several minutes. That is the core concern of the deterioration standard, which covers detecting and acting on worsening vital signs. The medicine standard is tempting because contrast triggered the reaction, but the drug use itself is not what failed. The handover and governance standards are also relevant to safe care in general, yet nothing in the scenario describes a communication gap or a missing system, only an unreviewed chart.
  154. The correct answer is D (A near miss, which should still be reported so the weak step can be fixed): The mix up was caught before any exposure, so it never reached the patient and is classed as a near miss. Reporting it still matters because it shows exactly which identification step is weak, and that learning is free because nobody was harmed. A no-harm incident describes an event that did reach the patient, and an adverse event requires some harm to have occurred. A sentinel event is limited to death or severe harm, so an uncompleted identification error does not qualify.
  155. The correct answer is A (A sentinel event, sometimes called a never event in older terminology): Rare, serious events causing death or severe harm that sit on the national list are called sentinel events. A serious adverse event can describe severe harm, but it is a broad term that is not tied to the national list. Reportable and critical incident are local or state labels that vary between services and do not name the nationally listed category. Only the sentinel event term matches both the severity and the national list in the description.
  156. The correct answer is A (A sentinel event, needing immediate care, prompt reporting and a formal investigation): Wrong site treatment causing serious permanent harm is a sentinel event, so it needs immediate patient care, prompt reporting and a formal investigation. The plan being checked and signed does not change this, because the error arose at setup and the harm reached the patient. Calling it a general adverse event with audit reporting understates the required escalation. Locating the problem in the plan ignores the stated setup error, and an investigation run only by the treating team lacks the independence a formal review needs.
  157. The correct answer is B (Asking why it happened, finding system causes, then redesigning the process to prevent a repeat): A root cause analysis asks why the event happened and looks for system causes such as labelling, workload or form design. The fix is then a redesign of the process so the same trap does not catch the next person. A compliance check and more training assume the cause is individual behaviour, which leaves the system weakness in place. Ranking individual concerns repeats the blame approach the quality officer is arguing against, and grading severity or notifying a regulator does not explain the cause.
  158. The correct answer is C (Coach her and fix the workload and process pressures that made skipping the check normal): Knowingly skipping a check because lists run late is at-risk behaviour, which Just Culture answers with coaching and correction of the pressures that made the shortcut normal. Consoling is the response for an unintended slip, which this was not. A formal warning is reserved for reckless disregard and would overlook the workload that drove the behaviour. Removing her from injecting duties treats a competent practitioner as the problem and leaves the same pressure on everyone else.
  159. The correct answer is B (Regret, the facts as known, actions underway, and steps to prevent recurrence): The framework asks for an expression of regret, the facts as known, the actions already underway and the steps to prevent a repeat. Facts are shared as they are known, not held back until a final cause is confirmed. Compensation offers and staff names are not part of the conversation. Handing over an internal investigation report and omitting prevention both leave out elements the framework expects, and waiting for the investigation to close breaches the aim of timely disclosure.
  160. The correct answer is D (Report it through the incident system as a process failure and tell the supervisor): A required policy step was missed, so the process failed even though the patient was not harmed. Reporting through the incident system and telling the supervisor lets the service learn and decide on any further action. The patient's notes and the referrer are not the channel for a process failure. Personal reflection helps her learn but gives the service no data, and contacting the patient alone bypasses the supervisor and local disclosure policy.
  161. The correct answer is A (Tell the supervisor straight away so disclosure is done properly and in line with local policy): A newly independent practitioner should tell the supervisor straight away so that clinical review and open disclosure proceed together under local policy. Explaining the error alone risks guessing at effects or causes and reverses the order of escalation. The radiologist can help with clinical risk, but disclosure is not delegated to the referrer. Finishing paperwork before speaking up delays both the supervisor and the patient, who is already asking questions.
  162. The correct answer is C (Notify the Board, because a reasonable belief of risk to the public exists): Under the National Law, a practitioner who forms a reasonable belief that another practitioner's impaired practice places the public at risk of substantial harm must notify the Board. Telling the employer or escalating within the hospital is sensible but does not remove her personal legal duty. The radiation regulator oversees radiation safety, not practitioner impairment. The duty arises from the risk of harm, so she need not wait for harm to occur.
  163. The correct answer is B (A risk, scored as likelihood multiplied by consequence on a risk matrix): Risk is the likelihood that harm occurs combined with how severe that harm would be, usually scored on a matrix. A hazard rating looks only at severity and omits the chance of it happening. A control measure is what is done in response to a risk, not the risk itself. An incident rating looks back at an event that has happened, whereas risk concerns the chance of future harm.
  164. The correct answer is C (The faulty couch lock, which scores 12, the highest risk score): Risk score is likelihood multiplied by consequence: the cable scores 8, the couch lock 12 and the step 10. With time to fix only one item, the highest score goes first, and that is the couch lock. The step has the worst consequence and the cable is the most likely, so each is tempting, but neither has the top score. Choosing the quickest repair ignores the ranking the register was built to provide.
  165. The correct answer is B (Eliminate, substitute, engineering controls, administrative controls, then personal protective equipment last): The hierarchy runs from elimination through substitution and engineering controls to administrative controls, with personal protective equipment as the last line. Engineering controls change the workplace itself, so they rank above administrative measures that rely on people following rules. Swapping those two, moving engineering above substitution, or lifting PPE above administrative controls each puts a weaker control higher than a stronger one.
  166. The correct answer is D (Install an enclosed, shielded automated dispensing unit so staff no longer handle liquid): An enclosed, shielded automated unit removes the handling step itself, so staff are no longer exposed to the liquid. A supervisor rule is an administrative control and still depends on people behaving correctly. Face shields and gloves are protective equipment, the weakest level, and staff already wear some without stopping the splashes. Trays and a splash shield reduce the spread but keep manual dispensing, so the exposure remains.
  167. The correct answer is A (About 1.3 µSv/h, worked out as 12 µSv/h divided by 3 squared): A small sealed source behaves as a point source, so dose rate falls with the square of distance. The working is 12 ÷ 3² = 12 ÷ 9, which is about 1.3 µSv/h. Dividing by 3 gives 4 µSv/h and treats the fall as linear. Dividing by 27 gives 0.44 µSv/h and uses a cube law. Multiplying by 9 gives 108 µSv/h, which has the rate increasing as she steps away.
  168. The correct answer is D (2 µSv, worked out as 5 µSv/h × 0.4 h): Dose equals dose rate multiplied by time, and 24 minutes ÷ 60 = 0.4 hours. So 5 µSv/h × 0.4 h = 2 µSv. Using 24 directly overstates the dose sixty fold, dividing by the time gives the wrong units, and 0.24 of an hour is only 14.4 minutes. Checking 0.4 × 60 = 24 minutes confirms the conversion.
  169. The correct answer is A (20 mSv per year averaged over five years, no more than 50 mSv in one year): The standard occupational limit is 20 mSv per year averaged over five years, with no more than 50 mSv in any single year. A three year average changes the period the average is taken over. Raising the annual figure to 50 mSv and the ceiling to 100 mSv mixes up magnitudes. Keeping the correct average but stating a 30 mSv ceiling gives the wrong single year cap, so only the 50 mSv cap completes the rule.
  170. The correct answer is C (Dose limits do not apply to justified medical exposures, which rely on justification and optimisation): Dose limits are set for staff and the public, not for patients receiving a justified medical exposure. Those patients are protected through justification, optimisation and diagnostic reference levels where they exist. A reference level is a benchmark for review, not a cap. The public limit is not waived by a signature, and the occupational limit does not apply to patients merely because they are in a controlled area.
  171. The correct answer is A (Pause and contact the referrer to confirm the repeat is justified before any exposure is made): Reviewing the referral comes first, and an apparently unnecessary repeat should be queried with the referrer before any exposure. A signature does not remove the radiographer's own duty to check justification. Contacting the referrer after the exposure is too late to prevent the dose. A patient's agreement is not clinical justification, and a senior radiographer can advise but is not the person who ordered the study.
  172. The correct answer is D (Use the paediatric protocol, and seek radiation safety officer advice if a change seems needed): Optimisation for children relies on an evidence based paediatric protocol, with any change guided by expert advice such as from the radiation safety officer. A slightly grainy chest image is not a reason on its own to increase dose. Raising factors by personal judgement and matching a past image both bypass the protocol. Moving to a larger size band increases dose for appearance alone.
  173. The correct answer is B (Optimisation, also expressed as keeping doses as low as reasonably achievable): The statement describes keeping exposure as low as reasonably achievable while still answering the clinical question, which is how the Board describes the second of the three principles. Justification is tempting because it also involves benefit, but it asks whether the exposure should occur at all, before any technique is chosen. Limitation sounds protective, yet it concerns annual dose limits for people, not how an individual examination is planned. Authorisation is a regulatory approval step, not one of the three principles.
  174. The correct answer is C (Check the new state's legislation, regulator and forms, since these differ although national codes are shared): National codes are shared guidance, but each state and territory enacts its own legislation, with its own regulator and forms, so she must learn the Victorian arrangements. Applying the codes as written ignores local adoption differences. Board registration is a professional credential and does not transfer a state radiation licence. Checking only radiation law is incomplete because Victoria has its own occupational health and safety legislation rather than the model national scheme.
  175. The correct answer is D (The person conducting a business or undertaking, often shortened to PCBU): The law places the primary duty of care on the business or undertaking itself, to keep workers and others safe so far as is reasonably practicable. Officers must exercise due diligence, which is a real duty but sits beneath the primary one. Workers must take reasonable care, yet that is a secondary duty. The regulator enforces the law and issues notices, but it does not hold the duty to keep the workplace safe.
  176. The correct answer is D (Take reasonable care, stop using the room and report the fault, since workers share the duty): Workers share the duty, so she must take reasonable care, stop using a room whose safety interlock is faulty, and report it herself. A person watching the door is a weaker control that leaves a known faulty barrier in service. Stopping while leaving the report to the manager misses her own reporting duty. Testing before each patient does not repair the fault, and a documented check cannot make a flickering interlock reliable.
  177. The correct answer is B (Wait for adequate staff and use a slide sheet, hoist or other transfer aid): A frail 110 kg patient who cannot assist needs enough trained staff and a proper aid such as a slide sheet, hoist or transfer board, so the sensible step is to wait until both are available. A coordinated lift with good technique still asks two people to carry a heavy load. A slide sheet with only two people is an aid but not enough staff for this weight. A transfer board still needs a team to operate it safely.
  178. The correct answer is C (Clinical Governance, the framework for organisational accountability for safety and quality): The executive is asking about leadership, policy, incident learning and board monitoring across the organisation, which is the role of the Clinical Governance Standard. Communicating for Safety deals with passing critical information accurately between clinicians. Comprehensive Care works at the level of each patient's plan. Medication Safety does involve policy and incident monitoring, but only for medicines, so it is narrower than the overall framework asked about.
  179. The correct answer is A (Review a sample of records against the policy standard, then feed the results back to staff): A clinical audit measures actual practice against a defined standard, here the pregnancy check policy, using a sample of last quarter's records, and then feeds the results back so practice can change. One busy day is not representative, and raising findings informally at handover is not structured feedback. A confidence survey captures opinion, not what was recorded. Benchmarking against another hospital does not test compliance with this department's own policy.
  180. The correct answer is B (Take the machine out of use, label it, report it, and treat only once fixed): A failed daily output check means the machine is taken out of use, labelled, reported, and not used for treatment until the problem is fixed and rechecked by someone qualified. Choosing patients whose plans are less sensitive still treats on a machine that has failed its test. A correction factor from a colleague's estimate has no physics basis. Reporting and labelling are right, but a colleague is not the person who can approve return to clinical use.
  181. The correct answer is B (Inform the supervising specialist and radiation safety officer at once, then report through the incident system): The supervising specialist and the radiation safety officer must be told at once, because they can assess the clinical consequences and radiation risk of the wrong radiopharmaceutical. The incident report follows, and open disclosure to the patient should be planned with their input. Explaining first gives the patient information before the risk is assessed. The referring doctor is not the person who manages the nuclear medicine response. Writing the report first delays that advice.
  182. The correct answer is A (Partnering with Consumers and Clinical Governance, since disclosure and oversight both failed): The stem describes two separate failures: the patient was never told, which engages Partnering with Consumers, and the incident was only recorded locally, which engages Clinical Governance. Naming Clinical Governance alone explains the weak escalation but leaves the missing disclosure unexplained. The combinations that add Comprehensive Care are tempting because they pair a correct standard with a plausible one, but nothing in the stem concerns planning or delivering that patient's care.
  183. The correct answer is C (Communicating for Safety, since patient identity was passed on wrongly at handover): The wrong bed was named because identity information was passed on incorrectly between staff at handover, which is a communication failure covered by Communicating for Safety. Clinical Governance is the broad umbrella but does not name this specific failure. Comprehensive Care covers planning and delivering care, not accurate information exchange. Partnering with Consumers is about involving patients, yet the stem describes an error between staff, not a missed patient check.
  184. The correct answer is C (Eight standards, including Blood Management and Recognising and Responding to Acute Deterioration): The current framework has eight standards, and Blood Management and Recognising and Responding to Acute Deterioration are both among them. The ten standard version belongs to the earlier edition, which had separate handover and falls standards. The option with the correct count of eight fails because patient identification is no longer a stand alone standard. Seven is wrong because Blood Management is its own standard and is not merged into Medication Safety.
  185. The correct answer is A (Reducing time of exposure, increasing distance, and using shielding where appropriate): The three core controls in the Board's notes are less time, more distance and shielding where appropriate, because each one directly lowers dose. Restricting access is a sound administrative control, but it is not one of the three. Collimation is a good dose reduction technique that is not part of the named set. Personal dosimetry records dose after the fact and does not reduce it.
  186. The correct answer is D (Give written instructions based on time, distance and shielding, tailored to the patient's household): Written instructions built on time, distance and shielding and adapted to the household, which here includes young children, are the expected approach before discharge. A generic sheet is written but ignores who lives with her. Verbal advice alone is easily forgotten and written instructions are available. Sending her to her general practitioner leaves the department's own responsibility unmet, since the nuclear medicine team holds the relevant knowledge.
  187. The correct answer is A (Tell the radiation safety officer or supervisor so her exposure for that period can be estimated): A missed monitoring period should be reported to the radiation safety officer or supervisor promptly so the dose can be estimated and the legal record stays accurate. Wearing the dosimeter later and noting the gap covers only future work. A colleague's reading is an informal substitute that has not been approved. Raising it at a later meeting delays the estimate, and her belief that the dose was low has not been measured.
  188. The correct answer is B (Redesign the request form with a mandatory side field and add a forced pause): The analysis found system causes, so the best fix changes the process: a mandatory side field on the form plus a forced pause make the error hard to repeat. A refresher session depends on memory and attention, which failed three times. An optional box and a poster can be skipped on a busy list. Reducing list length helps with time pressure, but it leaves the form without a side field.
  189. The correct answer is C (Practising while intoxicated by alcohol or drugs, placing patients at risk): Practising while intoxicated by alcohol or drugs, with patients at risk, is a listed ground for mandatory notification. A health condition is notifiable only when it places the public at risk of substantial harm, and this one has not affected any patient. A continuing professional development shortfall is dealt with under registration standards. A minor error that was disclosed and corrected without harm does not reach the notification threshold.
  190. The correct answer is B (Express regret, explain the facts are being reviewed, and involve the supervisor): Open disclosure means honest regret, what is currently known, a statement that the facts are being reviewed, and involvement of the supervisor. Naming the colleague and giving full detail before the review is premature and not fair to either party. Asking the colleague to explain hands the conversation to someone who has not been supported. Deferring all questions about cause leaves out the supervisor and the continuing communication the mother is asking for.
  191. The correct answer is D (Reckless behaviour, calling for an accountability process alongside fixing the system): Deliberately defeating a known safety device and telling colleagues to do the same is a conscious disregard of a substantial risk, which Just Culture classes as reckless and which calls for accountability alongside fixing the system. At risk behaviour involves a drift in which the person does not see the risk, but she was trained on the danger. Human error is inadvertent, and this act was deliberate. Roster pressure should be reviewed, but it does not remove individual accountability.
  192. The correct answer is D (Collecting data, comparing it with a standard, and feeding results back to change practice): Continuous quality improvement is a loop in which data are collected, compared with a standard, and the results are fed back so that practice changes. Reporting to the executive is useful but does not itself change what happens in the department. Recognising staff who met the standard rewards success but gives no route to improve where the standard was missed. Repeating the audit annually measures again without acting on what was found.
  193. The correct answer is B (Take the faulty unit out of use, label and report it, and use the second unit): A known intermittent fault means the unit is withdrawn, labelled and reported, and a working unit is used for the urgent film. Using it once more is tempting because the film is urgent, but the second unit is available and the fault can recur at any exposure. A colleague at the isolator or a manager's approval may sound careful, yet neither makes the unit safe to use. The delay of fetching the other unit is the accepted cost.
  194. The correct answer is D (Tell the team about the hazard and agree positions, distance and shielding before continuing): The radiographer is responsible for telling everyone in the room about the hazard and agreeing positions, distance and shielding before the case goes on. Briefing only the nurse leaves the rest of the team unaware, and an apron is the last line of control rather than the plan. The surgeon is focused on the field and does not set radiation controls. Collimating and moving the unit helps, but without communication the new nurse can still step into the beam.
  195. The correct answer is C (The likely, major event rates high and goes first; the rare, minor event rates low): A risk matrix combines likelihood and consequence, so a likely event with major consequences rates high and is dealt with first, while a rare event with minor consequences rates low. Treating both equally defeats the purpose of ranking. Scoring consequence alone ignores half of the matrix. Calling the rare, minor event moderate overstates its score and would pull resources from the higher risk.
  196. The correct answer is B (Substitution, replacing the solvent with a less hazardous product): The cleaning task remains and a less hazardous product replaces the solvent, which is substitution. The solvent has left the department, which makes elimination tempting, but a cleaning chemical is still in use, so the hazard is reduced rather than removed. No equipment or process was redesigned, so it is not an engineering control. Staff may write a new procedure, yet the change that matters is the product itself.
  197. The correct answer is D (Issuing ear muffs, since personal protective equipment is the last line of defence): Personal protective equipment sits at the bottom of the hierarchy because it protects only the wearer and depends on correct use. Rotation relies on behaviour and enclosure leaves some noise, which makes them tempting, but both sit higher. A quieter compressor reduces the hazard at its source and also ranks above ear muffs. All three alternatives act on the hazard or exposure before protective equipment is needed.
  198. The correct answer is A (Stop the exposure, make the patient safe, get help, then report to the radiation safety officer): The sequence is to stop the exposure, make the patient safe, get help and then report to the radiation safety officer, who assesses dose and advises on notification. Telling biomedical engineering fixes the pedal but leaves the extra dose unreviewed. The manager should be informed, yet the manager does not assess radiation incidents. Recording details before the patient is safe and help has arrived puts paperwork ahead of care.
  199. The correct answer is C (The radiation safety officer, who assesses the event and advises on any further reporting): The designated radiation safety officer assesses the exposure and advises on any duty to notify the state regulator. The Board deals with practitioner conduct and notifications, not incident reporting for patient exposures. The national commission sets standards and does not receive local reports. The referring doctor should be informed clinically but does not decide regulatory reportability.
  200. The correct answer is A (Pause, follow local policy, and discuss with the referrer and supervisor first): A possible pregnancy changes the balance of benefit and risk, so the radiographer pauses, follows local policy and involves the referrer and supervisor before exposure. Urgency and the existing request are tempting, but the referrer did not know about the possible pregnancy. A signed form does not replace justification. Lowering factors without advice risks a non-diagnostic image and still skips the decision.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (4 m, since 16 ÷ 4² = 1 µSv/h): With the inverse square law, rate = 16 ÷ d². Setting this equal to 1 gives d² = 16, so d = 4 m. Check: 4 × 4 = 16 and 16 ÷ 16 = 1 µSv/h. At 3 m the rate is 16 ÷ 9 ≈ 1.8, which is above the target and cannot be rounded down. A linear fall would wrongly give 16 m, and 8 m gives 0.25 µSv/h, which is safe but further than the closest distance asked for.
  2. The correct answer is D (Blood Management, which covers the safe and appropriate use of blood and blood products): Blood Management is the Standard written for the safe and appropriate use of blood and blood products. Medication Safety is tempting because blood is given to patients, but it addresses medicines. Clinical Governance is the framework for all systems rather than a topic Standard. Comprehensive Care covers planning and delivering care generally and does not set the blood specific requirements.
  3. The correct answer is D (Pause the scan, check the record number and have the nurse confirm identity): Two sources conflict on date of birth, so identity is unconfirmed until the record number is checked and the treating nurse verifies the patient. A phone call to the doctor does not identify the person on the trolley. Scanning on a name match risks a wrong patient scan, and reporting afterwards cannot undo the dose. Issuing a new wristband before knowing which record is correct could fix an error in place.
  4. The correct answer is B (Ask her to state her name and date of birth, then compare both with the request): Asking the patient to state her own name and date of birth and comparing them with the request gives identifiers from the person herself. Reading the details out for her to confirm still lets a confused patient agree wrongly. Reception staff saw her at booking but cannot confirm who is in the room. A relative can help if the patient cannot answer, but she should be given the chance first.
  5. The correct answer is A (At least three, such as full name, date of birth and medical record number): National patient identification practice in Australia expects at least three recognised identifiers, such as full name, date of birth and a record number. Two matching identifiers feel adequate and are often used informally, but they fall below the standard for every patient. A signature on consent is not a recognised identifier. Limiting the third identifier to some patients leaves the rest less protected.
  6. The correct answer is A (Check the child's details with the carer and the booking system first): The date of birth is unconfirmed, so identification is incomplete until the carer and booking system agree on three identifiers. Two matching identifiers are not enough, and writing the doubt on the report leaves the risk in place. A carer's signature may support consent, but it does not identify the child. Checking the details first is quick and settles who is in the room.
  7. The correct answer is C (A time-out, and any member of the team may call a stop): The pause just before the procedure is the time-out, and every team member may call a stop. A sign-in check happens earlier, before anaesthesia or the procedure starts, and a sign-out check happens at the end. Limiting the stop to the proceduralist or a senior person is tempting because they lead the case, but it defeats a safeguard that depends on junior members speaking up.
  8. The correct answer is B (Call a stop, hold treatment and reconcile request with plan through the treating team): Radiation cannot be undone, so the therapist stops, holds treatment and resolves the discrepancy through the treating team before any beam is delivered. Two documents agreeing and an earlier check are tempting, but the request still conflicts. A patient's pain location is not the authority for treatment site. A reduced dose is not a recognised way to manage an unresolved side conflict.
  9. The correct answer is A (Place the left marker on the detector first, then check the side on the request): Placing the marker in the field and checking the side against the request before exposure completes the chain of patient, procedure, site and side. A digital marker added later depends on memory. Checking only on the image comes after the radiation has been delivered. Recording the patient's verbal confirmation is useful but cannot replace the physical marker.
  10. The correct answer is A (Zone 4 is the magnet room and Zone 1 is the general public area): Restriction rises with the zone number, so Zone 1 is the general public area and Zone 4 is the magnet room itself. Zone 2 is the interface where people are screened, so calling it the public area is a common mix-up. Zone 3 is the controlled area next to the scanner and not the magnet room. Placing Zone 3 as public would put unscreened people beside the magnet.
  11. The correct answer is C (Hold the scan until the device is checked against the manufacturer's MRI conditions): The scan waits until the device is identified and checked against the manufacturer's MRI conditions. Lower field strength can matter, but only when the device conditions allow it. The referrer cannot accept a risk that has not been assessed. A verbal cardiology go-ahead without device details is not verification, and screening for external metal does not address an implant.
  12. The correct answer is D (Stop them at the zone entrance and arrange screening and MRI conditional cleaning kit): The magnet field is always present, so the cleaner is stopped at the entrance, screened and given MRI conditional cleaning equipment. Removing personal items or waiting for a scan break does not remove the risk from a metal bucket. Leaving the bucket outside is helpful, yet the person still needs screening. Supervision cannot stop a ferromagnetic object being pulled toward the magnet once it is inside.
  13. The correct answer is B (Move the patient out of the magnet room to a safe area, then resuscitate): Zone 4 holds a strong permanent magnetic field, so the patient is taken to a safe area where the full team can work without projectile risk. Compressions in the bore do not remove the hazard and prevent proper airway and defibrillation care. Pausing for a safety officer check delays resuscitation, and a defibrillator is itself a ferromagnetic projectile, so bringing it in alone is not safe.
  14. The correct answer is D (A person pinned by a metal object and at risk of death): A quench is reserved for a life threatening event such as a person pinned by metal, because it has serious site consequences and takes time. In a cardiac arrest the patient is moved out of the room, so a quench adds nothing. A stuck object with nobody at risk is handled by controlled removal procedures. A suspected implant problem is managed by stopping the scan and checking safely.
  15. The correct answer is C (Radiofrequency heating and burns, so remove the loop and keep leads straight): Looped conductive leads can carry induced radiofrequency current and burn the skin, so the loop is removed and leads laid straight. Lowering the SAR setting is a real control but leaves the loop and the hot spot in place. Gradient nerve stimulation is a different hazard unrelated to leads. A ferromagnetic detector addresses projectiles and attraction, not heating.
  16. The correct answer is B (Before touching the patient, such as when she takes the patient's arm): The first of the 5 Moments applies before touching the patient, so hands are cleaned before she takes the patient's arm. The clean or aseptic procedure moment comes later, just before the injection. Exposure risk and after patient contact are also required moments, but they occur later and cannot replace the first one.
  17. The correct answer is D (Clean hands after touching the surroundings, before moving to another patient): The patient's surroundings count as part of the patient zone, so touching the rail and call button triggers the fifth moment. Skin contact is not required for that moment. A procedure moment does not apply because none was done, and wiping equipment does not clean her hands. Cleaning hands before leaving prevents carrying organisms to the next patient.
  18. The correct answer is D (Clean hands again just before the clean or aseptic procedure, then put on gloves): The moment before a clean or aseptic procedure requires a fresh hand clean directly before handling sterile items, then gloves are applied. Cleaning gloved hands does not make them clean beforehand and gloves are not a substitute for hand hygiene. An earlier clean at the greeting does not cover touching the trolley and equipment since. Moments after cannulation are also required but do not replace the moment before.
  19. The correct answer is B (Wash hands with soap and water at the sink): Alcohol does not reliably kill Clostridioides difficile spores, so hands are washed with soap and water, which physically removes them. Longer contact time or extra alcohol products do not change this. Rubbing now and washing later leaves spores on the hands while she touches other items. Washing straight after glove removal is the safe sequence.
  20. The correct answer is C (Change gloves between patients and clean hands, as gloves are no substitute): Gloves are changed between patients and hands are cleaned each time, because gloves do not replace hand hygiene. Rubbing gloved hands is not an approved practice and can damage gloves. A set time interval ignores the patient to patient spread risk. Restricting changes to known infections misses unrecognised carriage, since standard precautions apply to everyone.
  21. The correct answer is A (Place it in a sharps container at the point of use without recapping): Sharps are disposed of at the point of use in a sharps container, without recapping. Carrying an exposed needle even in a dish risks a stick on the way. Passing it to the nurse transfers the hazard and hand passing sharps is unsafe. A yellow waste bag is not puncture resistant and puts waste handlers at risk.
  22. The correct answer is B (Close and seal the container, replace it and report the overfill): An overfilled sharps container is closed, replaced and the overfill is reported so the cause can be fixed. Delaying the replacement leaves the next case without a compliant container. Stacking and taping containers is an improvised arrangement that can leak or allow a stick. Raising the overfill only at a later meeting gives no timely correction for staff who use the room tonight.
  23. The correct answer is A (Wash the site, report at once and follow the exposure management plan): After a needlestick the site is washed first, the incident is reported at once and the exposure management plan is followed so timely assessment happens. Finishing the patient first lets the assessment window narrow. Squeezing the finger is not part of the recommended first aid. Delaying the wash while arranging tests puts the sequence in the wrong order.
  24. The correct answer is A (Hand hygiene, PPE as needed, sharps handling, respiratory hygiene, cleaning, waste handling): Standard precautions are the baseline measures used for all patients: hand hygiene, PPE as needed, sharps handling, respiratory hygiene, cleaning and waste handling. The other lists each include a transmission based measure such as contact isolation, droplet precautions or cohorting. These are added for known or suspected infection and are not part of the standard set, and one list also drops waste handling.
  25. The correct answer is C (Add the PPE listed on the sign to standard precautions, then clean the equipment): Droplet precautions are added to standard precautions, and the sign lists the PPE to use, including eye protection. A mask alone does not meet the sign. Using the sign's PPE in place of standard precautions drops baseline measures such as hand hygiene. Equipment is cleaned after use between patients, not only when visibly soiled, because organisms are not always visible.
  26. The correct answer is C (Discard the unlabelled syringe and draw up a fresh, labelled one): A syringe with no label cannot be verified, so it is discarded and a fresh one drawn up and labelled. Appearance and volume do not identify a drug or its strength. A phone confirmation depends on memory and does not restore the checks. Matching it to another syringe assumes they are the same, and labelling after the fact does not prove what was drawn up.
  27. The correct answer is B (Stop the injection, call for help and follow the acute reaction plan): Symptoms of a possible allergic reaction mean the injection stops, help is called and the acute reaction plan is followed. Slowing the rate still delivers contrast to a reacting patient. Observing before calling help wastes time if airway problems develop. Removing the cannula loses the route needed for emergency drugs, and moving the patient away takes him from equipment and staff.
  28. The correct answer is D (Patient, drug, dose, route and time, plus allergy status, expiry and labelling): The person giving the drug repeats the full set of checks: patient, drug, dose, route and time, plus allergy status, expiry and labelling. Relying on the pharmacy label for allergy and expiry hands over checks that belong to the administering person. Skipping route and time ignores errors in the order. An identity check at booking does not replace identification at the point of giving.
  29. The correct answer is D (Pause, document the history and discuss with the radiologist before contrast): New allergy history from the patient overrides an incomplete form, so the scan is paused, the history documented and the radiologist decides how to proceed. Being ready for a reaction does not replace that review. Choosing another agent is a medical decision outside the radiographer's scope. A signed consent form does not remove the need for clinical assessment of the risk.
  30. The correct answer is C (Raise the rails, secure the patient, and keep him in view or have staff stay): A drowsy patient at risk of falls needs raised rails, secure positioning and a person keeping him in view or staying with him. Lowering the table and raising the rails reduces injury risk but leaves him unobserved. Asking him to wave relies on a sedated patient to act. A passing visitor is not trained or responsible for his safety.
  31. The correct answer is A (Keep the patient safe, take the trolley out of use and report the fault): A faulty trolley is taken out of use and reported straight away so nobody else uses it. Moving the patient and reporting later leaves the trolley available meanwhile. Repairing a brake with a department tool kit is outside the radiographer's role. Using it for transfers still exposes patients and staff to a fall risk with unreliable brakes.
  32. The correct answer is B (Limit time on the hard surface, reposition her as planned, report the change): Pressure injury prevention means limiting time on hard surfaces, repositioning according to the care plan and reporting skin changes promptly. A barrier cream does not relieve pressure. Waiting until tomorrow to report loses the chance for early review and care. Massaging a reddened area can damage fragile skin and adds to the injury.
  33. The correct answer is A (Use the slide sheet with two staff, keep the load close and avoid twisting): Using the slide sheet with both free staff keeps the load close to the body and removes the need to twist, which is how manual handling injuries to staff and patient are prevented. One colleague alone is not enough to control a heavy patient who cannot assist. A transfer board is a reasonable device, but lifting across it brings back the compressive load that sliding avoids. Bending to pull uses the right equipment and team yet adopts a posture that strains the back.
  34. The correct answer is D (Take the machine out of use, label it and treat no patients until it passes): A repeated out of tolerance output means the machine is not fit for treatment, so it is removed from use, labelled and kept out until a qualified person fixes it and it passes. A correction factor applied at the console is not a verified solution and changes every delivered dose on an unproven basis. Recalibrating the machine is a task for the physicist or engineer, not the therapist running the check. The oncologist is responsible for the prescription rather than machine performance, so their agreement cannot return the machine to use.
  35. The correct answer is C (Stop use, report the fault and arrange another scanner for the child): A new grinding noise during rotation points to a mechanical fault, so the scanner is stopped, the fault reported and the child scanned on another unit so that care is not delayed. A telephone opinion cannot confirm that a rotating gantry is safe without inspection. Phantom results describe image quality and say nothing about bearings or the drive. Slowing rotation changes how the fault is stressed but leaves a patient on a machine that may fail.
  36. The correct answer is B (Record and report the result; keep the room out of use until it passes): A result outside the manufacturer's limit means the detector is not performing to its set standard, so the result is recorded, reported and the room stays out of use until it passes. Limiting the room to non-urgent work still sends patients through an unverified detector. Recalibrating in service mode is engineer work and logging two results blurs the failure. Telling the physicist is correct, but verbal guidance does not substitute for a passing test before use.
  37. The correct answer is C (A comparison of practice with a standard, fed back to change practice): A clinical audit measures actual practice, such as how often a time-out is documented, against a standard and feeds the findings back to change practice. A structured event review examines one incident to find system causes, so it does not measure routine practice over three months. Accreditation compares a department with national standards, but it is an external, periodic assessment. A staff survey captures opinion, which may differ from what is recorded in the chart.
  38. The correct answer is A (Remove people in immediate danger, then raise the alarm): The first letter of RACE is Remove, so people in immediate danger are moved first, and raising the alarm follows directly. Alarm first reverses the first two steps and delays the rescue of the two patients beside the fire. Closing the door after removal is a correct later step, but going to it before the alarm leaves others unaware. Containing the fire before moving the patients puts the building ahead of people.
  39. The correct answer is B (Contain the fire by closing doors, then Extinguish if safe or Evacuate): After Remove and Alarm, the letter C stands for Contain by closing doors, and E means Extinguish if safe or Evacuate. Calling the brigade belongs to the alarm step that has already happened, so it is not the C. Insisting on extinguishing the fire before anyone leaves ignores the condition that it must be safe. Clearing the corridor is sensible housekeeping but it does not limit the spread of smoke and fire.
  40. The correct answer is D (Call for help on the code blue number and follow DRSABCD, using the recovery position): Calling code blue sends trained help and equipment while you remain responsible for the patient, and DRSABCD with the recovery position protects the airway of a person who is breathing. Staying beside him without calling leaves him without backup, which is the gap in the recovery position option. Treating him as a faint and measuring vital signs focuses on observations when he is unresponsive. Fetching equipment first delays the alarm and leaves him alone, and he does not need a defibrillator while he is breathing.
  41. The correct answer is C (They must be found quickly in an emergency, so learn them beforehand): Emergencies leave no time to search, so every new employee learns where the trolley, defibrillator, exits and extinguishers are before they are needed. Limiting this to the emergency team ignores that the first person at a collapse is often whoever is nearest. Waiting for the annual drill leaves months of exposure, and a drill covers fire rather than all emergencies. Signs help but are hard to read under stress, and equipment moves are managed by updating knowledge rather than skipping it.
  42. The correct answer is C (Ask for full identifiers from the chart and confirm at the bedside): With two similar surnames in adjacent bays, full identifiers from the chart are checked and then confirmed with the patient at the bedside. Confirming a surname and room number by phone checks the same weak details that created the risk. Asking a ward nurse substitutes another person's recollection for identification. A room number describes a location, which changes, so matching it to a request form does not identify the patient.
  43. The correct answer is B (Patient identifiers, what was given and when, instructions to follow, and pending items): A safe handover tells the nurse who the patient is, what was given and when, what to do next and what is still pending. Batch numbers and scanner settings are accurate records but give the ward nothing to act on. Diagnosis and history without scan details leave the nurse unaware of what has just happened. Stating the report time covers one pending item but omits instructions for the ward, so the nurse still has to guess.
  44. The correct answer is D (Review the process as a system and redesign the form to prompt the pause): A system review with a redesigned form that prompts the pause fixes the cause, because busy staff will keep missing a step that nothing reminds them to do. Re-auditing after a reminder leaves the same prompt-free process in place for ten more cases. Asking each therapist for a personal goal depends on memory under pressure, which is exactly what failed. Counselling the individuals concerned treats a pattern across the department as a personal failing.
  45. The correct answer is D (Discard the pack and open a new sterile one): A pack that touched the floor can no longer be trusted, so it is discarded and a new sterile one opened, which is how doubtful items are handled in aseptic technique. Inspecting contents for dampness cannot show contamination that is not visible. A second drape covers the field but still brings in an item of doubtful sterility. Wiping the wrapper with alcohol cleans the outside but does not make the pack sterile.
  46. The correct answer is A (Give written instructions and apply time, distance and shielding principles): Written instructions with time, distance and shielding advice give the patient a lasting record and keep exposure to other people as low as reasonably achievable. Hygiene and flushing advice is part of the picture but does not address exposure from the patient on a train. Dose limits for the public do not apply to a patient receiving a justified medical exposure, so holding release to them is incorrect. The right principles delivered only as brief spoken advice are easily forgotten after discharge.
  47. The correct answer is B (Pause and screen the eye injury before she enters the restricted zone): A record that conflicts with the signed form is resolved before she enters the restricted zone, because an eye injury may have left a metallic fragment. Repeating the same questions will probably give the same answer she has already given. A second declaration is more paperwork and does not investigate the injury. Using a lower field or relying on the knee being far from the eye does not remove the danger to the eye once she is in the magnet area.
  48. The correct answer is B (Clean the couch, detector and accessories between patients): Standard precautions require cleaning the couch, detector and accessories between patients, because contamination is not always visible. Cleaning only what looks soiled misses surfaces carrying organisms. Skipping the detector ignores that it can contact skin or fluid, especially with a weeping wound. One dry wipe moves dust around without removing or killing organisms.
  49. The correct answer is C (Report it through the incident system and tell the supervisor): A skipped identification check is a process failure even when the details happened to match, so it is reported as a near miss through the incident system and to the supervisor. Phoning the patient helps this one case but does not tell the service where the process is weak. Raising it as a reminder at a meeting spreads the message without recording the event. A chart note records what happened to the patient but is not read for system learning.
  50. The correct answer is A (Move it to a safe place, remind the colleague, report if repeated): A hazard that takes seconds to remove is removed at once, then the colleague is reminded and the matter is reported if it recurs. A formal report naming the colleague after one lapse is out of proportion and skips the conversation that usually fixes it. Calling the colleague back leaves the apron in the doorway for as long as it takes them to return. A sign warns people but a trolley wheel can still catch the apron.
  51. The correct answer is A (Administrative controls such as signs, and personal protective equipment): The weakest measures are administrative controls such as signs and personal protective equipment, because both depend on people acting correctly each time. Engineering controls build safety into the environment and rank well above them, so pairing them with administrative measures is incorrect. Substitution and engineering controls are both higher in the hierarchy, so that pair is not the least effective. Substitution also outranks personal protective equipment, so combining the two misreads the order.
  52. The correct answer is D (Notify the Board, as a reasonable belief of impaired practice triggers a legal duty): A practitioner who forms a reasonable belief that another practitioner is placing the public at risk through impaired practice has a personal legal duty to notify the Board. Escalating again within the workplace does not move the duty to someone else, particularly when the manager has already failed to act. Informing human resources deals with the employer but not the regulator. Asking the peer to notify the Board themselves leaves the public exposed while the follow-up waits.
  53. The correct answer is A (It applies to every registered practitioner and shows up in small daily acts): The domain is written for every registered practitioner, so a new graduate shows it through small daily acts such as clarifying a handover or raising a concern. Tying it to a management role is tempting because leadership is often linked to titles. Adding a registration or experience threshold sounds careful but the domain contains no such condition. Requiring a formal program before it applies confuses development opportunities with the expectation itself.
  54. The correct answer is B (Improve healthcare delivery, lead stewardship of healthcare systems, and show leadership as a professional practice): The domain lists improving healthcare delivery, leading and supporting stewardship of healthcare systems, and showing leadership as a professional practice. Each other set keeps two genuine capabilities and swaps the third for a look-alike drawn from another area of practice, such as supporting learning or generating evidence. A safety advocacy wording sounds close but is not the stewardship capability as written. Only one set matches all three as the Board states them.
  55. The correct answer is C (Is the student competent, is the task in scope, and is supervision right for the risk): Safe delegation rests on competence for this task, whether the task sits within the student's scope, and supervision matched to the risk. Checking enrolment, documentation and patient information is useful but does not test whether the student can do the task safely. A routine task with patient consent still needs scope and supervision decided. Being reachable by phone is one possible supervision level, but it is not matched to the risk of setting up this patient.
  56. The correct answer is D (Decline politely, explain the student needs supervision, then supervise it or share the load): Busyness does not change the supervision standard, and two observed attempts do not make independent positioning safe. The practitioner should decline respectfully, give the reason and either supervise or share the work. Staying nearby or checking the image afterwards still leaves the patient unsupervised during the risky part. Raising the issue with the manager later is a sensible follow-up but does nothing for this patient now.
  57. The correct answer is C (The technologist remains accountable for the decision that assigning the task was safe): A task can be delegated, but the decision that delegating it was safe stays with the delegating technologist, and that decision is what is examined. Presence during the task matters for supervision but does not define who made the delegation decision. Splitting the blame between the trainee's task and earlier training misses the decision itself. The manager's authority covers the department, not this technologist's choice to assign the task.
  58. The correct answer is B (The range of activities a person is educated, competent and authorised to carry out): Scope of practice has three parts: education, competence and authorisation. Having been educated and having watched a task does not show competence or authority to do it alone. A colleague's sign-off may support competence but does not by itself authorise the activity. A position description lists duties for a role, whereas scope belongs to the person and depends on all three parts.
  59. The correct answer is A (Image the emergency patient first, then tell the outpatient about the delay and inform the coordinator): Clinical priority follows risk and urgency, so the suspected serious injury is imaged first. The outpatient is owed an honest explanation of the delay and the coordinator should hear about the pressure straight away. Waiting for the coordinator to choose delays an obvious priority. Ranking by distress or length of wait is understandable but does not reflect clinical risk. Leaving the update until the routine study is done keeps the outpatient uninformed for longer.
  60. The correct answer is D (Tell the coordinator early what is at risk and offer a specific suggestion): A leader says early that the day will not work, names what is at risk and brings a proposed solution. Raising gaps at the huddle and asking someone else to solve them leaves the leader without a contribution. Emailing everyone shares the worry with people who cannot change staffing. Telling the coordinator the day is unworkable is timely but gives them nothing specific to decide on.
  61. The correct answer is C (Tests or treatments giving little or no benefit, or where harm or cost outweighs benefit): Low-value care is defined by benefit: it gives little or no benefit, or the harm or cost outweighs the benefit. Experimental care awaiting approval is a different issue about evidence and regulation. An expensive service can still be highly valuable, so cost alone does not define it. Shared decision making is encouraged in the same initiative, but skipping it does not make a test low value.
  62. The correct answer is B (Phone the referrer, ask what has changed, and share what the recent study showed): Every exposure must be justified, and nothing here shows what a repeat would add. Contacting the referrer, asking what has changed and sharing the recent result tests the justification before exposure. Asking afterwards is too late because the dose is already given. The patient's usual GP is not the person who made this request. Reducing field size optimises the dose but cannot make an unjustified exposure justified.
  63. The correct answer is D (Contact the referrer, ask about the clinical question, and agree the next step before exposing): Appropriate use means the examination answers a real clinical question, so the radiographer contacts the referrer and agrees the next step before exposing. Sending the patient away without a conversation delays care when a quick call could resolve it. A patient's description of symptoms cannot replace the referrer's question and leaves the radiographer choosing the study. Asking the radiologist after exposure is too late to prevent an unjustified dose.
  64. The correct answer is A (Radioactive, clinical and sharps waste must go to the correct stream whatever the sustainability goals): Sustainability goals matter, but they sit beside waste rules, so radioactive, clinical and sharps waste each follow their proper stream. Decay to background removes the radiation issue but a used syringe is still a sharp and clinical waste. Sending only radioactive waste to the decay store misses the clinical items. Monitoring and label removal are good practice, yet they do not make the items recyclable.
  65. The correct answer is A (Using the minimum contrast volume needed for the clinical purpose of each examination): Using only the contrast volume each examination needs reduces waste and cost while keeping image quality and care. A single standard volume is simple but gives some patients more contrast than needed. Moving to single-use items trades cleaning time for extra waste. Preparing the whole list in advance saves minutes but leads to discarded product if the list changes.
  66. The correct answer is C (Medicare is the national public insurance scheme, and the MBS lists services that attract a rebate): Medicare is the national public insurance scheme, and the Medicare Benefits Schedule lists the services that attract a rebate, including many imaging services. The schedule fee is a benchmark, and clinics are not required to charge it. Subsidised medicines belong to the Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme. The schedule lists services and items, not the providers who may bill.
  67. The correct answer is D (Check with the local state radiation regulator and the department's radiation safety officer): States and territories hold the main powers over radiation safety legislation and licensing, so the local state regulator and the department's radiation safety officer are the right sources. The Board deals with practitioner registration, not state radiation licences. The Queensland regulator has no authority over practice in Western Australia. A senior colleague can be helpful but may not know current licence details.
  68. The correct answer is B (Explain that funding differs between public and private services and refer billing questions to administration): Public and private services are funded differently, and the administration team can give accurate billing and rebate information. Sending her to the referring doctor's staff puts her with people who do not manage this clinic's fees. Looking up the schedule oneself is helpful in tone but billing advice belongs to administration. Quoting a range with a caveat still risks misleading the patient when the radiographer is unsure.
  69. The correct answer is A (Clinical Governance, because the other standards depend on its systems of accountability): Clinical Governance is the first standard because the others depend on organisational systems for accountability, policy, measurement and learning. Partnering with consumers, comprehensive care and communicating for safety are all important, which makes them tempting. Each of them still relies on the governance framework to operate reliably.
  70. The correct answer is B (The plan, do, study, act cycle, run on a small scale first): Trying the prompt card on one list, studying the result and then acting on it is the plan, do, study, act cycle run on a small scale. An audit measures compliance but does not test a new idea. Root cause analysis examines a specific event rather than trialling a change. Benchmarking compares results with other hospitals and tests nothing in the department.
  71. The correct answer is C (Report the near miss factually and suggest a name alert and an extra identifier check): Near misses reveal weaknesses before a patient is hurt, so they are reported factually with a practical fix such as a name alert and an extra identifier. Telling patients about an event that did not reach them adds worry without improving the process. A verbal chat leaves no record for the safety team. Writing it in patient files does not feed the incident system or help other staff.
  72. The correct answer is D (An event that could have caused harm but did not, and which reveals a weakness): A near miss is an event that could have caused harm but did not, and it exposes a weakness worth fixing. Minor harm that was corrected is still an incident with harm. Harm judged unavoidable also involves harm, so it is not a near miss. A staff injury is a separate type of event and does not describe potential patient harm that was avoided.
  73. The correct answer is C (Take part in open disclosure per organisational policy, and treat mandatory notification as a separate duty): Open disclosure follows the organisation's policy and starts promptly, while mandatory notification under the National Law is its own duty with its own requirements. The local incident report serves internal learning and does not discharge that duty. Handing the conversation to one doctor and assessing notification later leaves both duties poorly met. Waiting for a confirmed cause before meeting the family delays honest communication that the framework expects early.
  74. The correct answer is D (Acknowledge what happened, express regret, explain what is known, and describe actions to prevent recurrence): The Australian Open Disclosure Framework combines acknowledgement, an expression of regret, an explanation of what is known and what is being done to prevent it happening again. Naming a staff member as responsible shifts blame before any review has taken place. Leaving out regret makes the conversation factual but cold. Skipping acknowledgement and offering a likely cause moves into speculation before facts are established.
  75. The correct answer is B (Ask patients, carers and community groups, including Aboriginal communities, what times work for them): Co-design starts by asking patients, carers and communities, including Aboriginal communities, what suits them, before options are fixed. A working group that invites representatives to approve a plan gives them a veto but not authorship. Commenting on proposed times and piloting a decided schedule both bring people in after the key choices are made, so neither shapes the design from the start.
  76. The correct answer is A (Working with other professions toward the patient's goals, respecting roles and deciding together): Interprofessional practice means professions work with each other toward shared patient goals, respect each other's roles and decide together. Separate assessments with a summary note share information but not decisions. A leader who assigns every role coordinates tasks without joint decision making. Learning about other disciplines describes interprofessional education, which supports practice but is not the same thing.
  77. The correct answer is D (Pause the team calmly, state the concern, and ask for the check to be completed): The radiographer speaks up directly, calmly and before the risk develops, asking for the check to be done. Asking a nurse to relay the concern leaves the person who noticed it silent. Waiting until the first stage ends lets the omission stand during the highest-risk moment. Noting it and reporting afterwards is a sound habit, but it does nothing for this patient now.
  78. The correct answer is C (State what you see, state your concern, say what you suggest, and ask for agreement): The pattern moves from the observation to the concern, then to a suggestion and finally a request for agreement. Asking for agreement before the suggestion is stated leaves the colleague agreeing to nothing specific. Opening with the concern or with the suggestion gives the colleague no shared facts to respond to, which makes the exchange sound like criticism.
  79. The correct answer is B (Escalate through the organisation's chain of command before proceeding with the exposure): When a serious risk remains after speaking up once, the practitioner escalates through the chain of command and holds the exposure until identification is resolved. Reporting through the incident system is worthwhile, but proceeding first leaves the wrong-patient risk in place. Repeating the request publicly adds pressure yet still ends in an exposure. Phoning the referrer afterwards is too late to prevent harm.
  80. The correct answer is A (Arrange a professional interpreter and allow extra time before the first treatment session): Advocacy here means arranging a professional interpreter and giving extra time to an anxious patient.   81.   82. The correct answer is B (The person receiving care, not the practitioner providing it): Cultural safety is defined by the person receiving care, who decides whether the experience felt safe. Standards, liaison officer review and peer reflection all support good practice, and each is useful for improvement. None of them can replace the patient's own experience as the measure, so none can be the one who decides.
  83. The correct answer is D (Ask if she wants a support person, offer a same-gender practitioner, and involve the liaison officer): Cultural safety starts with the patient: ask whether she wants a support person, offer a same-gender practitioner and bring in the liaison officer. Inviting family or asking about their preferences assumes what she wants rather than asking her.
  84. The correct answer is C (Follow the command structure, focus on urgent cases, and keep identification and radiation safety in place): In a declared surge the radiographer follows the command structure, images the most urgent patients first and keeps the core safety steps in place. Reducing identification to one check or recording radiation safety afterwards trades protection for speed, and misidentification risk rises during chaos. Triaging independently ignores the structure that coordinates the whole response.
  85. The correct answer is D (Follow the command structure, communicate clearly, keep core safety steps, and report problems quickly): In a disaster response, roles can change, so the practitioner follows the command structure, communicates clearly, keeps core safety steps and reports problems quickly. Saving concerns for a later debrief lets problems grow. Waiting for written instructions slows the response, and swapping roles privately undermines the command structure.
  86. The correct answer is D (Follow the approved process, explain the safety reason, and pass speed concerns to the process owner): Leadership here means following the approved process, explaining why it protects identification, and taking the speed concern to the person who can fix it. Asking for a faster process without addressing the colleagues leaves the unsafe method in use. Raising the practice at a team meeting skips the fair first step of speaking to them. A side-by-side trial keeps the old method alive while the risk remains.
  87. The correct answer is B (The expected benefit to the patient should outweigh the risk and answer a real clinical question): Justification asks whether the expected benefit to the patient outweighs the risk and whether the examination answers a real clinical question. A valid referral, a low dose and consent are each good practice, but they can all be present while the examination adds nothing to care. Keeping dose low belongs to optimisation, which comes after justification has been established.
  88. The correct answer is C (Do not perform the examination, and document the discussion with the referrer): Once the discussion shows no clinical reason and the earlier study answered the question, the examination is not justified, so it is not performed and the conversation is recorded.   89. The correct answer is B (Decline until the assistant's competence and role scope are confirmed, then supervise closely): Safe delegation needs a delegate whose competence and role scope are confirmed, with supervision matched to the risk. Direct supervision of an unconfirmed assistant relies on the therapist to catch errors as they happen. A phantom trial shows manual skill but not whether the task sits in her role. A supervisor's verbal comment that she has watched enough does not establish competence.
  90. The correct answer is A (Describe the facts of what happened, without opinions or conclusions about character): A useful incident report sticks to observable facts so reviewers can find system causes. Adding a personal view of cause moves from facts to speculation and can bias the review. Sending a copy to the colleague first compromises the report's independence and delays lodging. Mentioning the report in the clinical notes mixes two records that serve different purposes.
  91. The correct answer is D (Image the unstable ward patient first and tell the colleague the clinical need comes first): Priority follows clinical risk, so the unstable patient is imaged first and the colleague is told why. Asking the coordinator while starting the friend's examination lets the booked patient go first in practice. Following the booking and apologising treats a scheduling issue as more important than a clinical one.
  92. The correct answer is A (Raise the problem together with a proposed solution): A leader presents the problem together with a proposed way forward, which gives the meeting something to act on. Waiting for an audit, collecting endorsement from colleagues or briefing the manager beforehand can each look responsible. None of them brings a solution to the discussion, so none separates leadership from complaining.
  93. The correct answer is C (A horizontal beam cross table lateral, with the patient and collar left undisturbed): In trauma the patient stays still, so a horizontal beam lateral taken with the patient flat shows the cervical spine without moving the neck. Removing the collar is a decision for the doctor in charge, which rules out the AP view. The open mouth view is an add on for C1 and C2 and needs a patient who can be moved safely. A flexion view moves the neck and is unsafe before injury has been excluded.
  94. The correct answer is A (A left lateral decubitus abdomen, right side up, taken with a horizontal beam): Free gas rises to the highest point, and it only shows as a gas layer when the beam runs horizontally across the patient. Her low blood pressure rules out the erect chest film, which would otherwise be a good choice. A vertical beam through a lateral decubitus position cannot show the gas layer. A higher exposure on a supine film does not move the gas to where it can be seen.
  95. The correct answer is C (Increased object to image distance causes magnification and a loss of sharpness): Structures further from the receptor are blurred and magnified, and here the fingers are lifted away from it. That is object to image distance, not source distance. Raising kVp changes penetration and contrast, not geometric size. The inverse square law describes how dose falls with distance and does not explain the larger, softer fingers.
  96. The correct answer is D (Longer distance reduces magnification, but the inverse square law cuts the exposure reaching the receptor): A longer source to image distance reduces magnification and improves sharpness, which is what the practitioner saw. It also reduces the intensity reaching the receptor by the inverse square law, so the unchanged exposure factors now underexpose the image and give noise. Distance does not change kVp or the focal spot size. The fix is to raise the mAs to compensate for the extra distance.
  97. The correct answer is B (Repeat after coaching a full, held deep breath): A good chest film shows about ten posterior ribs above the diaphragm, so seven means the patient did not inspire fully. Shallow inspiration crowds the bases and makes the heart look larger, so the film cannot answer the clinical question. The patient is cooperative, so coaching is the direct fix. Lying supine raises the diaphragm, and kVp does not change the depth of breath.
  98. The correct answer is C (The patient was rotated, so the heart and mediastinum may look distorted): The medial clavicle ends should sit an equal distance from the spinous processes, so unequal distances show rotation. Rotation alters the apparent heart and mediastinal outline, which matters for this request. Depth of breath is judged by the ribs, not the clavicles. Scapular position affects whether the scapulae overlie the lung fields rather than clavicle symmetry.
  99. The correct answer is B (Fluid layering behind the lung on a supine film, giving a general increase in density): On a supine film fluid layers out behind the lung, so an effusion appears as a hazy increase in density across the hemithorax with vessels still seen, not a clean meniscus. The symmetrical clavicles argue against rotation. Consolidation would be patchy or lobar with air bronchograms. A pneumothorax would look darker with absent markings, not hazier.
  100. The correct answer is D (The heart sits further from the receptor on an AP supine film, so it appears magnified): In an AP projection the heart lies further from the receptor than in a PA, so it is magnified and looks larger. That is a projection effect, not necessarily a change in the patient. Bedside films usually use a shorter distance, not a longer one. Shallow inspiration on the earlier film is a guess unsupported by the stem.
  101. The correct answer is A (Reposition so the upper edge of the receptor sits above the diaphragm and repeat): For acute cases the upper edge of the receptor must be above the diaphragm so free gas is not cut off. This image does not show that anatomy, so it cannot answer the question and a repeat is justified. An erect film may be added later but does not excuse an incomplete supine film. Lowering the receptor makes the cut off worse.
  102. The correct answer is A (Take the AP pelvis with the legs as they lie, without rotating the injured leg): The routine internal rotation is meant for a normal patient. With trauma and an externally rotated, shortened leg a fracture is likely, so the film is taken as the patient lies. Forcing rotation can displace the fracture and cause pain. A frog leg view needs hip abduction, which is unsuitable here.
  103. The correct answer is B (Lift the uninjured leg clear and use a horizontal beam across the table): A cross table lateral leaves the injured leg where it lies. The uninjured leg is lifted out of the way, the receptor is placed against the outer side of the hip, and the beam runs horizontally. A frog leg lateral needs the patient to abduct the hip and is unsuitable when a fracture is possible. Rolling onto the injured side or rotating the leg would move the fracture.
  104. The correct answer is A (Take two projections at right angles to each other): A fracture or foreign body lying along the beam can vanish on one view, so a second projection at 90 degrees shows it from another plane. A second view taken only after an abnormal first view fails because the first view can look normal when it hides the problem. Two near identical AP views do not change the plane enough. Careful centring improves the view but cannot show what lies in the line of the beam.
  105. The correct answer is B (Recall the patient and repeat with a correct side marker inside the collimated field): A wrong side on an image is a patient safety matter because surgery can be planned from it. Here the side cannot be established with certainty from the image and the record, so the exposure is repeated with a marker in the beam. Guessing or digitally adding a marker you cannot be sure of is unsafe. The patient's recollection is not a reliable record.
  106. The correct answer is C (It is the original record of side in the beam, and surgery may rely on it): The marker in the beam is the original record of side made at the time of exposure, and a side error can contribute to wrong site surgery. An audit use for markers is a side benefit and not the reason for placing one before exposure. Patient identity is protected by separate checks and not by the side marker.
