Treating the marker as a time saver misses its safety role.
  107. The correct answer is C (A scaphoid series using ulnar deviation to separate and show the bone): The scaphoid is notorious for fractures that do not show on the first films. A scaphoid series uses ulnar deviation to separate the bone and show it along its length. Repeating the same views at a higher exposure does not change the angle of view. A forearm or hand series images the wrong region.
  108. The correct answer is C (A joint effusion, which may come from an occult fracture): A raised anterior fat pad shaped like a sail, or any visible posterior fat pad, suggests a joint effusion. In children this often comes from an occult fracture that the films do not yet show. It is not a normal variant. A dislocated radial head would show as malalignment, which the films do not show.
  109. The correct answer is D (A Y view or an axial view to show how the head sits on the glenoid): An AP view alone can miss a posterior dislocation because the head still overlaps the glenoid. A second view in another plane, a Y view or axial, shows whether the head is truly seated. The patient cannot rotate the arm, so another AP with rotation is not possible. A humerus lateral and a chest film do not show the joint relationship.
  110. The correct answer is D (Rotate the leg inward about 15° to 20° so the joint space is open): The mortise view needs the leg internally rotated about 15° to 20° so the joint space is open, which is what the request asks for. Outward rotation would not open the mortise. Angling the beam does not replace the rotation. A flexed knee with the foot flat gives a different projection altogether.
  111. The correct answer is B (A lateral taken with a horizontal beam while he lies supine): After an intra articular fracture fat and blood layer out in the joint, and this shows as a lipohaemarthrosis only on a horizontal beam lateral. He cannot bend the knee, so the skyline and fully flexed options are not possible. A weight bearing film is unsafe and does not show the layering. A vertical beam cannot show a fat fluid level.
  112. The correct answer is A (Both the wrist and elbow joints, on at least one view): For long bones the joint above and the joint below should be on at least one view. A fracture of one forearm bone often comes with a dislocation at the other end of the pair, so both joints matter. Collimating tightly to the fracture risks missing that. Leaving a joint out to save dose is not justified here.
  113. The correct answer is A (Dense metal creates artefacts that can hide anatomy on the panoramic image): Dense metal in the field produces artefacts that can obscure the jaws, teeth or joints on the panoramic image, so jewellery is removed during patient preparation. Hygiene is a real concern in a clinic, but it is not why metal is removed before this scan. Metal does not alter the exposure index or the thyroid dose in the way suggested. Panoramic images do not depend on an automatic exposure reading being thrown off by earrings.
  114. The correct answer is B (Give him a lead apron and place him outside the primary beam): When immobilisation devices fail, a carer who is not pregnant may hold the child, wearing a lead apron and kept outside the primary beam. An apron alone does not make it safe to stand in the beam, even at its edge. A short exposure is not a reason to skip protection. A pregnant staff member should not be asked to take on this exposure.
  115. The correct answer is D (Ask the non pregnant nurse to hold the child, wearing a lead apron): A person who holds a child should not be pregnant, and should wear a lead apron and stay out of the primary beam. The nurse meets all of these. A pregnant aunt is excluded as a helper whatever protection is offered. Exposing without holding risks a blurred image and a repeat, which doubles the child's dose.
  116. The correct answer is C (Follow local policy and report through the child protection pathway, without questioning the carer): Suspected non accidental injury must be reported through the correct pathway under local policy and legal obligations. The radiographer does not question the child or the carer. A skeletal survey is a defined protocol set up on request, not an individual decision. Waiting for the report delays protection of the child.
  117. The correct answer is A (A buckle or torus fracture, with a bulge of the cortex): A torus or buckle fracture in a child shows as a bulge in the cortex rather than a clean break. A greenstick fracture breaks one cortex and bends the other, which is not seen here. Comminuted needs more than two fragments, and an avulsion involves a fragment pulled off by a tendon or ligament.
  118. The correct answer is D (Comminuted, because the bone has broken into more than two fragments): A fracture with more than two fragments is called comminuted, and the three pieces on this femur match that definition. Spiral and oblique describe a single fracture line with a particular direction. Avulsion describes a fragment pulled off by a tendon or ligament, which does not fit a high speed fall with a shattered shaft. The number of fragments decides the term.
  119. The correct answer is A (Leave the collar and board in place until the doctor in charge removes them): Collars, splints and spinal boards stay on until the doctor in charge removes them. Image quality does not outrank spinal safety. Loosening, briefly removing or rolling the patient to remove the collar all risk movement of an unstable spine. A cross table beam can be adjusted without touching the collar.
  120. The correct answer is B (Warn all staff, wear a lead apron, and stand clear of the primary beam): In theatre the practitioner keeps the sterile field intact, wears a lead apron, stands as far from the patient as the cable allows and out of the primary beam, and warns all staff before the exposure. Moving the drape breaks sterility. Standing behind the receptor places the practitioner in the primary beam. Standing next to the surgeon increases scatter exposure.
  121. The correct answer is B (The patient was overexposed and received extra dose, even though the image looks acceptable): Digital systems can hide a poor exposure by adjusting brightness, so a clean looking image can still be overexposed. A very high exposure index means overexposure, which adds dose even if the image looks fine. A very low index would give a noisy, grainy image instead. The finding should prompt a review of the exposure factors.
  122. The correct answer is C (Too little radiation reached the receptor, giving noise that can hide detail): A very low exposure index gives a noisy, grainy image because too little radiation reached the receptor. That noise can hide fine detail relevant to a fracture. Overexposure would give a high index. Motion blur would soften edges, and the cortex is still seen, and a grid artefact would show line patterns.
  123. The correct answer is C (Repeat with a shorter exposure time, because the blur hides the suspected finding): A repeat is justified when motion blur hides detail needed to answer the clinical question, and here a subtle buckle fracture could be missed. A shorter exposure time reduces blur. Both views are blurred, so repeating only one is not enough, and using the same time risks the same result. Adding a note does not fix an unreadable image.
  124. The correct answer is D (Accept the image, because it answers the clinical question and avoids extra dose): The question to ask is not whether the image looks perfect but whether it can answer the clinical question and whether a repeat is justified. All the needed anatomy is shown with clear joint spaces. Every repeat gives the patient more radiation, so repeating for symmetry is not justified. A second view might still be requested separately if the clinical question changed.
  125. The correct answer is C (Alert the clinical team immediately and do not wait for a formal report): This appearance shows a tension pneumothorax with mediastinal shift, which is a clinical emergency. The practitioner alerts the team at once and does not wait for the report. A repeat erect film would delay care and is not feasible in this patient. Writing on the request form is too slow for an immediately life threatening finding.
  126. The correct answer is D (In the trachea and above the level of the carina): An endotracheal tube tip should lie above the carina, within the trachea. A tip at or below the carina, or in a main bronchus, risks ventilating only one lung. The radiographer should communicate a low tip promptly. Both bronchus options describe malposition.
  127. The correct answer is B (Tell the nurse the tube is not clearly in the stomach, so feeding must wait): A nasogastric tube must be below the diaphragm and clearly in the stomach before feeding. This tip is above the diaphragm, so feeding into it risks aspiration into the lungs or oesophagus. The finding should be communicated at once. Advancing the tube is a nursing decision, and feeding must wait for a repeat image.
  128. The correct answer is B (Water soluble iodinated contrast, because barium causes severe inflammation if it leaks): Barium is not used when a perforation or leak is suspected because it causes severe inflammation in the peritoneum or mediastinum. A water soluble iodinated contrast is used instead. Diluting barium does not remove the risk. Gas alone would not show a leak with the detail needed.
  129. The correct answer is A (The pectoral muscle and inframammary fold are included and the nipple is in profile): An adequate oblique view includes the pectoral muscle and the inframammary fold, with the nipple in profile and no skin folds. Excluding the pectoral muscle loses posterior tissue, and cutting off the fold loses the lower breast. Having the nipple in profile is only one criterion, so skin folds over the lower breast still make the image inadequate.
  130. The correct answer is A (Release the compression, discuss her concern, and proceed only if she agrees): Compression is held only as long as needed, and the patient is told she can ask to stop. She has asked to stop, so compression is released and her concern discussed before any further decision. Pressing on overrides her consent. Reducing compression without changing the exposure would also change image quality and dose.
  131. The correct answer is C (Prepare a sterile trolley and check consent, allergy history and pregnancy status): Arthrography injects contrast into a joint under fluoroscopy using aseptic technique, so the trolley is sterile. The practitioner also checks consent, allergy history and pregnancy status. A clean trolley risks joint infection, and a shortened check list omits the allergy history. Oral contrast has no role in a shoulder arthrogram.
  132. The correct answer is D (Use pulsed mode, collimate tightly, and keep the detector close to the patient): In fluoroscopy dose is kept low through pulsed mode, collimation and keeping the image intensifier or detector close to the patient. Each wrong choice drops one of these steps. Continuous mode adds dose, and an open field irradiates more tissue. A high frame rate also raises dose without necessarily improving the swallow assessment.
  133. The correct answer is B (A thin dark crescent of gas beneath the diaphragm on the erect film): Free gas rises to the highest point, so on an erect film it collects under the diaphragm as a thin lucent crescent. A meniscus with a blunted angle is a pleural effusion. Dilated central loops with air fluid levels suggest small bowel obstruction, and a lung edge without markings is a pneumothorax.
  134. The correct answer is C (Small bowel obstruction, shown by dilated central loops with air fluid levels): Small bowel obstruction shows dilated central loops with air fluid levels, and the collapsed peripheral colon supports that. Large bowel obstruction would show a dilated colon around the edge of the abdomen. Free gas appears as a crescent beneath the diaphragm. Air fluid levels with dilated loops are not a normal finding.
  135. The correct answer is C (Restore the full uncropped image and repeat only if the angles were never imaged): Post processing such as cropping, edge enhancement or inversion can hide a finding, so the image is checked against the clinical question. The costophrenic angles matter for an effusion, so the original full image is restored first. A repeat is justified only if the angles were never imaged. Inversion cannot recover anatomy that was cropped away.
  136. The correct answer is D (Alert the trauma team now, because a widened mediastinum may mean aortic injury): A widened mediastinum in trauma is an urgent sign of a possible aortic injury and should be communicated quickly. It is true that supine films can exaggerate width, but the mechanism makes the risk too serious to defer. Sitting a trauma patient up is unsafe. A decubitus view looks for fluid or air and does not address the aorta.
  137. The correct answer is B (The whole area of interest plus a small margin, and nothing more): Collimation means narrowing the beam to the area of interest. The light field should include all the anatomy needed plus a small margin, and nothing more. Including the forearm adds dose. A field with no margin risks cut off, and filling the receptor irradiates unneeded tissue.
  138. The correct answer is A (An open mouth view showing the atlas and axis, taken through the mouth): The open mouth view is the cervical spine addition for C1 and C2, the atlas and axis. The swimmer's view targets the lower region, flexion and extension views are used only when the clinical question needs them, and obliques show the foramina. Only the open mouth view addresses the stated suspicion.
  139. The correct answer is D (A pleural effusion of fluid collected at the base of the lung): A blunted costophrenic angle and a meniscus shaped upper edge are the signs of a pleural effusion on an erect film. Consolidation would appear patchy or lobar with air bronchograms. A pneumothorax shows a lung edge with no markings beyond it, and free gas appears as a crescent beneath the diaphragm.
  140. The correct answer is B (A visible edge of the lung with no lung markings beyond it): A pneumothorax shows a visible lung edge with no lung markings beyond it, because air has collected between the lung and the chest wall. Patchy white areas with air bronchograms are consolidation. A blunted angle with a curved upper edge is an effusion. A widened mediastinum is an urgent sign of possible aortic injury in trauma.
  141. The correct answer is A (A bitewing film showing the crowns of upper and lower teeth together): In the bitewing view both crowns of the upper and lower teeth show on the same film. A periapical view shows the whole tooth including the root tip. An occlusal view shows the floor of the mouth, and an orthopantomogram is a panoramic image of both jaws and the joints.
  142. The correct answer is D (Underpenetration, so the area behind the heart is not shown and a repeat is justified): On a good chest film the vertebral bodies can just be seen through the heart shadow. When they cannot be seen at all, the beam has not penetrated enough, so the area behind the heart is not shown and a repeat with more penetration is justified. Inspiration and rotation are described as satisfactory, and overpenetration would show the vertebrae too clearly.
  143. The correct answer is B (Rotate the tube so the cathode lies over the feet, repeating with unchanged factors.): The beam is weaker on the anode side, so the thicker lower thoracic spine needs the cathode end over it, and here the anode sat over the feet. Reversing the tube direction fixes the cause without changing dose. Raising mAs darkens the whole image and doubles dose without correcting the gradient. A shorter SID makes the heel effect stronger, and a grid addresses scatter, which is not the problem here.
  144. The correct answer is A (6 mAs, half the original, since higher kVp roughly doubles detector exposure.): 75 × 1.15 = 86.25, so the kVp rise is one full 15 percent step, which roughly doubles detector exposure. Halving 12 mAs gives 6 mAs and the signal stays about the same. Doubling would overexpose the detector and raise dose. A quarter cut is not part of the rule, and leaving mAs alone ignores the large change in detector exposure.
  145. The correct answer is C (32 mAs, since lowering kVp by 15 percent roughly halves detector exposure.): 80 × 0.85 = 68, one full 15 percent step down, which roughly halves detector exposure. To restore the signal the mAs must double: 16 × 2 = 32 mAs. Halving again would leave the image badly underexposed and noisy. A quarter increase is too small, and keeping 16 mAs ignores the loss of exposure, giving a grainy image.
  146. The correct answer is C (4.4 mAs, worked out as 10 mAs × (120 ÷ 180)²): Detector signal depends on mAs divided by distance squared, so the new mAs is 10 × (120 ÷ 180)² = 10 × 0.444 = 4.4 mAs. The shorter distance gives a stronger beam, so mAs must fall by more than the simple ratio. Using the unsquared ratio gives 6.7 mAs and leaves the signal too high. Inverting the ratio, squared or not, raises mAs to 15 or 22.5 and would overexpose the detector.
  147. The correct answer is A (20 mAs, worked out as 4 mAs multiplied by a factor of 5): A 12:1 grid has a conversion factor of about 5, so the new exposure is 4 mAs × 5 = 20 mAs. Each of the other values is correct arithmetic using the factor that belongs to a different grid ratio, which is why they look reasonable at a glance. Using a lower factor would leave the detector underexposed with the 12:1 grid, and a higher factor would overexpose it and add unnecessary dose. Matching the factor to the grid ratio actually fitted is the step that matters.
  148. The correct answer is C (30 mAs, dividing by the old factor of 4, then multiplying by 6.): Dividing 20 mAs by the old factor of 4 gives the non-grid value of 5 mAs. Multiplying by the new factor of 6 gives 30 mAs. The doubled ratio does not double the factor, and keeping 20 mAs would leave the detector under exposed. The 24 mAs figure comes from using 5 as the old factor, which is wrong for an 8:1 grid.
  149. The correct answer is D (+3.0, meaning the detector received about double the target exposure.): EI ÷ EIT = 800 ÷ 400 = 2, and log10(2) = 0.301, so DI = 10 × 0.301 = +3.0. A DI of +3 means about double the target. Leaving out the factor of 10 gives 0.3 and hides a clear overexposure. The negative value would suggest under exposure, which the data do not support.
  150. The correct answer is A (40 mm, worked out as 46 mm ÷ (110 ÷ 95)): Magnification equals SID ÷ SOD, which is 110 ÷ 95 = 1.16. The true length is the image size divided by this factor, so 46 ÷ 1.16 = 39.7 mm, about 40 mm. Multiplying by 1.16 gives 53 mm and makes the implant look even larger than the image. Using an object distance that does not match the stem changes the factor and gives 42 mm or 33 mm.
  151. The correct answer is A (0.15 mm, worked out as 0.6 × 20 ÷ 80): The source to object distance is 100 − 20 = 80 cm. Geometric unsharpness is the focal spot size × OID ÷ SOD, which is 0.6 × 20 ÷ 80 = 0.15 mm. Using the full SID in the denominator understates the blur to 0.12 mm. Swapping the distances produces values that are too large, such as 0.75 mm or 2.4 mm.
  152. The correct answer is B (0.30 Gy·cm², worked out as 0.5 mGy × 600 cm², divided by 1000): The field area is 20 × 30 = 600 cm². The dose area product is 0.5 mGy × 600 cm² = 300 mGy·cm², and dividing by 1000 gives 0.30 Gy·cm². Adding the sides gives a length rather than an area, which leads to 0.025. A wrong power of ten in the conversion gives 0.03. Skipping the conversion leaves 300, which is a number of milligray rather than gray and overstates the logged dose a thousandfold.
  153. The correct answer is C (5 µSv per hour, worked out as 80 ÷ 4²): The distance rises four times, so the rate falls by 4² = 16 and 80 ÷ 16 = 5 µSv per hour. Dividing by 4 treats the fall as proportional to distance rather than its square. Dividing by 2 reflects a single doubling and ignores the second. Dividing by 8 mixes up the exponent and is not the law for a point source.
  154. The correct answer is D (0.4 mm, twice the pixel pitch, the theoretical limit of the detector.): The Nyquist limit says the smallest detail that can be shown is twice the pixel size: 2 × 0.2 = 0.4 mm. A detail needs at least two samples, so one pixel is not enough, and detail below the pixel pitch cannot be recorded. Four times the pitch is more than the theoretical limit.
  155. The correct answer is D (Select the side chambers or use manual factors, then repeat.): The prosthesis blocks the beam over the centre chamber, so the chamber collects signal slowly and the exposure runs long. Choosing the side chambers or manual factors removes the cause. A lower density setting still leaves the chamber under the metal, and a higher kVp or no grid do not solve it, so another painful repeat could follow.
  156. The correct answer is B (Off-focus cutoff, as the SID lies outside the grid's focused range.): Density that is normal in the centre and falls toward both edges at an SID outside the focused range is the pattern of off-focus cutoff. The heel effect gives a gradient from one side only, not matching loss at both edges. A grid ratio problem and shutter closure do not follow from an SID outside the focused range. Moving the SID into the stated range is the correction.
  157. The correct answer is D (Refit the grid with the tube side facing the tube, then repeat.): Very low density at both edges with the tube side label facing the detector shows the grid was fitted upside down, so refitting it the right way round removes the cause. A different grid would be unnecessary and could fail in the same way. Removing the grid and raising mAs adds dose and changes image quality without fixing the fitting error. Raising kVp does not correct a geometric cutoff.
  158. The correct answer is B (Record the finding, check technique and AEC settings, and report it through the local quality process.): A high index means the patient received more radiation than needed even when the image looks fine, and the technique may be copied by others. The correct response is to record it, check the settings and report it. Repeating adds more dose with no benefit, and windowing changes display only, not the exposure recorded.
  159. The correct answer is C (Incomplete plate erasure, so erase the plate again first.): A faint outline of a previous patient is ghosting, which means the plate was not fully erased. Erasing again solves it. Dead pixels belong to DR detectors and give fixed dots, moiré gives a regular wavy pattern, and a scratch would show in the same place on every image from that plate, not a past patient's anatomy.
  160. The correct answer is A (Inspect and clean the plate, and take it out of use if the line stays.): The same mark on every image from one plate points to dust or a scratch on that plate. Cleaning it or removing it from use is the fix. Extra erasing cannot remove physical damage, reader calibration would affect all plates, and a grid frequency problem would not follow one cassette.
  161. The correct answer is C (Report it for recalibration or service as dead pixels.): A fixed dot that stays in the same place and persists on a flat-field test is a dead pixel. It needs recalibration or service. Erasing is a CR step, a grid change addresses moiré, and more mAs adds dose without removing a dead pixel.
  162. The correct answer is D (Remove the grid and use a paediatric protocol with reduced mAs.): A thin paediatric part produces little scatter, so a grid raises dose for no gain. Removing it and following a size-based protocol lowers dose. Lowering kVp needs more mAs, a higher ratio grid needs even more exposure, and a shorter SID makes the beam more intense.
  163. The correct answer is B (Off-level cutoff, from a tilt across the strips.): A tilt across the strips lightens the whole image, which is off-level cutoff. Off-focus affects the edges, off-centre arises from the central ray missing the grid centre, and upside-down gives a very low density at both edges. The stem states the tilt, which decides it.
  164. The correct answer is A (Accept the diagnostic image, record the index and review the technique chart against the target.): Grain is quantum mottle from fewer photons, and a repeat should only follow if it makes the diagnosis unsafe. Here the key findings are clear, so the image is accepted and the technique reviewed. Repeating adds dose, processing does not change the index, and a higher kVp lowers contrast rather than curing noise.
  165. The correct answer is C (Reprocess the stored data once with the correct examination selected.): The wrong body part menu makes the processing and index inaccurate, and the cause is the menu selection. Fixing it and reprocessing once avoids extra dose. A repeat adds dose with the same risk, windowing hides the cause, and repeated reprocessing is the habit the process should avoid.
  166. The correct answer is B (Stop and confirm identity and side with the patient and referrer.): A mismatched birth date and a conflicting side are two warning signs, so exposure must wait until three identifiers and the side are confirmed with the right people. Exposing first creates an unjustified exposure and a wrong record. One matching identifier is not enough.
  167. The correct answer is A (Call it dose creep, audit the indices and review technique charts.): Rising indices with better-looking images is the pattern of dose creep, since digital overexposure does not look too dark. Auditing indices and technique charts and teaching staff fixes the cause. Moving the target hides it, and halving mAs in all rooms with no audit risks poor images.
  168. The correct answer is D (Move the tube away, bring the receptor close and stop using magnification.): Skin dose falls when the tube is far from the patient and the receptor is close, and magnification modes raise dose rate. Continuous fluoroscopy raises dose, a more distant receptor raises the patient's dose, and a higher frame rate means more pulses per second, not less dose.
  169. The correct answer is C (Remove it from service and inform the radiation safety officer.): A fault that can send the beam to the wrong place affects safety, so the unit is taken out of service and the radiation safety officer is told. Wider collimation adds dose, limited use still risks exposures, and a self-repair is not the radiographer's role.
  170. The correct answer is B (Use paediatric AEC settings or manual factors chosen for the child's size.): Adult AEC settings can overexpose a small child, so paediatric settings or size-based manual factors are used. A grid adds dose for a thin chest, lower kVp pushes mAs up, and a shorter SID raises beam intensity.
  171. The correct answer is D (Collimate tightly to the lumbar region to cut scatter before changing factors.): Scatter adds fog and lowers contrast, and tight collimation is the first and cheapest tool. A higher kVp produces more scatter, more mAs raises dose without fixing fog, and a lower ratio grid removes less scatter.
  172. The correct answer is D (A shorter time with a higher mA, keeping the mAs similar.): Motion unsharpness is cured by a short exposure time, so the mA is raised to keep the signal. A longer time worsens motion, a smaller focal spot affects penumbra and limits the mA, and a longer SID does not stop the child moving.
  173. The correct answer is A (Leave filtration in place and report low output for service, since removal raises skin dose.): Filtration removes low energy photons that add skin dose and no useful image, so it stays in place and low output is reported for service. Removing it for adults or for thick parts still raises skin dose for those patients. Lowering kVp afterwards does not restore the filtering effect and makes penetration worse. Low output is an equipment fault, not a reason to remove a protective component.
  174. The correct answer is A (Repeat with adequate mAs, because windowing cannot add information the detector never recorded.): Quantum mottle comes from too few photons reaching the detector, and windowing cannot repair underexposure. Because the image is non-diagnostic, a repeat with enough mAs is justified. Reprocessing and smoothing only hide the problem.
  175. The correct answer is B (Train staff on positioning, then recheck the reject rate.): Reject analysis points to the main cause, and here positioning accounts for most repeats, so training on positioning followed by a recheck is the response that addresses it. Revising exposure charts or raising mAs deals with the small share caused by exposure errors and can add dose. Waiting a year keeps unnecessary repeats going in the meantime.
  176. The correct answer is D (Investigate technique and equipment and optimise, since the reference level is a benchmark for review.): Diagnostic reference levels are benchmarks for typical doses on standard patients, not limits. A typical dose above one calls for investigation and optimisation under ALARA. Stopping examinations is unnecessary, moving the benchmark hides the problem, and non-diagnostic images are poor practice.
  177. The correct answer is C (Ask the father to help while wearing a lead apron, with the mother outside.): A carer who is not pregnant may help while wearing a lead apron. The father fits that role, so the pregnant mother does not need to be exposed. Staff should not routinely hold patients, and the carer still wears an apron.
  178. The correct answer is B (Kilovoltage peak, which sets photon energy and so penetration and contrast.): Kilovoltage peak sets the energy of the photons, which controls penetration and contrast in the pelvis. Milliampere-seconds changes only how many photons are made. Focal spot size affects sharpness and tube loading, and grid ratio changes scatter reaching the receptor, so neither sets photon energy.
  179. The correct answer is C (Dose doubles, because dose is directly proportional to the mAs.): With every other factor unchanged, mAs is proportional to the number of photons, so doubling it doubles the dose. The exposure index only reports the detector signal and does not lower what the patient received. The inverse square law concerns distance, and the 15 percent figure relates to kVp changes, so neither applies to a change in mAs.
  180. The correct answer is D (Gy·cm², the dose in air multiplied by the area of the beam.): Dose area product is dose in air multiplied by beam area, measured in Gy·cm². Effective dose is in mSv, entrance skin dose is the dose at the skin entry point, and a plain gray figure has no area term.
  181. The correct answer is C (The amount of radiation reaching the detector, compared with a target for that examination): The exposure index indicates how much radiation reached the detector and is compared with a target for the examination. Dose area product and skin dose describe the patient, and the index is not either of them. The deviation index is a related figure that expresses the difference from the target, but it is not the index itself. A reading of 520 is not 520 units of patient dose.
  182. The correct answer is A (A laser scans the plate and releases light in proportion to the stored energy.): CR uses a photostimulable phosphor plate read by a laser, which releases light in proportion to stored energy. Photodiodes and scintillators describe indirect DR and photoconductors describe direct DR.
  183. The correct answer is A (A direct digital radiography panel with a photoconductor layer.): A photoconductor such as amorphous selenium converts x-rays directly into charge, which defines direct DR. Indirect DR uses a scintillator to produce light first, CR uses a plate and a laser, and film-screen is not digital.
  184. The correct answer is B (The height of the lead strips divided by the gap between them.): Grid ratio is strip height divided by the gap between strips. A higher ratio removes more scatter but needs more exposure and more accurate positioning. Strips per centimetre is the grid frequency.
  185. The correct answer is A (A smaller focal spot, a longer SID and the smallest OID.): Penumbra = focal spot × OID ÷ SOD, so it falls with a smaller spot, a longer SID and a smaller OID. Each wrong combination has at least one change that raises blur.
  186. The correct answer is D (Elongation, from poor tube, part and detector alignment.): Elongation is a shape distortion that makes the bone look longer, and it arises from poor alignment of tube, part and detector, as with a steep tube angle. Foreshortening makes a part look shorter, and magnification enlarges all dimensions evenly. Movement blur smears edges but does not lengthen the bone.
  187. The correct answer is C (The standard file format and how imaging devices share information, including the header data.): DICOM defines the file format and the way imaging devices share information, and its header holds the patient name, identifier, date of birth and exposure data. Hospital messaging standards and billing codes are separate systems that also move patient details but do not define the image file. Compression is only one small part of the standard.
  188. The correct answer is D (Effective dose, which estimates the risk to the whole body.): Effective dose in mSv estimates whole-body risk. Entrance skin dose is local, DAP is in Gy·cm², and a diagnostic reference level is a benchmark, not a measured patient dose.
  189. The correct answer is B (The line-focus principle, giving a small effective spot from a larger real one.): The line-focus principle uses an angled target so the real focal spot is large enough to spread the heat, while the effective spot seen from the detector stays small for sharpness. The heel effect is a result of the same angle but describes intensity, not spot size. Space charge and off-focus radiation are tube effects that do not explain why the target is angled.
  190. The correct answer is B (Moiré, from grid frequency and detector sampling mismatch.): A regular wavy pattern that appears only with the grid is a moiré pattern from grid frequency and detector sampling mismatch. Erasure is a CR step, the dead pixel tests were normal, and off-focus cutoff lowers density at the edges.
  191. The correct answer is C (Dose area product, measured in gray square centimetres (Gy·cm²).): Dose area product multiplies dose in air by beam area, so it includes field size and relates to total energy. It is the usual quantity for reference levels. The other three do not meet all those features.
  192. The correct answer is A (A higher kVp, a thicker body part and a larger field size.): Scatter grows with higher kVp, greater tissue volume from a thicker part, and a larger irradiated field, so combining all three gives the most fog. A higher grid ratio is tempting because grids are linked to scatter, but it removes scatter before the detector. Lower kVp is a common slip, since absorption rises but the scatter fraction falls. A longer air gap lets scattered photons miss the receptor.
  193. The correct answer is A (Water set at 0 HU and air at about -1000 HU): The Hounsfield scale is fixed by water at 0 HU and air at about -1000 HU, with every other tissue placed relative to them. Muscle sits a little above water but is a tissue measured on the scale, not an anchor. Bone is very high on the scale, but it is not a reference, and the zero point belongs to water, not to fat.
  194. The correct answer is B (800 mAs, worked out as 200 mAs multiplied by 4): Image noise falls with the square root of mAs, so halving noise needs the mAs multiplied by 4: 200 × 4 = 800 mAs. Doubling to 400 mAs reduces noise only to about 71 percent of its value, which is the tempting linear assumption. A rise to 300 mAs changes noise only slightly. Going to 1600 mAs would cut noise well below the target and quadruple the dose again for no benefit.
  195. The correct answer is B (540 mGy cm, worked out as 12 mGy × 45 cm): Dose length product is CTDIvol multiplied by the scanned length, so 12 mGy × 45 cm = 540 mGy cm. A quick check is 12 × 40 + 12 × 5 = 480 + 60 = 540. Entering the length in millimetres makes the figure ten times too large for a result quoted in mGy cm. Rounding the length to 40 cm understates the irradiated range, and halving the length gives 270, which would misstate the examination dose in the audit register.
  196. The correct answer is C (Limit the range to diaphragm through pelvis, since extra length adds DLP without benefit): CTDIvol describes dose per slice volume, but DLP rises with scan length, so extra range at the same CTDIvol means more total radiation. The clinical question concerns the abdomen and pelvis, so range to the knees adds dose without benefit. Cropping afterwards does not undo exposure. Cutting the current degrades the diagnostic images instead of fixing the cause.
  197. The correct answer is D (Only the range of HU values displayed from black to white): Window width sets how many HU values are spread between black and white, so it only alters the display. Window level, not width, moves the centre of the grey scale. Noise comes from acquisition and reconstruction choices such as slice thickness, and the raw data are untouched by windowing again. Dose and pitch are fixed at the time of the scan.
  198. The correct answer is A (A wide window, spreading a large range of HU across the greys): Lung and bone sit at opposite ends of the HU scale, so a wide window is needed to show both on one image. Each narrow window is correct for its own tissue but loses the other: a soft tissue setting greys out lung and bone, a bone setting leaves lung black, and a lung setting makes ribs a featureless white. Choosing a narrow window trades coverage for contrast.
  199. The correct answer is D (Select the size-based paediatric protocol so tube current is matched to the child): A child is smaller and more radiosensitive, so the scanner needs a protocol built around body size, with exposure control adjusting tube current inside it. Shortening an adult scan only trims coverage and does not change the dose per slice. Capping the current on an adult protocol is a partial fix that still ignores the child's dimensions, and a low-dose adult protocol is tuned for adult anatomy and noise targets.
  200. The correct answer is D (Metal artefact from the prostheses, which strongly attenuate and harden the beam): Hip prostheses are dense metal that strongly weaken and harden the beam, producing bright and dark streaks around them. Motion artefact appears as blur, and the patient held still. Contrast phase changes how structures enhance, not streaking. Thick slices blur detail but do not produce streaks radiating from metal.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Faster coverage and lower dose, with some loss of fine detail): Pitch is table travel per rotation divided by beam width. A higher pitch covers the body faster and, at fixed tube current, delivers less dose, but detail can blur. It does not sharpen images, slow the scan, or work through slice thickness.
  2. The correct answer is C (Acquire a delayed phase minutes later, once contrast has collected in the urinary tract): The delayed phase, minutes after injection, shows contrast collecting in the urinary tract. The portal venous phase at about 60 to 70 seconds is the routine abdominal phase and does not fill the ureters. An arterial phase is earlier still. More mAs cannot fix a timing problem and only adds dose.
  3. The correct answer is A (A typical dose value used to review whether practice is out of line): A diagnostic reference level is built from typical doses for common examinations and is used to flag practice that may need review. When a department's typical values sit well above it, the department investigates and optimises. It is not a staff limit, which belongs to occupational exposure rules, and it is not a legal cap on one patient. Nor is it a dose each patient must receive, since dose is tailored to the clinical question and the patient.
  4. The correct answer is A (Investigate protocols and settings to find the cause, then optimise the examination): When typical values regularly sit well above the DRL, the department investigates and optimises. A DRL is not a legal limit for one patient, so stopping all head CT or reporting every exceedance as an incident misuses it. Doing nothing ignores its purpose, which is to prompt review.
  5. The correct answer is B (Short TR and TE, with fat bright and fluid dark): T1 weighting uses short TR and short TE, which makes fat bright and simple fluid such as CSF dark. It is good for anatomy and gadolinium enhancement. The long TR and long TE option describes T2 weighting. The mixed-timing options are not standard weightings and have the appearances reversed.
  6. The correct answer is C (Diffusion weighted imaging, because restricted water movement shows early infarction): Diffusion weighted imaging detects restricted water movement within minutes of vessel occlusion, so it is the sequence to prioritise when scanner time is short. FLAIR and T2 changes appear later, and FLAIR is often normal in the first hours. T1 with gadolinium shows enhancement that develops days after infarction, so it cannot answer the urgent question.
  7. The correct answer is D (FLAIR, a T2-type sequence in which the bright CSF signal is suppressed): FLAIR keeps T2 sensitivity but suppresses CSF, so periventricular lesions such as plaques are easier to see. On standard T2 the bright CSF can mask them. T1 shows anatomy and enhancement, and diffusion weighted imaging is the sequence for early stroke.
  8. The correct answer is D (Stop him at the door, since the magnet is always on and steel items can fly): The static magnetic field of an MRI scanner is on all the time, whether or not a scan is running. A steel cylinder or trolley can be pulled towards the bore at speed, injuring people and damaging the magnet. Standby mode stops imaging but does not remove the field. Keeping the cylinder upright or removing a watch and keys does not make a ferromagnetic item safe.
  9. The correct answer is B (Zone III, restricted so that only screened staff and patients may enter): Zone III is the restricted area where only screened people may enter, because uncontrolled access there could lead straight into the magnet room. Zone II is where screening happens, so it is tempting, but it does not restrict entry to screened people only. Zone I is open to the public, and Zone IV is the scanner room itself, always supervised by trained MRI staff.
  10. The correct answer is D (Stop, identify device and leads, confirm MR status, arrange cardiac support): A pacemaker can heat, move or reprogram itself in the field whatever body part is scanned. The radiographer must find the manufacturer and model, check the MR status of the whole system including leads, and arrange the cardiac support. Distance, SAR reduction or field strength alone do not make the scan safe.
  11. The correct answer is A (Hold the scan until records or imaging identify any implant, and ask the safety lead): The patient's memory alone is never enough. Use the implant card, records and imaging, and when in doubt do not scan and ask the MRI safety lead or radiologist. Lower SAR does not remove the pull or heating risk of an aneurysm clip, and consent does not replace screening.
  12. The correct answer is C (Hold the scan and arrange orbit imaging or radiologist advice to exclude metal): A possible metallic fragment in the eye can move or heat in the field whichever body part is scanned, and the eye is very vulnerable. The history needs to be checked with orbit imaging or radiologist advice before any scan proceeds. Time since injury and distance from the lumbar spine do not exclude retained metal. A lower field strength or a signed consent form does not remove the physical hazard.
  13. The correct answer is A (Move her to the 1.5 T scanner and follow the stated conditions exactly): MR Conditional means the implant is safe only when every stated condition is met, and field strength is one of them. Lowering SAR on the 3 T does not meet a condition written for 1.5 T. A radiologist's approval cannot replace the manufacturer's conditions, and the correct scanner with standard sequences still ignores the remaining conditions on the card. With a free 1.5 T slot, moving her and following the conditions closely is the practical answer.
  14. The correct answer is A (Stop the scan, pad between skin surfaces, straighten the cable and check the patient): Burns can occur when cables form loops or skin touches skin or the bore wall, and large patients raise SAR. A warm sensation is a warning, so the scan is stopped, the position corrected with padding and the cable straightened, and the patient is checked. Scanner limits do not remove this risk.
  15. The correct answer is B (Iodine allergy does not rule it out, but any gadolinium reaction must be recorded): Gadolinium is not an iodine contrast, so an iodine allergy does not rule it out. A previous reaction to a gadolinium agent must still be recorded and communicated, and kidney function is checked in patients at risk. Allergy history is therefore relevant, and kidney function is not ignored.
  16. The correct answer is C (Report the low eGFR to the radiologist, who will choose a stable macrocyclic agent): NSF has been linked to older linear agents in patients with severe kidney failure. Current practice uses stable macrocyclic agents and checks kidney function in patients at risk, with the decision made with the radiologist. Iodinated contrast is not a safer substitute for the kidneys, and injection rate does not remove the risk.
  17. The correct answer is D (Scan without gadolinium, since it is generally avoided in pregnancy unless essential): A clinically needed MRI in pregnancy can go ahead once the radiologist approves it, but gadolinium is generally avoided unless essential. Absence of ionising radiation does not cover the contrast, and halving the dose does not remove the concern. The scan proceeds without contrast unless the radiologist decides otherwise.
  18. The correct answer is C (Wraparound, where anatomy outside the field of view folds into the image): Wraparound happens when anatomy outside the field of view is folded back into the image. Motion causes ghosting, but the patient stayed still. Metal causes signal loss and distortion, but there are no implants. Beam hardening is a CT artefact.
  19. The correct answer is C (A high-frequency probe of about 10 to 18 MHz, trading penetration for detail): Higher frequency gives better detail but penetrates less, so a high-frequency probe suits shallow structures such as the thyroid, breast and tendons. A low-frequency probe is used for deep organs such as the abdomen. Detail does depend on frequency, so no single mid probe gives the same detail at every depth.
  20. The correct answer is B (Change to a lower-frequency probe of about 2 to 5 MHz to improve penetration): Higher frequency gives better detail but less penetration, so the 12 MHz probe cannot reach the deep liver in a large patient. A lower-frequency probe of about 2 to 5 MHz is used for deep organs such as the abdomen. Raising frequency makes penetration worse, and pressure or element count does not solve it.
  21. The correct answer is B (15 cm, worked out as 1540 × 97.5 × 10⁻⁶ m): The pulse travels to the reflector and back, so the one-way time is 195 ÷ 2 = 97.5 µs. Depth = 1540 × 97.5 × 10⁻⁶ = 0.150 m, which is 15 cm. Using the full time gives 30 cm and halving twice gives 7.5 cm. A power of ten slip gives 1.5 cm. The rule of 13 µs per centimetre agrees, since 195 ÷ 13 = 15.
  22. The correct answer is D (Gel removes air between probe and skin, because air reflects almost all of the sound): Air between the probe and skin reflects almost all of the sound, so little enters the body. Gel removes that air gap. It does not change crystal frequency, set the speed of sound, or abolish shadowing behind bone or gas.
  23. The correct answer is B (Steer or reposition so the beam angle to the vessel is 60° or less, then measure): The Doppler shift is largest when the beam is more parallel to the flow, and at 90° little or no shift is seen. For speed measurements the operator keeps the angle at 60° or less. Gain makes the trace brighter but does not make the shift real, and probe frequency does not fix the angle.
  24. The correct answer is A (Acoustic shadowing behind a strong reflector such as a gallstone): A dark area behind a strong reflector such as a gallstone or bone is acoustic shadowing. Posterior enhancement is a brighter area behind a fluid-filled structure. Reverberation shows repeated parallel lines, and aliasing is a Doppler colour wrap, not a greyscale band.
  25. The correct answer is D (Raise the velocity scale so the fastest flow can be shown without wrapping): Aliasing in Doppler occurs because the flow is faster than the chosen scale can measure, so the colour wraps. Raising the scale lets the jet be displayed without wrapping. Lowering it makes aliasing worse, and a 90° beam would remove the shift instead of fixing the scale.
  26. The correct answer is C (Decline a non-medical scan, explaining foetal exposure should be avoided without a reason): Ultrasound follows the ALARA principle. Heating and cavitation are the concern, and Doppler delivers more energy than greyscale. Scans of the embryo or foetus use the lowest output and shortest time that answers a clinical question, and should be avoided when there is no medical reason.
  27. The correct answer is A (Breast tissue has small density differences, so low energy gives the contrast needed): Breast tissue has very small differences in density, so low-energy x-rays give high contrast. Low energy also means higher absorption, so dose must be controlled and the mean glandular dose is monitored. Compression is still required to spread tissue, lower dose, cut scatter and reduce motion blur.
  28. The correct answer is A (Release compression, explain why it is needed and gain her consent): A patient who asks to stop has withdrawn consent for the moment, so compression is released and the reason for it is explained before she decides whether to continue. Continuing at lower compression overrides her request and also weakens the image. Offering an analgesic is outside the radiographer's role, and the radiologist is not needed to handle a consent conversation the radiographer can have directly.
  29. The correct answer is B (The pectoral muscle is visible and the nipple is in profile): An adequate oblique view shows the pectoral muscle and the nipple in profile so that as much tissue as possible is imaged. A view with the muscle but a turned nipple can hide retroareolar tissue. Cropping the muscle or leaving out the axillary tail removes posterior and upper outer tissue, and even compression does not make up for that. Each of these looks acceptable at a glance but misses tissue the view is meant to include.
  30. The correct answer is C (Osteoporosis, because a T-score of -2.5 or below meets that category): The T-score compares a patient with a young healthy adult reference. A value of -1.0 or above is normal, values between -1.0 and -2.5 indicate low bone mass, and -2.5 or below is osteoporosis. At -2.7 the lumbar spine result sits below the -2.5 threshold. Age-matched comparison belongs to the Z-score, which is why that reading is tempting but does not define this classification.
  31. The correct answer is D (Scan the hip and note the spine values are distorted by hardware and degeneration): Spinal hardware and degenerative change can distort DXA results. DXA is usually done at the lumbar spine and hip, so the unaffected hip can be scanned while the distortion in the spine is noted for the reporter. DXA does not ignore metal, the distortion cannot be subtracted, and hardware is not a contraindication.
  32. The correct answer is C (Tube as far from the patient as practical and receptor as close as possible): Skin dose falls as the tube moves away from the patient, because the beam spreads and the entrance dose per unit area drops. Keeping the receptor close to the patient limits magnification and lets the automatic brightness control work at a lower dose rate. A close tube is tempting for sharpness but raises skin dose, and lifting the receptor away adds magnification and extra dose.
  33. The correct answer is D (Move to the receptor side, away from the beam entry point, behind a shielding screen): Scatter is highest where the beam enters the patient, which is the tube side. Staff should not stand near that side, should step back, use shielding screens and wear lead protection and a thyroid collar. An apron reduces dose but does not make the tube side safe, and moving closer raises exposure.
  34. The correct answer is C (Use pulsed mode at the lowest frame rate that answers the question): Pulsed fluoroscopy at the lowest frame rate that answers the question, plus last image hold instead of continuing to screen, lowers dose. Magnification raises the dose rate and should be avoided when not needed. Continuous mode and higher frame rates deliver more radiation.
  35. The correct answer is B (A deterministic effect, because it appears only above a threshold dose): Deterministic effects such as skin reddening and hair loss appear only above a threshold dose to one area of skin. Stochastic effects such as cancer risk have no threshold, and their chance rises with dose. Hereditary effects are not what skin injury is.
  36. The correct answer is A (Tell the operator and suggest lower frame rate, tighter collimation and a changed angle): Deterministic skin effects depend on the cumulative dose to one patch of skin, so the team must be told while it can still lower it. Moving the beam angle spreads dose across the skin, and frame rate and collimation cut dose further. Staying silent or adding magnification builds skin dose.
  37. The correct answer is B (Step back during the run, as acquisition dose rate is far above that of fluoroscopy): Acquisition runs deliver a much higher dose rate than fluoroscopy, so staff step back during them. Protective equipment reduces dose but does not make standing at the table acceptable. The tube side has the highest scatter, so moving there is the wrong direction.
  38. The correct answer is C (Pause, re-drape the C-arm parts over the field, and avoid reaching across it): The sterile field must be protected. The C-arm parts that pass over it must be draped, and nobody reaches across the field. Lead protects against radiation, not contamination, and a disinfectant wipe does not replace a sterile drape.
  39. The correct answer is A (Ask him to leave or stand behind a lead screen, and check all staff wear protection): In theatre imaging, other people should stay out of the room or behind a lead screen, and everyone in the room should wear protection. Distance from the sterile field is about infection control, not radiation, and a collar alone or standing behind the surgeon does not give adequate protection.
  40. The correct answer is A (Use the usual urgent findings route, treating the AI flag as an aid only): AI is a tool, not the final decision-maker. The practitioner checks its results, keeps human oversight, and an urgent flag still goes through the usual route for communicating urgent findings. Telling the patient or acting on the flag alone skips clinical review, and waiting for a routine report delays an urgent finding.
  41. The correct answer is D (Staff training and quality assurance testing are completed first): New technology needs staff training and local quality assurance testing before it touches patient care. Vendor test reports show how the tool behaves elsewhere, not in this department. One sign off does not replace local checks of image quality and dose. Children are the least suitable first group because any error costs them most.
  42. The correct answer is D (Inform the patient of the high skin dose and arrange follow-up): Deterministic skin effects appear above a threshold dose to one area of skin and can show up later. Patients who have had a high skin dose should be told and followed up as per local policy. Waiting for symptoms or reassuring the patient wrongly delays follow-up.
  43. The correct answer is B (About 523 MBq, worked out as 740 × 2^-0.5): Three hours is 0.5 of the 6 hour half-life, so the factor is 2^-0.5 = 0.7071 and 740 × 0.7071 = 523 MBq. A value of 370 MBq would need a full 6 hours to pass. The 262 MBq figure comes from reading three hours as 1.5 half-lives. Dividing by 1.5 treats decay as a straight line, but radioactive decay is exponential.
  44. The correct answer is D (Recalculate the volume for the current concentration, measure it, and record the time): The decayed syringe no longer contains the prescribed activity, so the volume must be recalculated from the current vial concentration, then measured and recorded with the time of measurement. Giving the original volume repeats the common error of using a volume calculated for an earlier time. Documenting a low dose after giving it is still an out of range administration. Topping up to the same volume ignores activity.
  45. The correct answer is A (About 92.5 MBq, after two half-lives of 110 minutes have passed): From 09:00 to 12:40 is 220 minutes, which is exactly 2 half-lives of 110 minutes. Activity is 370 ÷ 2 ÷ 2 = 92.5 MBq. One half-life would give 185 MBq, three would give 46.3 MBq, and 123 MBq results from treating decay as linear rather than exponential.
  46. The correct answer is C (4 hours, worked out as (6 × 12) ÷ (6 + 12)): Effective half-life is the product of the physical and biological half-lives divided by their sum: (6 × 12) ÷ (6 + 12) = 72 ÷ 18 = 4 hours. The result must be shorter than both inputs because two removal processes act together. An average of 9 hours or a sum of 18 hours is longer than the physical half-life, which cannot happen. Using 6 hours alone ignores clearance by the body.
  47. The correct answer is C (Beta plus decay, where a proton becomes a neutron and a positron is ejected): F-18 decays by beta plus (positron emission). The positron meets an electron, and annihilation produces two 511 keV photons about 180 degrees apart, which is the basis of coincidence detection. The other modes are real but belong to other radionuclides: Mo-99 and I-131 for beta minus, I-123 and Ga-67 for electron capture, and Tc-99m for isomeric transition.
  48. The correct answer is B (Isomeric transition from a metastable state, giving a 140 keV gamma ray alone): Tc-99m sits in a long-lived excited state, marked by the letter m, and drops to the ground state by isomeric transition, releasing a 140 keV gamma ray with no particle emitted. Because no beta particle is released, the dose per detected photon stays low. Beta plus decay with 511 keV photons describes F-18 and PET, electron capture describes agents such as I-123, and beta minus decay with an ejected electron describes the parent Mo-99.
  49. The correct answer is C (555 MBq, worked out as 15 mCi × 37 MBq per mCi): One millicurie equals 37 MBq, so 15 mCi is 15 × 37 = 555 MBq. A factor of 370 gives 5,550 MBq, ten times too much activity, and a factor of 3.7 gives 55.5 MBq, ten times too little. Dividing by 37 inverts the conversion and gives a tiny figure. Decimal place errors of this size are medication errors.
  50. The correct answer is B (3.0 mL, worked out as 300 MBq ÷ 100 MBq per mL): At 08:00 the concentration is 1,200 ÷ 6 = 200 MBq per mL. Six hours is one half-life, so at 14:00 the concentration is 100 MBq per mL. The volume for 300 MBq is 300 ÷ 100 = 3.0 mL. Using 200 MBq per mL ignores decay and would under-dose the patient, while 400 MBq per mL moves the decay in the wrong direction.
  51. The correct answer is D (Withhold the dose, investigate the eluate and kit, and make a new kit from fresh eluate): A kit that fails radiochemical purity must not be given. An old eluate contains more Tc-99 that competes with Tc-99m for labelling, so a fresh elution is the likely fix, and the cause should be investigated. Adding activity does not change the bound fraction, and software cannot undo poor biodistribution that follows a failed label.
  52. The correct answer is B (Sterile saline is drawn through the column by an evacuated vial, giving sodium pertechnetate): Saline pulled through the alumina column by an evacuated vial washes off the loosely held Tc-99m as sodium pertechnetate while the Mo-99 stays bound. A molybdate product would mean Mo-99 had come off the column, which is breakthrough rather than normal elution. Acid is not the eluent for this alumina generator, and a labelled colloid needs a separate kit reaction after elution.
  53. The correct answer is A (About 2,000 MBq, worked out as 4,000 MBq × 0.5 for six hours of ingrowth): Tc-99m has a half-life of about six hours, so after six hours roughly half of the maximum ingrowth has returned. Half of the 4,000 MBq expected at the full wait gives about 2,000 MBq, allowing for a small loss from Mo-99 decay. The full 4,000 MBq needs the longer wait of about a day. A value as low as 500 MBq underestimates ingrowth, and 8,000 MBq has no physical basis because the daughter cannot exceed the parent activity in transient equilibrium.
  54. The correct answer is A (Radionuclidic purity, because higher energy Mo-99 photons pass through the lead sleeve): Mo-99 emits photons of higher energy than the 140 keV photons of Tc-99m, so a lead sleeve that blocks the 140 keV signal still passes the Mo-99 signal. The remaining reading is therefore the Mo-99 breakthrough, a test of radionuclidic purity. Geometry is a separate dose calibrator correction. Radiochemical purity is checked by chromatography, and aluminium uses a colorimetric spot test.
  55. The correct answer is D (A colorimetric spot test for aluminium ions against the permitted limit): Aluminium ions can come off the alumina column, interfere with labelling and clump some kits such as sulphur colloid and MAA. A colorimetric spot test is compared with the limit. The lead sleeve test finds Mo-99, chromatography finds free or reduced technetium after labelling, and the constancy check tests the calibrator, not the eluate.
  56. The correct answer is D (Transient equilibrium, since the parent half-life is about 11 times the daughter's): Mo-99 at about 66 hours is roughly 11 times the 6 hour half-life of Tc-99m, so the pair reaches transient equilibrium. The daughter activity peaks at about 23 hours and then falls at the parent's rate, slightly above it. Secular equilibrium would need a parent far longer lived, usually thousands of times. Reversing the ratio is a common slip, since the parent is the longer lived member.
  57. The correct answer is C (The collimator, a lead or tungsten plate whose tiny holes pass only photons travelling along them): The collimator sits closest to the patient and uses lead or tungsten septa to accept only photons travelling along the hole direction. Without it the crystal would record photons from every direction and no useful image would form. The crystal and tubes come after it in the chain. The head shielding stops stray photons from the room but does not select direction.
  58. The correct answer is B (A low energy high resolution collimator, trading some sensitivity for finer detail): The stem says detail of small nodules matters and counts are plentiful, so the resolution advantage of an LEHR collimator outweighs its lower sensitivity. A high sensitivity type does the reverse. A medium energy type is for photons above Tc-99m, and the general purpose type is a compromise, not the best for fine detail.
  59. The correct answer is A (A medium or high energy collimator, because thicker septa stop higher energy photons): A 364 keV photon is far more energetic than the 140 keV photon of Tc-99m, so it can pass through the thin septa of a low energy collimator and blur the image with septal penetration. Thicker septa in a medium or high energy collimator are needed to absorb those photons. Finer holes improve resolution only when septal penetration is controlled. I-131 is imaged with single photons, so coincidence detection does not apply.
  60. The correct answer is C (Tag the detector out of use, report it, and image on another detector if possible): A repeated cold area suggests a crystal crack, a PMT drift or a collimator fault. The peak is already correct, so retuning does not help. The detector should not be used for patient studies; it is tagged out and reported. Software correction after the fact is the common wrong choice.
  61. The correct answer is C (Sodium iodide doped with a small amount of thallium, written NaI(Tl)): The gamma camera crystal is NaI(Tl), sodium iodide with thallium. BGO and LSO or LYSO are used in PET detector rings. The mixed answer pairs a crystal with a collimator material and is not a gamma camera crystal.
  62. The correct answer is B (129.5 to 150.5 keV, worked out as 140 ± (0.075 × 140) keV): A 15 percent window is the total width, which is 0.15 × 140 = 21 keV, so the limits sit 10.5 keV either side of 140 keV. That gives 129.5 to 150.5 keV. Applying 15 percent to each side gives 119 to 161 keV, a window twice as wide. The 126 to 154 keV range belongs to a 20 percent window, and 7.5 keV is not a percentage of anything.
  63. The correct answer is D (Scattered photons that lost energy in the patient were accepted by the wide window): The only change was a much wider energy window. Photons scattered in the patient lose energy, and a wide window accepts them, which adds haze and lowers contrast. Collimator faults and patient motion could degrade an image, but nothing in the stem points to them and the settings were otherwise standard. Penetration or thick septa would not be tied to the window change.
  64. The correct answer is C (The sum of all tube signals, which is proportional to the photon energy): The Z pulse is the sum of all tube signals, and that sum is proportional to the photon energy. The pulse height analyser compares it with the energy window to accept or reject an event. Position comes from the ratio of neighbouring tube signals, and the matrix is filled afterwards by the computer. Coincidence timing belongs to PET, and uniformity correction is a separate calibration map.
  65. The correct answer is A (The centre of rotation test, checking the offset between mechanical and electronic centres): Streaks and blurring that appear only on reconstructed SPECT slices point to a mismatch between the mechanical and electronic centres of rotation. The centre of rotation test measures that offset directly. Planar images and the flood were fine, so a repeat flood, a planar resolution check or an energy peaking check would be unlikely to explain slice artefacts. The dose calibrator does not influence image geometry.
  66. The correct answer is B (More smoothing lowers noise but also blurs fine detail, hiding small lesions more easily): A smoothing filter averages neighbouring values, so noise falls but fine detail is blurred and small lesions lose contrast. It cannot add counts, sharpen resolution or change how many OSEM iterations are needed. Both resolution and contrast of small lesions suffer, which is the trade the physician needs to understand. Attenuation correction is a separate step.
  67. The correct answer is D (Check the fusion, since movement between scans can cause false hot or cold areas): The CT supplies attenuation correction and localisation, so a cough between the scans misaligns them and can create false hot or cold areas. The fusion should be checked and the data realigned or reacquired if needed. Reporting uncorrected images swaps one limitation for another, and smoothing hides the artefact rather than fixing it. Repeating the CT at a higher current does not repair registration.
  68. The correct answer is B (Coincidence detection of two 511 keV photons along a line of response): A positron annihilates with an electron to give two 511 keV photons about 180 degrees apart. Detectors that register both within a few nanoseconds define a line of response, which replaces the collimator. Time of flight can refine the position along that line but needs a coincidence pair first. The other options describe SPECT, direct positron detection or only part of the camera.
  69. The correct answer is A (Scatter coincidences, where one or both photons changed direction in the body): Scatter changes the photon direction so the line of response is wrong, blurring the image, and it is worse in a large patient. True coincidences give useful data. Randoms rise with injected activity and a wider window, but both were standard here. The remaining answer is not a separate degrading class.
  70. The correct answer is C (Random coincidences, which rise with injected activity and with a wider timing window): Random coincidences come from photons of different annihilations arriving inside the same timing window by chance. They rise with injected activity and with a wider window, matching both changes in the stem. Scatter depends mainly on patient size, and true events are the useful signal. Multiple coincidences also rise with activity, so they do not fall.
  71. The correct answer is A (Review the uncorrected images, since CT metal artefact can create a false hot spot): Metal causes a CT artefact that carries into the attenuation correction and can appear as a false hot spot. Reviewing the images without attenuation correction shows whether the focus is real. Lowering the current usually makes streaks worse, smoothing does not settle the question, and CT based correction is not reliable beside metal.
  72. The correct answer is D (SUV 3.75, worked out as 15 kBq/mL ÷ 4 kBq per gram): The injected activity of 280 MBq is 280,000 kBq, and 70 kg is 70,000 g, giving 4 kBq per gram. SUV is 15 ÷ 4 = 3.75. Inverting the ratio gives 0.27. Using kilograms in place of grams gives about 0.004, and leaving MBq unconverted gives 3,750.
  73. The correct answer is C (Body weight, blood glucose level, uptake time and dose calibrator accuracy): SUV depends on body weight, blood glucose, uptake time and the accuracy of the activity measurement from the dose calibrator. Each other list contains one or two real influences, such as uptake time or reconstruction method, but also includes items that have no effect on the number. A list is only correct if every item in it alters SUV.
  74. The correct answer is D (Stop dispensing, repeat the check, test for contamination, and report if it persists): A constancy reading that drifts on every setting suggests a fault or contamination in the chamber. Because patient doses depend on this instrument, dispensing stops while the check is repeated, contamination is tested and the problem is reported if it persists. A linearity test is a real test but does not answer the failed one. Applying an old correction or resetting the baseline would hide the drift.
  75. The correct answer is D (Linearity, which checks the reading against activity from a few MBq to GBq): Linearity checks the response across the whole activity range, often using decay of a high activity Tc-99m source or calibrated attenuating sleeves. Constancy is the daily reference check, geometry looks at volume and container, and accuracy compares with standard sources of known activity.
  76. The correct answer is B (Accuracy, linearity and geometry, in addition to a constancy check): After repair the calibrator needs accuracy, linearity and geometry tests as well as a constancy check, because each tests a different property. Electronics work can alter response across the activity range, and the container and geometry still need confirming. No single test or pair of tests can stand in for the full set.
  77. The correct answer is A (The geometry test, comparing readings across different volumes and containers): Geometry shows whether the reading changes with the volume or container. Accuracy uses traceable standards, constancy looks at day to day consistency with a reference source, and linearity looks at proportionality across activity.
  78. The correct answer is B (Standard sources such as Co-57 and Cs-137 are measured against their certificates): Accuracy asks whether the calibrator reads the true activity, so it needs sources of known activity with certificates, for example Co-57 and Cs-137. A source measured daily against its own earlier reading checks constancy, not accuracy. Different volumes test geometry, and a decaying source measured over days tests linearity. Each of those is a real calibrator test, which is why they tempt, but none compares the reading with a certified value.
  79. The correct answer is A (The point source is intrinsic uniformity and the flood phantom is extrinsic uniformity): Intrinsic uniformity is measured with the collimator removed, which fits the point source at a distance. Extrinsic uniformity is measured with the collimator attached, which fits the flood phantom. Calling both by one name ignores the collimator, which is the only difference between the names. Swapping the two names reverses the definitions.
  80. The correct answer is C (A Geiger-Muller survey meter, which responds to beta and gamma radiation on surfaces): A GM survey meter detects beta and gamma and is used to look for contamination on surfaces, hands and clothing. A well counter is for small samples, an ionisation chamber is best for higher dose rates, and badges record dose over time and do not find contamination.
  81. The correct answer is C (An ionisation chamber survey meter, which gives a more accurate exposure rate at higher levels): Near a patient with a large I-131 dose the rate is high, and an ionisation chamber gives a dependable exposure rate across higher levels. A Geiger-Muller meter gives counts whose response changes with photon energy. A sodium iodide probe is very sensitive at low levels but saturates when the rate is high. A personal dosimeter records the wearer's dose, not the exposure rate at a point.
  82. The correct answer is B (A well counter, which counts small samples with high sensitivity): A wipe holds a tiny activity, so the instrument must be very sensitive and take a sample, which is what a well counter does. A surface meter reads counts at the bench, not the activity on the wipe. A dose calibrator suits syringes and vials with far larger activities and is not sensitive enough for a wipe. A camera without a collimator can detect photons but is not a calibrated sample counter.
  83. The correct answer is C (Tell others nearby and stop the spread, then keep people out of the area): The sequence starts with telling others and stopping the spread, then keeping people out, covering the spill with absorbent paper, putting on gloves and a second layer of protective clothing, and cleaning from the outside edge toward the centre. Cleaning from the centre outwards spreads activity, and reporting and surveys follow containment.
  84. The correct answer is A (From the outside edge of the spill inwards towards the centre, so contamination does not spread): Working from the outside edge inwards keeps the contaminated area from growing, so clean surfaces stay clean. Starting at the centre seems to remove the bulk first but pushes activity outwards. Long sweeps with reused paper drag activity across the bench. Wet swabs and circular wiping also spread liquid beyond its original edge.
  85. The correct answer is B (Wash with lukewarm water and mild soap, without scrubbing the skin raw): Lukewarm water and mild soap remove contamination gently. Scrubbing hard can break the skin and let activity in, solvents can increase absorption, and waiting leaves activity on the skin when it is removable.
  86. The correct answer is C (5 percent, worked out as 20 counts divided by 400 counts, multiplied by 100): For Poisson counting the standard deviation is √400 = 20 counts. The percentage error is 20 ÷ 400 × 100 = 5 percent. Quoting 20 percent confuses a number of counts with a percentage. Dividing by 4,000 moves the decimal point one place too far, giving 0.5 percent. Dividing by 200 uses half the true count and doubles the answer to 10 percent.
  87. The correct answer is D (4 minutes, worked out as 10,000 counts ÷ 2,500 counts per minute): Counting error is 100 ÷ √N per cent, so a 1 per cent error needs N = 10,000 counts. At 2,500 counts per minute that takes 4 minutes. Two minutes gives 5,000 counts and an error near 1.4 per cent, which is still too high. Longer counts of 10 or 16 minutes do reach 1 per cent but waste time, and error falls with the square root of counts, not linearly.
  88. The correct answer is D (Yes, the second error is 0.5 percent, worked out as 100 ÷ √40,000): Percentage counting error is 100 ÷ √N. With 10,000 counts it is 100 ÷ 100 = 1 percent, and with 40,000 counts it is 100 ÷ 200 = 0.5 percent. Because error falls with the square root of counts, quadrupling the counts halves the error. Dividing by 4 gives 0.25 percent and treats the error as inversely proportional to counts. Claiming no change ignores the benefit of the longer run.
  89. The correct answer is A (A ring badge worn on the hand that handles unsealed doses, beside the body badge): The hands come closest to the unshielded dose, so a ring badge is worn on the hand that handles doses, in addition to the body badge. A collar device and a waist badge are personal dosimeters but report trunk dose, which is much lower than finger dose. An area monitor warns about the room and says nothing about one person's hands. Only the ring badge measures the dose where the exposure is highest.
  90. The correct answer is B (6 days, worked out as (8 × 24) divided by (8 + 24)): Activity falls through both physical decay and biological clearance, so the effective half-life applies. It is (8 × 24) ÷ (8 + 24) = 192 ÷ 32 = 6 days, which is shorter than either input. The mean of 16 days is tempting but has no physical basis. Using 24 days or 8 days treats only one process as acting, so both overstate how long the patient stays radioactive, and the correct figure is what guides release advice under the licence.
  91. The correct answer is A (Effective half-life cannot exceed the shorter of the two, so the sum was done wrongly): Te = (6 × 10) ÷ (6 + 10) = 60 ÷ 16 = 3.75 hours. The 15 hours result is longer than both values, which cannot be right, and probably comes from multiplying without dividing correctly. The effective half-life is always shorter than both and cannot exceed the shorter one.
  92. The correct answer is D (3 hours, worked out as (12 × 4) ÷ (12 + 4)): The effective half-life combines both removal processes: Te = (12 × 4) ÷ (12 + 4) = 48 ÷ 16 = 3 hours. It must be shorter than either half-life because two processes remove the tracer together. The average of 8 hours and the sum of 16 hours are both longer than the shorter value, which is impossible. Using 4 hours alone ignores the physical decay.
  93. The correct answer is C (One nuclear decay occurring each second in the sample): A becquerel is a rate of exactly one nuclear transformation per second, so it describes activity and not a mass or a detected count.
