The thousand figure is a kilobecquerel mix up, and MBq and GBq are the multiples used on requests. Counts per minute depend on detector efficiency and are not the same as true decays. The radium figure is the curie, an older unit still seen in some texts.
  94. The correct answer is D (No, because without the sleeve the 140 keV photons swamp any signal from higher energy Mo-99): The breakthrough check depends on the lead sleeve stopping most of the 140 keV Tc-99m photons so the higher energy Mo-99 photons can be measured. Without the sleeve the Tc-99m signal swamps the small Mo-99 contribution, so the check is not valid. No calibrator setting can separate the two nuclides without shielding, and the vial wall does not provide it. A normal total reading cannot show purity because Tc-99m dominates it.
  95. The correct answer is A (Sodium pertechnetate, which is then reduced inside each cold kit vial): Saline pushed through the column carries off the Tc-99m made by Mo-99 decay as sodium pertechnetate, and the kit chemistry then reduces it so it can bind a carrier. The colloid form is an impurity that appears when labelling goes wrong, not what the column releases. A finished labelled agent only exists after the eluate meets a kit. Mo-99 staying on the column is the point of the generator, and any leaking through is a quality failure.
  96. The correct answer is D (It reduces the technetium so that it can bind to the carrier molecule): Pertechnetate cannot bind the carrier until it is reduced, and stannous chloride is the usual reducing agent in the kit. The buffer holds the pH in a workable range, which is a separate job that is easy to mix up. Sterility comes from aseptic technique and manufacture, not from this ingredient. Shielding is done with lead or tungsten containers, since a chemical in the vial cannot absorb gamma rays.
  97. The correct answer is C (Free pertechnetate in the injected product, from a labelling problem): Bright thyroid and stomach are the sites where free pertechnetate goes, so the first suspicion is poor labelling of the MAA kit and the RCP should be checked. A camera fault would not light up two organs that handle iodide like ions. A shunt would show particles in the brain and kidneys, not the stomach. Colloid impurity gives liver and spleen uptake, which is not what the stem describes.
  98. The correct answer is A (Reduced hydrolysed technetium, a colloid taken up by liver and spleen): Technetium that was reduced but bound to water forms a colloid, and macrophages in the liver, spleen and marrow trap it, giving exactly this picture. Free pertechnetate would show the thyroid and stomach instead. Mo-99 breakthrough adds dose but does not create organ specific colloid uptake. Poor renal function increases soft tissue background but would not make the liver and spleen stand out.
  99. The correct answer is B (Free pertechnetate, which travels up the strip with the solvent front): In this system only free pertechnetate runs with the solvent front into the top piece. Reduced hydrolysed technetium stays at the origin, so it is counted in the bottom piece. Labelled MDP also stays at the origin in saline, so a second solvent system is used to separate the remaining forms. Describing both soluble forms as migrating confuses solubility with how the strip behaves.
  100. The correct answer is B (Purity is 92 percent, below the limit, so the kit is quarantined): Total counts are 48,000, so free pertechnetate is 2,400 ÷ 48,000 = 5 percent. Purity is 100 − 5 − 3 = 92 percent, which is under the 95 percent limit. Using only the free fraction gives 95 and using only the colloid gives 97, both of which skip an impurity. The 8 percent figure is the combined impurity, not the purity.
  101. The correct answer is C (Quarantine the vial and record the failure per department procedure): A kit that fails RCP is not used on a patient, and the vial is quarantined, recorded and investigated under the department procedure. A higher activity gives the impurity a larger dose and still spoils the image. Retesting until a pass appears hides a real failure. Dilution lowers concentration but not the proportion of free pertechnetate, so it fixes nothing.
  102. The correct answer is D (Let the alcohol dry on the septum, then puncture without touching it): The alcohol needs time to dry to act, and a dried septum is then punctured without being touched again. Going straight through a wet septum carries alcohol into the vial and skips the drying step. Gauze that is not sterile adds contamination and touches the septum. A second swab only restarts the same wait.
  103. The correct answer is C (Change the gloves and use shielding and planning to limit exposure): Contaminated gloves are changed, and an aseptic step is never cut to reduce dose. Shielding, distance and planning cut exposure without harming sterility. Skipping the swab trades one risk for another. Shielding the syringe does not fix contaminated gloves, and a wipe leaves the contamination in place.
  104. The correct answer is B (It can clump the particles and spoil the preparation): Aluminium leaking off the column can clump particle based agents such as sulfur colloid and MAA, which spoils the preparation. It is a chemical impurity, not a sign of Mo-99 breakthrough, which is tested separately. It does not affect sterility, which is controlled by other steps. The reducing agent problem sounds close, but clumping of particles is the effect that matters here.
  105. The correct answer is D (Molybdenum-99 leaking into the eluate, which adds unwanted dose to the patient): Breakthrough means the parent Mo-99 has leaked into the eluate, which adds dose and no useful image. Normal decay of Tc-99m is expected and is not breakthrough. Aluminium leakage is the other quality limit and has a different effect. A weak vacuum affects yield but is not what the term describes.
  106. The correct answer is A (Reduce the particle number as the protocol directs and inform the physician): A right to left shunt is one of the situations where the MAA particle count is reduced, and the physician is told. Giving the full count risks many particles reaching the brain and kidneys, and imaging afterwards only documents it. Slow injection does not stop particles crossing the shunt. DMSA binds renal cortex and cannot show lung perfusion.
  107. The correct answer is B (Inject with the patient supine and avoid drawing blood into the syringe): Injecting supine reduces gravity effects on the distribution, and blood is not drawn back because clots form around the particles and make hot spots. The upright option has both errors. Drawing back while supine still risks clots. Standing makes the gravity effect larger, which is the opposite of what the technique aims for.
  108. The correct answer is A (DMSA, because proximal tubule cells hold it in the cortex for late imaging): DMSA binds to proximal tubule cells and stays in the cortex, so late images show scarring or functioning tissue. MAG3 and DTPA are cleared in urine, which suits dynamic studies but leaves little cortex activity later. MDP is a bone agent and does not target kidney cortex.
  109. The correct answer is D (Fat soluble cations enter cells with blood flow and stay in mitochondria): Sestamibi and tetrofosmin are lipophilic cations that cross the cell membrane in proportion to blood flow and are retained in mitochondria. Capillary blockade describes MAA in the lungs. Phosphorylation and trapping describes FDG. Glomerular filtration has nothing to do with myocardial uptake.
  110. The correct answer is B (Macrophages in the reticuloendothelial system remove the particles from blood): Colloid particles are removed by macrophages of the reticuloendothelial system in liver, spleen and marrow. Uptake into liver cells followed by bile excretion is how mebrofenin behaves. Capillary blockade describes MAA, whose particles are much larger. Kidney filtration does not deliver colloid to the spleen.
  111. The correct answer is A (Tell the nuclear medicine physician about the opioid before injecting the dose): Opioids can cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, so the bile duct may look blocked and the scan may be misread or need to be changed. The physician decides how to handle it. Fasting alone does not cover this interaction. More activity does not remove spasm, and changing a patient's medicines is outside the technologist's scope.
  112. The correct answer is C (It is handled like iodide and trapped by gastric mucosa): Pertechnetate behaves like iodide, so it is taken up by gastric mucosa, including ectopic gastric tissue in a Meckel's diverticulum. Staying in the blood pool is how labelled red cells show active bleeding, which is a different study. Biliary secretion is how hepatobiliary agents behave. Glucose trapping describes FDG, not simple pertechnetate.
  113. The correct answer is C (FDG enters cells like glucose, is phosphorylated and then trapped): FDG is a glucose analogue, so it enters cells that use a lot of glucose and, once phosphorylated, it is trapped. Binding a membrane antigen is how PSMA agents work. Mitochondrial retention in proportion to flow is the sestamibi pattern. Macrophage removal is the behaviour of colloid particles.
  114. The correct answer is D (Hold the injection and follow protocol to correct glucose or rebook): High blood glucose competes with FDG and lowers uptake, so the injection is held and the protocol is followed to correct the level or rebook. More activity adds dose without fixing competition. Injecting anyway wastes the dose and gives a poor study. Insulin drives FDG into muscle and fat, and its timing follows the protocol rather than being improvised.
  115. The correct answer is B (Ask the patient to rest quietly, because active muscle takes up FDG): Working muscle takes up FDG, so loud talking and arm movement during uptake create unwanted muscle activity. Resting quietly is the step that addresses what the technologist sees. Walking raises muscle uptake rather than helping delivery. A cool room increases brown fat uptake, since warmth is what reduces it. Voiding before the scan is a useful step but does not fix muscle uptake.
  116. The correct answer is A (It binds to prostate specific membrane antigen found on prostate cancer cells): PSMA agents bind to prostate specific membrane antigen on prostate cancer cells. Glucose trapping describes FDG. Filtration describes DTPA. Capillary lodging by particle size describes MAA in the lung.
  117. The correct answer is D (I-123 for the image and I-131 for the treatment, which emits beta particles): I-123 has a gamma energy suited to the camera and a half-life of about 13 hours, so it suits diagnostic imaging. I-131 has a half-life of about 8 days and emits beta particles, which destroy thyroid cancer tissue. Using I-131 for the image gives a higher dose and a poorer picture. I-123 has no significant beta emission, so a low dose is the reason it cannot treat cancer.
  118. The correct answer is C (Plastic, because lead struck by beta particles creates extra X-rays): Beta particles stopped in lead produce bremsstrahlung X-rays, so plastic is used first for beta emitters. Lead and tungsten are both dense materials that would generate those X-rays. Leaving the vial unshielded exposes staff to beta particles and scatter, so shielding is still required.
  119. The correct answer is B (Ga-67 has a half-life of about 78 hours, so safety advice must be adjusted): Ga-67 stays active for days because its half-life is about 78 hours, so the radiation safety advice for the patient and household differs from a technetium scan. Treating it like Tc-99m underestimates how long it remains. Its half-life is longer, not shorter. Distance is only one of the basic protections and does not replace adjusted advice.
  120. The correct answer is A (Tell the supervising doctor about the caffeine, since it can make the stress test fail): Caffeine blocks adenosine receptors and can make the stress fail, so the supervising doctor decides whether to delay or change the test. Regadenoson acts on those receptors, so recent caffeine does matter. An extra dose is not the technologist's decision. Water for an hour does not remove caffeine.
  121. The correct answer is B (Record both and tell the physician, since iodine sources can reduce uptake): Iodinated contrast and amiodarone both load the body with iodine and can block radioiodine uptake, so the technologist records both and tells the physician, who decides. Diet is only one source of iodine. Amiodarone persists for months, so stopping it now does not clear the effect, and stopping a drug is not the technologist's decision. Contrast iodine can also persist for weeks after a CT.
  122. The correct answer is B (Stop and resolve the discrepancy with the referrer before any injection): Identifiers must match across the patient, request and label, so a mismatch stops the process until it is resolved. A label that matches the form does not prove the right patient. Changing the form to fit hides a possible wrong patient error. The identifiers come from the patient answering open questions, not from a porter.
  123. The correct answer is D (Do not inject, recheck the calculation and reading, then redraw within tolerance): The activity must be within the allowed variation before injection, so the technologist holds the dose, rechecks the calculation and reading, and redraws. Changing scan time cannot undo a wrong administered activity. Injecting first and reporting afterwards leaves the patient with the wrong dose. Calling the difference close enough ignores a limit that is set for a reason.
  124. The correct answer is A (480 MBq, found by subtracting the residual from the reading): The patient receives what left the syringe, which is the pre-injection reading minus the residual, so 520 minus 40 gives 480 MBq. Recording the pre-injection value overstates the dose because activity stayed behind. Adding the residual gives more than was ever drawn up. Subtracting it twice understates the dose given.
  125. The correct answer is A (Stay with her, call for help, lie her flat, then record and report): A vasovagal faint is a mild and usual reaction, and the correct approach is to stay with the patient, call for help, record the event and report it through the incident system. Leaving her alone risks injury. Late recording and early discharge skip the monitoring and reporting steps. Adrenaline is for severe allergic reactions and not suggested by these signs.
  126. The correct answer is B (Stop injecting, note the site and amount, and tell the physician): Resistance, swelling and stinging suggest extravasation, so the injection stops and the site and amount are noted and reported to the physician. Resuming after the swelling settles puts more tracer into tissue. Removing the cannula and raising the arm may help the patient but skips the record and report. A full repeat dose needs a physician's decision.
  127. The correct answer is D (Stop the infusion and escalate to the physician at once): Extravasation of a therapy agent means stopping at once and escalating, because tissue damage is possible. Slowing the rate keeps pushing activity into tissue. Finishing and imaging later only measures the damage. A warm pack does not stop the leak.
  128. The correct answer is C (Test the cannula with a saline flush first): A saline flush shows whether the cannula sits in the vein before any radioactivity is given. Drawing back with the radioactive syringe contaminates the syringe and can clot some agents. Leaving the tourniquet on raises vein pressure and encourages leakage. Diluting the dose changes the prepared volume and does not stop leakage.
  129. The correct answer is C (The nuclear medicine physician decides, after the technologist records her answer): If a patient may be pregnant, the nuclear medicine physician decides on delay or change by weighing justification and optimisation. The technologist asks, records and escalates. The referrer's urgency matters but does not override the physician. The patient is informed but does not make the decision alone.
  130. The correct answer is D (Therapy is not given while she is breastfeeding, so refer her to the physician): Radioiodine moves strongly into milk, and therapy is not given to a woman who is still breastfeeding. A short break or discarding milk does not make therapy suitable while feeding continues. Lowering the activity would undertreat and still expose the infant. The physician manages the plan.
  131. The correct answer is D (Give the interruption time stated in the department protocol, in writing and in words): Breastfeeding advice depends on the agent, and the interruption time comes from the department protocol, given in writing and in words. A remembered figure may be wrong. MAA and free pertechnetate pass into milk more than some other agents, so assuming none is needed is unsafe. Permanent cessation is not supported by the protocol.
  132. The correct answer is C (Discard the vial and prepare a new kit, because the stated use-by time has passed): After reconstitution the kit has a shorter use-by time, because labelling can fall and sterility is not guaranteed, and a prepared dose past that time is discarded. An early purity pass does not cover later hours. Extra activity adds dose without fixing labelling. Expiry rules do not allow single patient exceptions.
  133. The correct answer is A (Place the needle in a sharps container, then store the waste to decay): Sharps go into a sharps container first, and short lived waste is then held to decay and surveyed before disposal under the licence. Sending it off site the same day or binning items on a low reading skips the decay and licence steps. Discarding the sharps container straight away gets the sharps step right but leaves out decay storage.
  134. The correct answer is A (Monday at 04:00, after 60 hours of decay in storage): Ten half-lives of 6 hours is 60 hours. Friday 16:00 plus 24 hours is Saturday 16:00, plus 48 hours is Sunday 16:00, and plus 60 hours is Monday 04:00. Sunday is only eight half-lives. Saturday is four. Tuesday is sixteen and holds the waste longer than needed.
  135. The correct answer is C (20 mSv per year averaged over five years, with 1 mSv per year for the public): The occupational limit is 20 mSv per year averaged over five years, with no more than 50 mSv in any single year, and the public limit is 1 mSv per year. The 50 mSv figure is the single year ceiling, not the average. The public limit is not 5 mSv. Swapping the two values would give the public a higher limit than workers.
  136. The correct answer is B (Plan the steps, use a shielded holder and keep the vial shielded): Planning the steps reduces time, a shielded holder and shielded vial provide shielding, and none of this breaks asepsis. An apron alone is thin protection against the 140 keV photons from a high activity vial. Speed and long forceps help with time and distance but leave the vial unshielded. Standing closer gives up distance, which is the most effective protection.
  137. The correct answer is C (Regulators and licence conditions differ by jurisdiction, so she checks local rules): Radioactive material is controlled under the law of each state or territory, and regulators and conditions differ. ARPANSA publishes national codes that most jurisdictions adopt, but it does not license every site. Storage conditions can differ as well as transport, so she must check local rules.
  138. The correct answer is B (The diagnostic reference level, used to review optimisation): The diagnostic reference level is the typical activity for a standard sized patient and is the figure an audit compares against when checking optimisation. The occupational limit and the investigation level both concern staff doses. The carer constraint protects people who look after patients, not the patient dose benchmark.
  139. The correct answer is A (120 MBq, the protocol minimum, as the scaled amount is lower): The weight fraction is 14 ÷ 70 = 0.2, so the scaled activity is 0.2 × 500 = 100 MBq. That is below the protocol minimum of 120 MBq, so the minimum is given. Using 100 skips the minimum. The full adult activity overdoses the child. A fraction of 0.1 would be a calculation slip.
  140. The correct answer is D (3.3 mL, using a vial activity of 600 MBq at 13:00): Six hours is one half-life, so the vial holds 600 MBq at 13:00 and the concentration is 600 ÷ 5.0 = 120 MBq per mL. The volume is 400 ÷ 120 = 3.33 mL. Without decay the concentration is 240 and the volume 1.7. Two half-lives give 300 MBq, 60 per mL and 6.7 mL. A 12 hour half-life gives about 849 MBq, then 2.4 mL.
  141. The correct answer is C (About 283 MBq, as 9 hours is one and a half half-lives): Nine hours is 9 ÷ 6 = 1.5 half-lives, so A = 800 × (1/2)^1.5 = 800 × 0.3536 = 283 MBq. One half-life gives 400 and two give 200, which bracket the true value. Dividing by 6 is not a decay calculation.
  142. The correct answer is A (About 92.5 MBq, worked out as 370 MBq × ½^(220 ÷ 110)): From 09:00 to 12:40 is 220 minutes, and 220 ÷ 110 = 2 half-lives. The activity falls to 370 × ¼ = 92.5 MBq. Treating the delay as one half-life gives 185 MBq, and three half-lives give about 46 MBq. Using a six hour half-life gives 370 × 0.655 = 242 MBq, which treats F-18 like technetium and understates the decay.
  143. The correct answer is C (0.74 mL, worked out as 185 MBq ÷ 250 MBq per mL): Volume equals activity ÷ concentration, so 185 ÷ 250 = 0.74 mL. Dividing the other way gives 1.35, which has the wrong form and would overdose the patient. A volume of 0.37 mL would deliver only 92.5 MBq. A decimal place slip gives 0.074 mL, ten times too little.
  144. The correct answer is B (A half-life of about 6 hours and a 140 keV gamma ray): Tc-99m has a half-life of about 6 hours and a 140 keV gamma ray, which suits a gamma camera. A 6 day half-life would leave activity in the patient for much longer. The 511 keV photons come from positron annihilation in PET. A 364 keV gamma ray is more typical of I-131.
  145. The correct answer is A (Do not administer, return the syringe and prepare the agent named on the request): The agent on the label must match the request, so a mismatch means the syringe is not given and the correct agent is prepared. A correct activity reading says nothing about which radiopharmaceutical is in the syringe. A referrer cannot make a bone agent suitable for a gastric emptying study, and noting a discrepancy afterwards does not undo giving the wrong agent. The patient was identified correctly, so the fault lies with the syringe.
  146. The correct answer is D (Record the event and report it through the incident system under local policy): Adverse reactions are recorded and reported through the incident system under local policy, whatever their severity, and the policy also directs any external reporting. A note in the patient record alone leaves the department without a trackable incident. Reporting only externally skips the local record and review. Making reporting depend on a serious classification would lose the mild events that show patterns.
  147. The correct answer is D (Stop, confirm identity using three identifiers, and resolve the mismatch first.): The mismatch between the label, request and patient is a stop point, and three identifiers must agree before the injection. A matching surname is tempting in a busy list but cannot separate similarly named patients. A single spoken identifier is too weak, and noting the problem in the report comes after an irreversible dose. A wrong-patient administration is a reportable incident, so the check is completed first.
  148. The correct answer is D (Hold the study and ask the referrer and nuclear medicine physician whether it should proceed.): A possible pregnancy means the study is usually stopped and the referrer and physician decide whether it goes ahead. Reducing the activity is tempting because the dose is low, but that is a clinical decision, not the technologist's. Injecting first reverses the correct order of checking, and a signed form does not replace the justification decision. The late period is the stem detail that triggers the hold.
  149. The correct answer is B (Radioiodine needs stricter advice, often ending breastfeeding, so the physician decides.): Many Tc-99m agents need only a short pause, but radioiodine is concentrated in breast tissue and milk, so advice is much stricter and the physician decides. The friend's experience is tempting because it was also a nuclear medicine study. Distance protects from external radiation but does nothing about iodine in milk. Discarding part of the milk leaves the rest contaminated.
  150. The correct answer is C (Recheck the decay correction, then compare the measured activity with the prescribed dose.): The calibrator measures what is actually in the syringe, and activity falls with time, so the label must be decay corrected to now. The decision rests on comparing the measured activity with the prescribed dose. Ignoring the reading or trusting the label skips the one measurement taken at the time of use. Topping up changes the preparation without a proper recheck and can give the wrong activity or volume.
  151. The correct answer is C (About 424 MBq, using exponential decay over three hours): Three hours is half of a 6 hour half-life, so the factor is 0.5 raised to the power 0.5, which is 0.707. Then 600 × 0.707 is about 424 MBq. A full half-life would give 300 MBq, but only half of one has passed. A straight line fall to 450 MBq ignores that decay is exponential, and 150 MBq needs two full half-lives.
  152. The correct answer is A (Recognise only about 140 MBq remains, below the 210 MBq required, and obtain a new dose.): The interval of 110 minutes is one half life, so 280 MBq has fallen to 140 MBq. The patient needs 70 × 3 = 210 MBq at injection, so this syringe is too weak. The label figure is tempting but describes the calibration time, and an estimate of 200 MBq is a decay slip. Longer scanning cannot replace missing activity, so a correct dose is prepared.
  153. The correct answer is A (186 MBq, worked out as 62 kg × 3 MBq per kg): The protocol sets the activity as body weight multiplied by the dose rate: 62 × 3 = 186 MBq, checked as 60 × 3 = 180 plus 2 × 3 = 6. Rounding the weight to 60 kg gives 180 MBq and drops the last two kilograms. A rate of 2 MBq per kg gives 124 MBq and a rate of 3.3 gives about 205 MBq. Both depart from the protocol rate stated in the question.
  154. The correct answer is B (Drink extra fluids, empty his bladder often, and void before the pelvic views.): Extra fluid and frequent voiding clear unbound tracer through the kidneys, which sharpens the images and lowers the bladder dose. Withholding fluid keeps more background tracer in the body. Food does not compete with MDP, so fasting is not needed, and brisk walking does not improve skeletal uptake in a useful way.
  155. The correct answer is B (Remove the metal items and repeat the pelvic view to see whether the defect disappears.): Metal attenuates photons, producing a cold defect exactly where the button and buckle lie. Removing them and re-imaging separates artefact from disease. Cold lesions can be real, which makes the first idea tempting, but the clothing detail decides it. Urine contamination causes hot spots, not cold ones, and longer acquisition cannot restore photons that metal has blocked.
  156. The correct answer is D (Clean the skin and clothing, then repeat the view): A single intense focus beside an incontinent patient with a soiled pad points to urine contamination, which can mimic a lesion. Cleaning and re-imaging settles it quickly. Calling it a bone lesion risks needless investigation, and calling it urinary activity without checking leaves the question unanswered. Waiting leaves the contamination on the skin, because it does not clear by itself in a useful time.
  157. The correct answer is A (Suspected bone infection, or complex regional pain affecting a limb): Three phase imaging adds flow and blood pool images, which show increased blood supply and inflammation, so it suits suspected infection or complex regional pain. Routine staging for metastases relies on delayed whole body images. A healed fracture or generalised degenerative change does not raise a blood flow question, so the extra phases add dose and time without benefit.
  158. The correct answer is C (Tell the physician, who will decide whether the study should go ahead.): Recent iodinated contrast can lower thyroid uptake, and the physician sets how long it must be stopped. Pertechnetate is trapped by thyroid cells, so contrast can still interfere, which makes the first idea tempting but false. Raising the activity adds dose with no guarantee of benefit. Choosing a delay yourself guesses at a decision that belongs to the physician.
  159. The correct answer is B (I-123 is trapped and made into hormone, whereas pertechnetate is only trapped.): Thyroid cells trap both agents, but only iodine is built into hormone, so I-123 follows the whole pathway and pertechnetate stops at trapping. Reversing them is a common slip. Saying both are incorporated ignores that pertechnetate is not iodine. Both do enter thyroid cells, which is why the thyroid is seen.
  160. The correct answer is D (Reproduce the early neck position and field of view so the two sets can be compared): An adenoma is recognised because it retains tracer longer than the thyroid, which only shows when early and delayed images can be compared directly. Identical positioning and field of view make that comparison valid. Comfort alone, a wider field or a different timing makes the sets hard to compare. The adenoma retains tracer, so washout timing at one hour would miss the pattern.
  161. The correct answer is C (A reversible defect, which suggests stress-induced ischaemia in that region): A defect on stress that fills at rest is reversible, meaning heart muscle becomes short of blood only under demand, which is ischaemia. A fixed defect would remain empty on both sets and suggests scar. Matched defect is a lung study term. Artefact is possible for any study, but this specific pattern is the classic sign of ischaemia.
  162. The correct answer is D (Pharmacological stress with a vasodilator, since exercise results are harder to read here.): Exercise is usually preferred, but left bundle branch block makes exercise perfusion results harder to read, so a vasodilator is chosen. His fitness is the tempting detail. Dobutamine is mainly the alternative when a vasodilator is contraindicated, and nothing in the stem rules that out. Stress is not forbidden, and rest-only imaging cannot show ischaemia.
  163. The correct answer is A (Stop and tell the physician, who may choose dobutamine stress or reschedule the study.): Active wheeze with recent reliever use is a contraindication to adenosine and regadenoson, so the physician must be told before any infusion. Dobutamine is the usual alternative when a vasodilator is avoided. Her caffeine avoidance is tempting but answers a different question. A reduced rate or pre-treatment with an inhaler does not make a contraindication safe.
  164. The correct answer is D (Stop, inform the physician, and arrange to reschedule the study for another day.): Caffeine blocks the receptor that adenosine acts on, so the result may be falsely normal. The correct response is to stop, tell the physician and reschedule. Normal observations do not change the pharmacology. A double dose adds side effects without a reliable result. Regadenoson acts on the same receptor, so it is also affected.
  165. The correct answer is B (Stop the stress test and call for help, acting first in this emergency.): Chest pain with ECG change is a stop point, and the technologist acts first in an emergency, working alongside the supervising clinician. Reaching the target heart rate or giving tracer is secondary to patient safety. Waiting for permission is tempting because the clinician leads, but delay can harm. Resuscitation equipment and a trained clinician should already be present.
  166. The correct answer is D (Near peak stress, with the patient continuing briefly afterwards): The tracer reflects blood flow at the moment of injection, so it is given near peak stress, and exercise continues for a short time so it is taken up under stress conditions. Injecting at the start, at half the target heart rate or after exercise ends would show flow at the wrong moment. Peak stress is when flow differences between vessels are greatest.
  167. The correct answer is C (It increases heart rate and the strength of contraction, raising the heart's demand.): Dobutamine raises heart rate and contractility, so it mimics exercise by increasing demand. The vasodilators adenosine and regadenoson dilate the coronary arteries instead, which is why the two types are not interchangeable in the same patients. Receptor blocking describes caffeine, and slowing the heart is the opposite of what dobutamine does.
  168. The correct answer is B (A mismatch: ventilated but not perfused, which raises suspicion of embolism.): A segment that is ventilated but not perfused is a mismatch and raises concern for embolism. A matched defect, absent on both, points more toward airway disease. The reverse version has the wrong image missing. Normal ventilation does not exclude embolism, because a blocked vessel is exactly what gives this pattern.
  169. The correct answer is A (Follow the physician's protocol, which reduces the number of MAA particles for this patient.): In a right to left shunt, particles can pass into the systemic circulation instead of lodging in the lungs, so fewer are given to limit that exposure. The protocol set by the physician decides the number. More particles would increase the number reaching the brain and kidneys. The study can still proceed with a modified preparation, so changing the study alone is unnecessary.
  170. The correct answer is A (Drawing blood back clumped the particles; avoid drawing back more than needed.): Pulling blood into the syringe can clump the macroaggregated albumin particles, which then lodge as dense hot spots. The patient history of drawing back points directly to preparation. Emboli appear as cold defects, not hot spots, and the ventilation gas is inhaled, not injected. Too few particles would give a poorer image, not intense foci.
  171. The correct answer is C (It binds to the kidney cortex, showing scars and the share of function in each kidney.): DMSA binds to the kidney cortex, so late images show scars and each kidney's contribution to function. Drainage and obstruction questions are answered by the dynamic MAG3 study with a diuretic. A bladder emptying question needs a different study. The long delay before imaging is the clue to a cortical agent.
  172. The correct answer is B (While tracer remains in the filled system, so the response can be judged.): The diuretic tests whether a full system is obstructed or simply baggy, and that can only be judged by watching tracer leave. If the tracer has already gone, there is nothing to measure. Giving the drug before injection changes the baseline, and waiting a day repeats the whole study without need. The still filled system in the stem is the cue.
  173. The correct answer is D (Tell the physician she has eaten, since the gallbladder may not fill, and rebook as advised.): A recent meal contracts the gallbladder, so it may not fill with tracer and the study can be wrongly read as a blocked duct. The physician decides whether to proceed or rebook. Proceeding looks reasonable because the liver would still be seen, but the gallbladder question would fail. Sincalide is a physician decision, and extra scanning does not make a contracted gallbladder fill.
  174. The correct answer is D (Morphine, which can help show a blocked cystic duct): Morphine tightens the sphincter at the bile duct outlet and raises pressure, which pushes tracer into the gallbladder if the cystic duct is open, so continued non filling suggests blockage. Sincalide is used to measure gallbladder contraction, not to test patency. Phenobarbital speeds bile flow in other settings, and furosemide belongs to renal studies.
  175. The correct answer is A (Do not substitute the meal; tell the physician the standard meal was not completed.): The result is compared with normal values for the standard meal, eaten fully and quickly, so a different or partial meal makes the numbers meaningless. The physician decides whether to rebook. Substituting a sandwich is tempting because it is also solid, but meals empty at different rates. Re-labelling food is not a validated method, and counting only the amount eaten has no reference range.
  176. The correct answer is C (The tracer stays in the blood, so a bleeding site shows as tracer in the bowel.): Labelled red cells stay inside the blood vessels, so tracer seen in the bowel and moving along it marks where blood has leaked. Because the label stays in the blood for a long time, repeat or continuous imaging can catch a slow bleed. The cells are not absorbed by the bowel or excreted by the liver.
  177. The correct answer is C (Delay the injection, tell the physician, and then follow the physician's plan for the study.): High glucose competes with FDG for uptake and lowers study quality, so the study is delayed and the physician decides what happens next. Insulin drives FDG into muscle and fat, so giving it without a plan can spoil the scan. A time limited slot is tempting pressure but does not justify injecting. More FDG does not undo competition from high glucose.
  178. The correct answer is B (Explain that the physician's protocol covers diabetes medicines, and refer the question to them.): Diabetes medicines and insulin timing are arranged under a protocol set by the physician, and a technologist never advises stopping or changing insulin on their own authority. Stopping insulin can be unsafe. Insulin near the injection pushes FDG into muscle and fat. Saying diabetes has no effect ignores the real reason the plan exists.
  179. The correct answer is A (Ask him to rest quietly without talking, chewing or walking, as muscles take FDG.): After injection FDG goes to any active muscle, so the patient should be still, quiet and warm with no chewing, talking or walking. Stopping only the gum leaves speech muscles in the larynx and jaw taking up tracer. Sweet tea lifts blood glucose, which competes with FDG. A cool room is tempting but cold brings shivering and brown fat uptake.
  180. The correct answer is B (Keeping the patient warm and resting in a quiet room during the whole uptake time): Cold activates brown fat, which takes up FDG and can mimic disease in the neck and shoulders. Keeping the patient warm lowers that uptake. Shortening the uptake changes tumour contrast, and longer fasting does not switch brown fat off. Not talking or chewing reduces muscle uptake in the neck, which is a different cause.
  181. The correct answer is C (Stop the injection, document the extravasation, and report it to the physician.): Swelling and burning during injection indicate extravasation, which lowers the dose delivered and affects quantification. The injection stops, the event is documented and the physician is told so they can decide on the next step. An easy flush earlier is the tempting detail but does not guarantee the cannula is still in the vein. Continuing would waste dose.
  182. The correct answer is D (Anatomical detail and the data for attenuation correction of PET images): The CT supplies anatomical localisation and the attenuation map that corrects the PET data. The 511 keV photon pairs come from the positron annihilation and are detected by the PET part. Localisation without the attenuation map is incomplete, and uptake values come from the corrected PET counts, not from the CT slices.
  183. The correct answer is A (Patient movement between the PET and CT acquisitions misaligned the two sets): Coughing or breathing changes between the two acquisitions shift the organs, so the PET and CT no longer line up. A fixed gantry offset would move everything equally, not displace the diaphragm differently. Reconstruction scaling and partial volume loss alter measured intensity, not where the uptake lies.
  184. The correct answer is B (These organs normally take up the ligand, so this pattern is expected.): PSMA ligands normally accumulate in the salivary glands, kidneys, liver and gut, and physicians know this pattern when they report. Reading it as spread would frighten the patient wrongly. The activity relates to weight protocols and does not explain normal organ uptake. A full bladder affects urinary activity near the prostate, not salivary or liver uptake.
  185. The correct answer is C (Check the timing with the physician, as the medicine can reduce tracer uptake.): Somatostatin analogues act on the same receptors the tracer targets, so recent doses can lower tumour uptake. The physician decides the timing relative to the injection. The first idea is tempting because the drug is a hormone treatment, but it works at the receptor. Raising the activity adds dose without restoring binding, and the study is often still possible with correct timing.
  186. The correct answer is A (Accurate injection at the correct site and good surface marking of the node): The sentinel node is the first node to drain the tumour site, so the injection position and a clear skin mark are what guide the surgeon's probe. Extra activity raises background and shine-through from the injection site rather than helping identification. A colloid that runs past the first node can light up second echelon nodes. SPECT/CT adds anatomical detail but does not replace a surface mark.
  187. The correct answer is C (Stop and verify each sample's identity before any labelled cells are returned.): Labelled cells are given back to the patient, so identification must be as strict as for a transfusion, and giving back another person's cells is a serious and dangerous mistake. A smudged label means the owner is uncertain. A colleague's guess, volume comparison or relabelling from the request all skip verification of the actual sample.
  188. The correct answer is D (Some drugs interfere with the transporter, which can change the study result): Drugs that bind the dopamine transporter can lower or alter ioflupane binding, so medicines are reviewed beforehand and the physician decides what to do. Thyroid blockade is a separate preparation step. The tracer dose does not depend on medicine doses, and the review is about binding, not about controlling movement.
  189. The correct answer is D (Use the protocol's minimum activity, since the image must still be diagnostic.): Paediatric activity is scaled by weight under a recognised protocol, but with a minimum below which images are not diagnostic. A repeat scan because of a poor image would mean more radiation overall. Lower values, adult steps or longer scanning each depart from the minimum that the protocol sets for the diagnostic image.
  190. The correct answer is B (Explain in age suitable words, involve the mother, keep him warm, and try again calmly.): A frightened child usually settles when the explanation suits his age, his mother is involved and he is kept warm and calm. Having the mother lie beside him helps, but starting without explaining leaves the fear unaddressed. Sedation is a medical decision and is premature before simple measures have been tried. Sending the mother out removes the person he trusts most.
  191. The correct answer is C (Lu-177 PSMA therapy given to treat advanced prostate cancer in selected patients): Lu-177 PSMA targets prostate specific membrane antigen and treats advanced prostate cancer. I-131 is for hyperthyroidism and thyroid cancer, Lu-177 DOTATATE is for neuroendocrine tumours, and Y-90 is injected into a joint to treat inflamed lining.
  192. The correct answer is B (Hold the treatment, check pregnancy status, and let the physician decide.): Pregnancy status must be checked carefully before therapy, with a test where appropriate, and the physician decides what happens next. An earlier consent discussion does not replace a current check. A lower dose does not remove fetal risk from radioiodine, and simply rebooking without testing leaves the question unanswered.
  193. The correct answer is A (Fit the written advice to his home: distance, sleeping alone, toilets and transport.): Release instructions are written and clear but fitted to the patient, so a small shared flat with a baby needs a different plan from someone living alone. The advice covers distance from others, sleeping alone, toilet and hand hygiene, and avoiding public transport. Advice that targets one person, ignores the household or relies on later questions leaves gaps.
  194. The correct answer is D (Activity, home setting, and the state or territory regulator's requirements): The decision depends on how much activity is given, whether the home can keep other people safe, and what the regulator requires, which differs among states and territories. ARPANSA guidance and the licence conditions set the process. Diagnosis alone, cost and willingness to follow instructions cannot replace a dose and home assessment.
  195. The correct answer is B (Warn others, wear gloves, contain with absorbent material, clean outside in, then survey.): The calm sequence is to warn others, wear gloves, contain with absorbent material, clean from the outside inward so the area of contamination shrinks, survey with a monitor and record it. Wiping outwards spreads the spill, and flushing sends activity into the drain. Covering it and leaving it still leaves a contaminated bench.
  196. The correct answer is D (The radiation safety officer, who directs decontamination and decides on any regulator report): Serious contamination is reported to the radiation safety officer, who decides on decontamination, surveys and any report to the regulator. The infection control nurse is tempting because cleaning is involved, but lacks radiation authority. The engineering supervisor cares for equipment, not contamination control. Notifying the regulator first skips the local chain, and the officer makes that report when needed.
  197. The correct answer is B (Do not use the camera until it is fixed, and report the fault.): A failed daily quality check means the camera is not used until it is fixed, because the fault can produce false hot or cold areas. Scanning and asking the physician to ignore it passes the risk to the report. Checking at the end of the day is too late. More counts cannot correct a uniformity problem.
  198. The correct answer is A (An incorrect uptake time, found once patient and dose were confirmed.): The troubleshooting order is patient, radiopharmaceutical, time, equipment and technique. Patient and dose were already confirmed, and the stem shows the scan started too early, so timing is the cause. An energy window, collimator or acquisition time setting would be found by checks or protocols, and would not explain a start 20 minutes before the uptake time.
  199. The correct answer is A (Stay with him, call for help, lie him down, and follow the local plan.): A faint patient needs someone to stay with them, help summoned, and a lying position that restores blood flow to the brain, all under the local emergency plan. Calling a physician is sensible but keeping him seated can make fainting worse. Imaging first ignores his condition. Ringing the referrer and arranging transport are later steps and leave him without immediate clinical care.
  200. The correct answer is B (Stop the injection, call the team, and protect the airway): Hives with facial swelling and breathing difficulty point to a severe allergic reaction, so the cause must stop entering the patient and help and airway protection come first. Slowing the injection still delivers more of the trigger, and watching for a few minutes risks the airway closing. Finishing the dose to save the study puts a wasted scan ahead of patient safety.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The bending magnet, which turns the electron beam and selects its energy): In beam order the electrons leave the waveguide, are turned through a large angle and energy selected by the bending magnet, and only then strike the target. The target comes after the turn because it is where photons are made. The flattening filter and the multileaf collimator act on the photon beam, so both sit further downstream.
  2. The correct answer is C (Bremsstrahlung, where fast electrons are suddenly slowed in a tungsten target): Megavoltage photons come from bremsstrahlung, the radiation given off when fast electrons decelerate in the heavy metal target. Characteristic emission adds only discrete lines to the spectrum and is not the main mechanism. Thermionic emission describes how electrons are freed from the filament, which happens before the target is struck, and annihilation does not drive the linac beam.
  3. The correct answer is D (At the point where the machine axes meet, not on the skin): Field size is stated at the isocentre, the point where the machine rotation axes meet, 100 cm from the source. The skin surface closer to the source receives a geometrically smaller field, so the smaller light field is expected and not a fault. The jaw opening is a different, smaller size, and the depth of maximum dose is a calibration depth rather than the reference plane for stated field size.
  4. The correct answer is A (A higher dose rate and a peaked profile across the whole field): Removing the flattening filter lets more photons through, so the dose rate rises and the profile is peaked toward the centre. A flatter profile is what the filter was there to produce. Skin sparing depends on photon energy and build-up, not on the filter being absent.
  5. The correct answer is D (A higher energy photon beam such as 15 MV, which penetrates deeper tissue well): Higher energy photons are more penetrating, giving better dose at depth and less dose to tissue on the way in. Electrons stop within a few centimetres and kilovoltage beams are highest at the surface, so neither reaches a deep pelvic target well. The 6 MV beam has the shallower dmax, so the stated reason is also wrong.
  6. The correct answer is C (Hold treatment and obtain the bolus, so the scar is not under-dosed): Megavoltage photons spare the skin, so a target at the surface needs bolus to move the build-up into the bolus material. Without it the scar receives too little dose, so treatment should wait until the bolus is available. An improvised towel is not the approved material or thickness, and changing energy is a plan change the therapist cannot make alone. Raising to 15 MV would give an even lower surface dose.
  7. The correct answer is C (Raising the photon energy, for example from 6 MV to 15 MV): Percentage depth dose at a fixed depth rises with higher photon energy because the beam is more penetrating. Using a smaller field and a shorter source to surface distance both lower it. Electrons of similar nominal energy fall off rapidly at 10 cm, so that change would reduce the dose there instead.
  8. The correct answer is B (Roughly 0.69 of the dose rate that was intended at 100 cm): By the inverse square law the relative dose rate is (100 ÷ 120)². That is 10,000 ÷ 14,400 = 0.694. The 0.83 figure is the unsquared ratio, 1.44 is the inverted square, and 0.92 comes from a square root, so only 0.69 follows the law.
  9. The correct answer is D (250 MU delivered through this single anterior field): Dose needed at dmax is 180 ÷ 0.70 = 257.1 cGy. Dividing by the field size factor gives 257.1 ÷ 1.03 = 249.6, so 250 MU. Check: 250 × 1.03 × 0.70 = 180.3 cGy. Leaving out the factor gives 257, multiplying by it gives 265, and multiplying the dose by 0.70 gives 126.
  10. The correct answer is B (244 MU, with the dose divided by both the depth dose and the factor): The dose at dmax is 160 ÷ 0.67 = 238.8 cGy. Dividing by the field size factor gives 238.8 ÷ 0.98 = 243.7, so 244 MU. Check: 244 × 0.98 × 0.67 = 160.2 cGy. The other results come from omitting the factor, multiplying by it, or multiplying by the depth dose.
  11. The correct answer is C (130 MU for each field, so that each field adds 100 cGy at mid depth): Each field must give 100 cGy at 10 cm. The dose needed at dmax is 100 ÷ 0.77 = 129.9, so 130 MU, and the field size factor of 1.00 changes nothing. Using 260 MU would deliver the full prescription through each field and double the dose, while 77 and 100 come from multiplying by the depth dose or ignoring it.
  12. The correct answer is A (It rose by about 11 percent compared with the plan): The relative dose rate is (100 ÷ 95)² = 10,000 ÷ 9,025 = 1.108, so about 11 percent higher. The patient was closer to the source, so the dose rate rose, not fell. Using the unsquared ratio gives only about 5 percent, and squaring the ratio the wrong way round gives a fall of about 10 percent. The event should also be reported through the incident system.
  13. The correct answer is A (It compares doses at two depths at one point, so SSD is irrelevant): The tissue phantom ratio compares the dose at a depth with the dose at the same point at a reference depth, so it is independent of the surface distance. Percentage depth dose is the quantity that rises with source to surface distance. Field size still affects the dose, so a correction remains.
  14. The correct answer is C (About 1.5 cm for 6 MV and about 3 cm for 15 MV): The build-up region deepens as photon energy rises, so dmax is about 1.5 cm at 6 MV, about 2.5 cm at 10 MV and about 3 cm at 15 MV. Reversing the pairing contradicts that trend. Field size has only a small effect on dmax, so one common value is wrong.
  15. The correct answer is B (12 MeV electrons, which treat to about 3 to 4 cm): The 90 percent depth for 12 MeV is about 12 ÷ 3 to 12 ÷ 4, or 3 to 4 cm, which covers an edge at 3.5 cm and then falls steeply. At 9 MeV the cover ends near 2 to 3 cm, so the deep edge is under-dosed. At 16 MeV the cover reaches 4 to 5 cm and sends more dose into lung.
  16. The correct answer is D (About 8 cm of tissue before the dose falls close to zero): Practical range is about 16 ÷ 2 = 8 cm. Dividing by 4 gives 4 cm, which is the 90 percent depth rule and not the range. Using 16 cm treats MeV as centimetres, and about 5 cm is the 90 percent depth, which is shallower than the range.
  17. The correct answer is C (Bone absorbs kilovoltage beams much more, so bone can receive a higher dose): At kilovoltage energies absorption depends strongly on atomic number, so bone takes up much more of the beam than soft tissue and can receive a higher dose. The assumption of equal absorption holds roughly at megavoltage energies. Kilovoltage beams also have no build-up region, as the dose is highest at the surface.
  18. The correct answer is B (The superficial X-ray unit, because dose is highest at the surface): Superficial X-rays deliver their highest dose at the surface and fall away quickly, which suits a lesion only a few millimetres deep. Every megavoltage beam has a build-up region, so the surface receives less dose than tissue at depth. The 6 MV beam has the shallowest build-up of those beams but still under-doses the skin surface. The 10 MV and 15 MV beams are more penetrating, with the deepest build-up at 15 MV, so they suit this lesion least.
  19. The correct answer is D (Explain that leaf motion during the beam makes the uneven dose by design): In intensity modulated treatment the leaves move during delivery so that the dose across the field is non-uniform on purpose. The therapist should recognise this and confirm it matches the approved plan. Stopping for a filter fault, widening the jaws or resetting the leaves would all depart from the plan without cause.
  20. The correct answer is A (Do not treat, correct the wedge to match the plan, then recheck): A wedge tilts the isodose lines, so the wrong orientation gives a wrong dose distribution even when other parameters match. The set-up must be corrected to the approved plan and rechecked before the beam is switched on. Rotating the collimator does not restore the intended distribution, and reporting afterwards allows the error to occur.
  21. The correct answer is B (Refit the bolus flat against the skin, as an air gap lowers skin dose): Bolus moves the build-up into the bolus so the skin receives full dose, and that only works when it is in contact with the skin. An air gap reduces the dose near the skin, so refit the bolus and tell the team if it cannot be fixed. Extra monitor units or removing the bolus would both change the planned dose.
  22. The correct answer is A (Agreement of the two fields within about 2 mm, checked every month): The light field shows where the radiation field is, so the two must coincide within the local tolerance, usually about 2 mm. A 10 mm gap would be unsafe near critical organs. The laser check and the 3 percent output check are separate tests, not the field coincidence test.
  23. The correct answer is D (It becomes wider at depth, so the edge is less sharp): Penumbra widens with depth because of scatter in tissue and the finite size of the source. The field edge is therefore less sharp deep in the patient than at the skin. It is not confined to the surface build-up region.
  24. The correct answer is C (Stay out, use the emergency retract, check the monitor, call the safety officer): If the source does not return to the safe, do not enter and rush to the patient. Use the emergency retract or stop, check the room monitor and call the radiation safety officer and physicist. The forceps and shielded container are for use under the emergency procedure, and restarting or waiting only prolongs exposure.
  25. The correct answer is B (About 92.5 GBq, after two half-lives have passed): 148 days is 148 ÷ 74 = 2 half-lives. The activity halves twice: 370 → 185 → 92.5 GBq. The 185 figure is one half-life, 46 GBq would need three, and a quarter loss ignores the exponential pattern.
  26. The correct answer is C (Its activity halves about every 74 days, lengthening treatments): Iridium-192 decays with a half-life of about 74 days, so its activity falls steadily and dwell times would otherwise have to grow. Decay lowers the activity, not the photon energy. Source exchange is scheduled for this reason, not because of corrosion or a treatment count.
  27. The correct answer is B (Two people independently check the applicator, patient identity and plan): Applicators, patient identity and plan are checked by two people before the source is sent. Plan approval earlier does not remove the need for a second person to check on the day. The annual calibration is a machine test and never replaces the check before a patient is treated.
  28. The correct answer is C (The licence conditions and the radiation safety officer's advice): Written advice for a patient leaving with permanent seeds must follow the facility's licence conditions and the radiation safety officer's instructions, since these set the precautions that apply locally. Supplier guidance gives useful source data but does not set local release rules. National guidance underpins the licence but is not applied as printed. A surgeon's discharge letter is a medical record for the general practitioner and does not carry radiation precautions.
  29. The correct answer is A (To scan the patient in the exact treatment position for reproducibility): The simulation CT is taken with the patient in the treatment position on a flat couch top, with lasers and skin marks so the position can be found again each day. This reproducibility is the main purpose. Staging belongs to diagnostic imaging, treatment imaging is still needed on the machine, and the scan does not measure beam dose.
  30. The correct answer is D (Do not treat, and tell the senior therapist and oncologist so fit is reviewed): A mask that no longer fits because of weight loss is a reason to stop and refer for review, not to treat in a poorly fitting device. Padding or tighter straps change the position that was planned, and imaging does not repair the fit. Waiting for drift to appear exposes the patient to missed dose.
  31. The correct answer is D (Breath-hold or gating, so the beam is on only at one phase of breathing): Motion management for breathing includes breath-hold, gating and tracking, which restrict delivery to one part of the cycle. External immobilisation holds the body still, but the tumour still moves inside it. Weekly images and a daily cone beam scan show where the target is at one moment, so neither controls movement during each breath.
  32. The correct answer is B (Megavoltage portal images show bone well and soft tissue poorly): Electronic portal imaging uses the treatment beam, and megavoltage photons give little soft tissue contrast. Bone shows well while the bladder and rectum are barely seen, so the image is behaving normally. Soft tissue is better seen on cone beam CT.
  33. The correct answer is C (Pause and ask the oncologist or senior therapist to review the soft tissue): A good bone match does not prove the prostate is in the right place when the bladder has changed. The target may have moved with the bladder, so the soft tissue should be reviewed and, if outside the protocol, a senior decision made. Centring on the bladder or applying a half shift are both unapproved changes.
  34. The correct answer is A (Surface guided radiation therapy, using cameras on the skin surface): Surface guided radiation therapy uses cameras to follow the skin surface continuously and gives no extra radiation, which suits breast treatment and breath-hold techniques. Spirometry reports air volume, not where the chest surface sits. Lasers and marks are checked only before treatment starts, and cone beam CT adds dose and cannot run through delivery.
  35. The correct answer is B (Follow the protocol, as each image adds dose and needs justifying): Image guidance is justified by the plan and not routine extra dose, and each image adds a small dose. The protocol sets what imaging is needed for this treatment. The prescription cannot be changed to offset imaging, and omitting the required first-day image would remove a necessary check.
  36. The correct answer is A (Hold and have the shift approved by the person the protocol names): The usual sequence is to match, check the result against tolerance, move the couch for the shift and verify. When the shift exceeds the action level, the department protocol decides who approves it. Moving at once, ignoring the image or splitting the shift all depart from that protocol.
  37. The correct answer is A (Do not treat, report it, and wait for the physicist to clear the machine): If a daily check fails, do not treat. Report and record it and wait for the physicist to clear the machine, even when the patient is waiting. A repeat reading cannot clear the machine, and trimming monitor units hides a fault in the output instead of fixing it.
  38. The correct answer is D (A physicist performs this each year, using a national standard): Full calibration of the beam output against a national standard, with profiles and depth dose for every energy and modality, is done by a physicist each year. Daily checks test constancy only, and monthly checks cover items such as field agreement and lasers. The manufacturer commissions the machine, but calibration is repeated and owned by the department physicist.
  39. The correct answer is D (Stop, do not bypass it, and call the physicist to clear the fault): An interlock must never be bypassed to save time, and if it cannot be cleared by the normal steps the therapist stops and calls the physicist. A camera view does not prove the interlock circuit is sound. Repeated resets or door cycling cover up a fault that has not been identified.
  40. The correct answer is C (Stop the beam first, then enter the room and check on the patient): If the patient moves, calls out or the machine reports a fault, stop the beam first, then enter the room, and record the event in the incident system. Entering with the beam on would expose the therapist and the patient to the wrong dose. Waiting for the field to finish delays the response.
  41. The correct answer is B (Primary barrier takes the direct beam; secondary handles leakage and scatter): The primary barrier is thicker where the beam points directly. The secondary barrier handles leakage and scatter. The maze and door lower the scatter reaching the entrance, but they are not the direct beam barrier.
  42. The correct answer is C (Above about 10 MV neutrons are also produced, and they can reach the door): Above about 10 MV the beam also produces neutrons, so the door can need extra shielding even though the beam does not point at it. Photon scatter and leakage matter for all energies, but neutron production is what changes the door requirement at the higher energy.
  43. The correct answer is D (20 mSv a year averaged over five years, never above 50 mSv in one year): The occupational limit is 20 mSv per year averaged over five years, with no more than 50 mSv in any single year. Swapping the numbers reverses the rule. The public limit is 1 mSv per year, and the limit for staff is not a flat single year figure.
  44. The correct answer is A (Stop the porter, as only screened non-ferromagnetic items may enter): The MR-linac has a strong magnet that stays on, so no ferromagnetic items can enter the room and all patients, staff and equipment are screened. Beam off does not remove the magnetic field. Holding or strapping a steel cylinder cannot stop it being pulled toward the magnet.
  45. The correct answer is B (Dose peaks at a depth set by energy, the Bragg peak, with almost none beyond it): Protons deposit most of their dose at a depth set by their energy, the Bragg peak, and almost none beyond it, which spares tissue past the target. This matters for some paediatric tumours. A photon-like curve or a flat dose would not give that sparing, and protons do deposit some dose on the way in.
  46. The correct answer is D (MU is a machine reading, and dose depends on output, depth and factors): Monitor units are a reading from the ionisation chambers in the head. The machine is calibrated so that a set number of MU gives a known dose under reference conditions, and depth and field factors change the dose at depth. MU is neither a dose nor a time.
  47. The correct answer is A (Hold treatment and refer the discrepancy to the physicist before any beam is delivered): An independent second check exists to catch errors, so a large disagreement must be resolved before any dose is given. The therapist cannot know which number is wrong without the physicist. Choosing either figure or the mean could deliver an incorrect dose.
  48. The correct answer is D (22 Gy remaining in 11 fractions, which is equal to 2200 cGy): Each fraction is 60 ÷ 30 = 2 Gy. After 19 fractions the dose delivered is 38 Gy, so 60 − 38 = 22 Gy remains over 30 − 19 = 11 fractions. Since 1 Gy equals 100 cGy, that is 2200 cGy.
  49. The correct answer is A (Reset the board to the recorded index and recheck the position before imaging): Immobilisation devices are indexed to the couch and the settings are recorded so that the position is repeatable. A different index changes the arm and thorax position even if the marks seem to line up. Reset to the recorded setting rather than changing the record or the patient.
  50. The correct answer is C (Cone beam CT, which shows soft tissue better than planar images): Cone beam CT makes a three dimensional image on the couch and shows soft tissue better than planar images. Portal images show soft tissue poorly and kilovoltage planar images show bony anatomy and markers. Surface cameras track the skin only and cannot show where an internal target lies.
  51. The correct answer is B (Palliative intent, aiming to relieve symptoms using lower doses over fewer visits): The stem states the aim is pain relief with few visits and a lower dose, which defines palliative treatment. Radical treatment uses higher doses to cure and is tempting only because bone metastases are still cancer. Adjuvant treatment follows another primary treatment and is not described here. A frail patient on a cure pathway would still have curative aims, which this plan does not have.
  52. The correct answer is C (Stop, tell the treating team, and arrange a refitted mask or repeat simulation): A loose mask means the position is no longer reproducible, so dose may fall in the wrong place. Matching tattoos does not prove the head and neck are in the planned position inside the mask. Forcing straps can mislead the image match and may harm the patient. Delaying the report lets several fractions go out with a known set-up fault.
  53. The correct answer is C (The visible tumour plus a margin for microscopic spread): The clinical target volume adds a margin for microscopic spread to the gross tumour volume. The gross volume is only what is visible. The internal target volume adds motion allowance, and the planning target volume adds set-up uncertainty. Each is a later step in the nested set, so each is tempting when the letters are confused.
  54. The correct answer is C (A planning target volume, built on the clinical target volume for set-up error): A margin for set-up error and delivery uncertainty defines the planning target volume. The internal target volume covers internal motion such as breathing, which the stem excludes. The planning organ at risk volume surrounds a normal organ, not the target. The gross volume is the visible tumour and carries no margin.
  55. The correct answer is B (An internal target volume allowing for motion of the target inside the body): Breathing moves the target inside the patient, and the internal target volume exists to cover that movement. The couch shift detail concerns set-up, which is a separate uncertainty handled by the planning target volume. The planning organ at risk volume protects normal tissue. Redrawing the gross volume would blur the definition and mix two different uncertainties.
  56. The correct answer is A (A planning organ at risk volume around the cord with a margin): The planning organ at risk volume adds a margin around a serial organ such as the cord, because a small overdose can cause serious harm. The plain contoured organ lacks that margin, which is the step the planner needs. The planning target volume belongs to the target series, and the treated volume describes where the prescribed dose lies, not a safety margin.
  57. The correct answer is D (A systematic error shifting the whole course one way, the more dangerous kind): The same shift on every fraction from a wrong simulation mark is a systematic error. It moves the whole dose distribution one way and does not average out, so it is the more dangerous kind. Random errors vary day to day and mainly blur the dose. The stem's within-spread image matching detail does not change that classification.
  58. The correct answer is D (Margins can be made smaller, which puts less normal tissue in the high dose region): Image guidance lets the team measure and correct the position, so less margin is needed for set-up error around the planning target volume. Smaller margins put less normal tissue in the high dose region. Some margin always remains for residual and random error, so it cannot fall to zero, and the clinical target volume is defined by disease, not by set-up accuracy.
  59. The correct answer is C (Volumetric-modulated arc therapy, usually faster than fixed-field IMRT): Modulated delivery during gantry rotation is volumetric-modulated arc therapy, and it is usually faster than fixed-field IMRT. Three-dimensional conformal therapy has no modulation. Stereotactic treatment is defined by dose and fractions, not by arcs alone. Two-dimensional planning uses plain images, not CT-based dose calculation.
  60. The correct answer is A (With so few fractions, any single error becomes a large share of the whole treatment): With only three fractions, one mis-set fraction affects a large share of the total, so set-up checks and image guidance are made stricter. Dose per fraction is high, but that alone does not mean a physicist must watch every beam. No repeat planning scan is required each time, and beam energy is not the reason for the extra checks.
  61. The correct answer is B (Most of the target receives the prescribed dose, so coverage is good): On a dose-volume histogram the vertical axis shows the percentage of volume receiving at least that dose. The target curve that stays high until the prescription dose and then drops sharply shows that most of the target is covered, with little spread above it. The steep fall is the ideal shape for a target, while a gradual fall would mean many parts receive different doses.
  62. The correct answer is B (Maximum point dose for the serial cord, and moderate-dose volume for the parallel lung): A serial organ such as the cord fails if a small part is overdosed, so the maximum point dose matters most. A parallel organ such as lung can lose part of its function and keep working, so the volume receiving a moderate dose matters most. Applying one measure to both ignores how the organs behave.
  63. The correct answer is D (It breaches a serial organ limit, so the plan goes back for review): For a serial organ, a small overdosed part can cause serious harm, so the maximum point dose matters most. A small volume above the limit is therefore not reassuring. Good coverage does not cancel a breached cord constraint, and the cord is serial, not parallel. The plan goes back for review before approval.
  64. The correct answer is A (The dose per fraction, plus the technique and the dose specification point or volume): The sheet already has site, total dose, number of fractions and energy, so it is missing dose per fraction, technique and the dose specification point or volume. Set-up notes, clinical history and plan evaluation details are useful in the record but do not complete the prescription itself.
  65. The correct answer is C (Hold the start until an authorised oncologist has approved the plan): A plan is clinically acceptable only when the target is covered, the constraints are met and the oncologist has approved it. Physics sign-off and planner records are different checks. Approving after the first fraction means unapproved treatment already reached the patient, so the start is held until approval.
  66. The correct answer is D (Between treatments, healthy tissue repairs itself better than tumour, so splitting spares normal tissue): Fractionation is biological: healthy tissue repairs between fractions better than tumour, which spares normal tissue. The reverse statement is tempting but wrong. Machine cooling plays no part, and splitting the dose usually requires a higher total dose, not a lower one.
  67. The correct answer is A (62.5 Gy, worked out as 50 Gy × (1 + 0.25)): The total dose is 20 × 2.5 = 50 Gy. The ratio d ÷ alpha/beta is 2.5 ÷ 10 = 0.25, so the bracket is 1.25 and BED = 50 × 1.25 = 62.5 Gy. Dropping the 1 gives 12.5 Gy, and stopping at the physical total gives 50 Gy. Using alpha/beta 3 Gy gives about 91.7 Gy, a value that suits late responding tissue and not this tumour.
  68. The correct answer is A (90 Gy, worked out as 45 × (1 + 3 ÷ 3)): The total dose is 15 × 3 = 45 Gy. With an alpha/beta of 3 Gy the ratio is 3 ÷ 3 = 1, so the bracket is 1 + 1 = 2 and BED = 45 × 2 = 90 Gy. Using 10 Gy for the ratio gives a bracket of 1.3 and 58.5 Gy, which suits tumour rather than late tissue. Omitting the bracket gives the physical dose of 45 Gy.
  69. The correct answer is B (Late-reacting tissues have a low alpha/beta ratio and are sensitive to fraction size): Late-reacting normal tissues such as spinal cord have a low alpha/beta ratio and are more sensitive to larger fractions, so late-effect risk rises faster than tumour effect and the total dose must fall. Most tumours and early-reacting tissues have high ratios and are less sensitive to fraction size.
  70. The correct answer is C (Larger doses per fraction than conventional, given in fewer fractions): Hypofractionation means larger fractions and fewer of them, giving a shorter course, as in many breast and prostate treatments and in palliation. About 1.8 to 2 Gy daily is conventional. Two fractions in one day is a different schedule, and a single large dose to a tiny target describes stereotactic radiosurgery.
  71. The correct answer is D (Ask the patient to state three identifiers, such as name, date of birth and record number): Identification uses at least three identifiers, asked of the patient where they can answer. Reading out a name and asking for a yes invites an automatic nod and uses only one identifier. Familiarity is not a check, and tattoos or couch position say nothing about whether this is the right patient.
  72. The correct answer is B (Ask the patient for three identifiers, then match them to the plan): Same surnames are a recognised risk, so identity is checked with three identifiers asked of the patient and matched to the plan before loading. A first image after loading is too late. A patient's agreement to a displayed plan is weak, and appointment order does not prove who is in the room.
  73. The correct answer is A (A pause where the team confirms patient, site, technique and special instructions aloud before treatment): The time-out is a short pause before treatment where the team confirms the patient, plan, site, technique and special instructions such as bolus or a cardiac device, aloud. Machine output checks belong to quality assurance, and image review happens after treatment has started. Confirming identity is part of a time-out, but it is only one item.
  74. The correct answer is C (Stop, find out why the warning appeared, and involve senior staff first): A warning means the machine does not match the approved plan, and overriding it without understanding it is a recognised way for errors to reach patients. Lateness does not change that. Checking afterwards is too late, and altering settings to clear a warning hides the cause, which should be found with senior staff.
  75. The correct answer is D (Apply the couch correction, then do the time-out): When the offset is within the action level, the practitioner applies the couch correction and proceeds to the time-out and delivery. Ignoring a measured offset wastes the purpose of imaging. Repeated scans add dose and chase noise, and escalation is needed for shifts beyond the action level, not within it.
  76. The correct answer is B (Do not apply it yet: recheck the set-up and match, and involve senior staff): A shift far beyond the action level should not simply be applied. The practitioner stops, rechecks the patient position, the set-up and the match, and involves senior staff or the oncologist as protocol says. A clean-looking match does not remove that step, and partial or informally agreed corrections are not protocol options.
  77. The correct answer is D (Raise it with the team, since a new scan and plan may be needed): Weight loss and a repeated shift are reasons for adaptive radiotherapy. The therapist notices and raises it, because a new scan and a new plan may be needed. Console margin changes are outside the therapist's role, and waiting until the end lets the dose drift for the whole course.
