  78. The correct answer is B (36 Gy remains, to be given over 18 fractions): The dose per fraction is 60 ÷ 30 = 2 Gy. After 12 fractions the delivered dose is 12 × 2 = 24 Gy. Remaining dose is 60 minus 24 = 36 Gy, and remaining fractions are 30 minus 12 = 18. The other options subtract the wrong quantity or report the delivered dose as remaining.
  79. The correct answer is A (Record the missed fractions and refer the decision on making up the dose to the oncologist): Missed fractions matter because tumour cells can multiply faster during a pause. How to make the dose up is the oncologist's decision, so the therapist records the missed fractions and refers. Treating twice in one day may be chosen by the oncologist, but not by the therapist alone. Adding fractions first and telling the oncologist later, or leaving it to the dosimetrist, bypasses the person responsible.
  80. The correct answer is C (Pause and tell the oncologist and radiation safety officer before going ahead): If the patient could be pregnant, the oncologist and the radiation safety officer are told before simulation or treatment goes ahead, because the foetus is very sensitive to radiation. The decision to treat, change or delay is medical, with radiation safety input, and it is never made at the console.
  81. The correct answer is A (Delay simulation and inform the oncologist and radiation safety officer under the department's pregnancy procedure): Because she could be pregnant despite the earlier negative test, the procedure is followed: oncologist and radiation safety officer are told before simulation. The decision to proceed, change or delay is medical with radiation safety input. A signed consent does not replace that process.
  82. The correct answer is C (Record the device and tell the team so local protocol handles it in the plan): Cardiac implantable electronic devices can be damaged by radiation and by the electrical effects of treatment machines, so they are identified at consultation. The device is considered in the plan, and a limit is set by local protocol with advice from cardiology or the device clinic. Waiting until treatment day is too late.
  83. The correct answer is D (Hold treatment until the oncologist has reviewed the plan with the device in mind): If a device is discovered that no one knew about, treatment waits until the oncologist has reviewed the plan. High energy beams that create neutrons are a particular concern, and the energy choice is reviewed by the team, not altered at the console. Waiting for symptoms is not a safeguard.
  84. The correct answer is A (Regular checks of accelerator output, beam symmetry, leaves, lasers and couch): Machine QA covers the equipment itself: accelerator output, beam symmetry, the multileaf collimator, lasers, couch and imaging system, checked on a schedule. Loading the right plan, calculating monitor units for one plan and fitting a mask are all checks on a single patient, so none of them tests the machine.
  85. The correct answer is B (Plan QA, confirming the measured dose agrees with the calculated dose within agreed limits): Delivering a complex plan to a measuring device and comparing it with the calculation is plan QA, which checks that the plan is correct and deliverable. Machine QA checks the equipment on a schedule, patient QA covers daily and weekly checks, and an image match does not test calculated dose.
  86. The correct answer is D (An incident, since the error reached the patient and could have caused harm): A near miss is an error caught before it reaches the patient. Here the treatment was delivered with the wrong bolus, so the error reached the patient, which makes it an incident. Both near misses and incidents are reported, and neither the small dose difference nor detection by a routine check removes that duty.
  87. The correct answer is B (Make the patient safe, tell the senior person, record the facts, and join open disclosure): The expected order is to make the patient safe, tell the senior person on duty, record the facts in the incident system, and take part in open disclosure led by a senior clinician. Fixing the error quietly is a breach of professional and ethical duty, and waiting for symptoms delays a required report.
  88. The correct answer is B (Honest and timely communication with the patient about what happened, led by a senior clinician): Open disclosure is a prompt, honest conversation with the patient about what happened, led by an appropriately senior clinician under the national framework. Having the clinician directly involved lead it is tempting but does not follow the framework. Regulator and insurer reports are separate duties that do not replace speaking with the patient. A letter alone lacks the discussion and support the patient needs.
  89. The correct answer is C (Notification of the state or territory radiation regulator, as local law requires): Significant radiation incidents must also be notified to the radiation regulator of the state or territory, and that regulator differs between states. Internal recording is necessary but does not meet that duty. The relevant regulator is the one where the incident occurred, not the manufacturer's.
  90. The correct answer is D (Tell the oncologist now, as this may be cord compression): Sudden loss of movement in the legs in a patient with spinal disease can mean spinal cord compression, which is an emergency, so the oncologist must be told at once. Fatigue is common and expected, but this change is not. Waiting for weekly review or treating first risks permanent loss of function.
  91. The correct answer is A (Blistering at the treated site, with size, date first seen and pain level): A useful handover states what was seen, where, how large it is, when it first appeared and how much it hurts, so the oncologist can decide. Calling blistering an expected reaction hides a finding that needs same day review. The patient's opinion on cause is not a clinical observation. Dose delivered is true and relevant to the plan, but it does not describe the skin finding itself.
  92. The correct answer is C (Pause, settle the patient, and tell the team positioning is not reproducible): If positioning is no longer reproducible, treatment is paused and the team is told, since dose may go to the wrong place regardless of how good the plan is. Holding the head during a beam is unsafe, and changing margins is a planning decision, not something done at the console.
  93. The correct answer is A (Stop, review the match on the target, and follow the protocol before applying the shift): SBRT uses very few fractions, so errors are a large share of the course and checks are stricter. A 4 mm target offset is beyond the 2 mm action level, so the therapist stops, rechecks the match on the target and follows the protocol, involving senior staff or the oncologist if required.
  94. The correct answer is B (A mean dose limit plus a limit on volume at higher doses): The heart is normally constrained by a mean dose limit together with a limit on the volume receiving higher doses. A point dose limit suits serial organs such as the cord, so it is a tempting but wrong pairing here. Coverage rules such as the 95 percent volume belong to targets, not organs at risk. A low dose volume limit alone omits the mean dose limit that the heart needs.
  95. The correct answer is D (The volume of lung receiving a moderate dose, since lung is a parallel organ): Lung is a parallel organ, so the volume receiving a moderate dose predicts function loss better than a small hot spot. Treating lung like a serial organ and watching the maximum point dose is tempting but misleading here. Cord tolerance is a separate constraint and does not replace the lung measure. The treated lung still has dose constraints, so it cannot be written off.
  96. The correct answer is B (Review the beam energy choice, as high energy beams that create neutrons are a concern): High energy beams create neutrons, which can disturb cardiac implantable devices even when the device is outside the field, so the energy choice is reviewed. Limiting the review to devices inside the field misses scattered neutrons. A device dose limit and tumour depth matter but do not replace this review. Choosing the highest energy for speed ignores the concern, and cardiology should be involved before treatment.
  97. The correct answer is C (Tell the team the target moves, so the plan allows for motion with an ITV): Target movement with breathing is covered by an internal target volume, so the team must be told that the target moves. Treating the blur as the visible tumour mixes tumour and motion, and daily imaging is not a substitute for planning for motion. A margin on the organ at risk does not protect the target.
  98. The correct answer is D (Obtain the recorded device and set the patient up exactly as the notes say): The immobilisation devices chosen at simulation are recorded so that the patient lies in the same position every day. A missing device is replaced with the recorded one, not a stand-in, because reproducibility is the aim. Lasers and marks do not confirm that the position matches the plan.
  99. The correct answer is A (Hold treatment and check the dose records with senior staff before any further beam): A mismatch between recorded and expected dose could mean a missed record or an overdose, and it needs to be understood before more dose is given. The therapist holds treatment and checks with senior staff. Editing the entry to fit her count hides the cause, and delivering first risks compounding an error.
  100. The correct answer is A (The PTV is a geometric margin, so the CTV can still be missed on bad days): The PTV is a geometric margin that gives a high chance of covering the CTV, but larger daily errors can still miss it, so daily imaging remains useful. Margins reduce the effect of random and systematic error but do not remove either. A margin set from the first scan cannot cover later changes such as weight loss or organ filling. Seeing the PTV edge alone does not confirm the CTV position.
  101. The correct answer is C (Whether cancer cells are present in the regional lymph nodes near the primary): The N in TNM describes regional lymph node involvement, and a higher number means more extensive nodal disease. The size of the primary belongs to T, distant deposits belong to M, and the grouped stage is worked out from all three values together. Each of those is tempting because they sit in the same summary line.
  102. The correct answer is A (Regional disease, because the cancer has reached nearby lymph nodes only): A tumour that has reached nearby lymph nodes is called regional, while metastatic is reserved for deposits in distant organs. Localised would only fit if nothing had left the primary site. The scan findings of no liver, lung or bone deposits rule out the metastatic label, and the nodal spread rules out stage zero.
  103. The correct answer is C (Say the aim is easing his pain, which takes weeks, and offer a team talk): He asked a direct question, so the honest and kind answer is that palliative treatment aims to relieve pain, that relief can take a couple of weeks, and that the oncology team can discuss the wider plan. Deferring the question leaves a frightened man without an answer, and false hope damages trust. A blunt answer that ends the conversation is honest but not caring.
  104. The correct answer is D (Double strand breaks in DNA, which cells find very hard to repair accurately): Double strand breaks are the hardest DNA damage to repair accurately and the most likely to kill a cell when it tries to divide. Single strand breaks and base damage are real DNA lesions, which makes them tempting, but cells usually repair them accurately. Mitochondrial damage is not the main route to radiation cell kill.
  105. The correct answer is B (Indirect action, where water splits to form free radicals that then damage DNA): Most DNA damage comes from indirect action: radiation splits water close to the DNA and the free radicals formed damage it. Oxygen helps fix that damage, but it does not stop repair enzymes. Radicals formed in the membrane are too far away to reach DNA. Direct ionisation of DNA does occur, which makes it tempting, but it is the less common route.
  106. The correct answer is B (Lining cells divide fast and show damage early, whereas slow dividing cord cells show it late): Fast dividing tissues such as bladder lining show injury early, while slowly dividing tissues such as spinal cord show it months or years later. The timing follows cell turnover. Oxygenation affects radiosensitivity but does not set the timing. Dose per fraction is chosen for the whole plan and does not delay injury, and a higher tolerance dose does not explain delayed appearance.
  107. The correct answer is C (The gap between the dose controlling the tumour and the dose causing unacceptable normal tissue damage): The therapeutic ratio compares the dose that controls the tumour with the dose at which normal tissue damage becomes unacceptable. Techniques such as immobilisation and image guidance widen that gap. A homogeneity measure and a prescribed dose gap are real concepts but not this one. Restricting complications to acute skin reactions narrows the idea too far.
  108. The correct answer is A (Tolerance depends on dose per fraction as well as on total dose and volume treated): A tolerance dose depends on the volume treated, the dose per fraction and the total dose, so the same total delivered in larger fractions can be more damaging to slowly dividing tissue such as cord. Larger fractions give less benefit from repair, not more. Tumour hypoxia is unrelated to cord tolerance.
  109. The correct answer is B (Report it today, since low haemoglobin reduces oxygen delivery and may reduce tumour sensitivity): Well oxygenated cells are more sensitive to radiation, and anaemia reduces oxygen delivery, so a low haemoglobin is a reason to tell the team the same day. Withholding treatment is the oncologist's decision, not the therapist's. Putting the paleness down to normal fatigue risks missing a result that may change management.
  110. The correct answer is D (Roughly two and a half to three times harder than for well oxygenated cells): For low LET radiation such as X-rays, hypoxic cells need roughly two and a half to three times the dose of well oxygenated cells, because oxygen fixes free radical damage. The figure near one belongs to high LET radiation, which relies less on oxygen. A value of one and a half to two undershoots the accepted ratio. The oxygen effect is strong for X-rays and weak for neutrons, not the reverse.
  111. The correct answer is C (As outer cells die, hypoxic cells nearer the blood supply gain oxygen and become more sensitive): Reoxygenation happens when dying tumour cells are lost and the hypoxic cells in the poorly perfused centre end up nearer the blood supply, gain oxygen and become more sensitive. Reassortment is a different process involving the cell cycle. Repair mainly spares normal tissue, and repopulation works against the treatment.
  112. The correct answer is A (Surviving tumour cells keep multiplying during the break, so tumour control may fall): During a gap, surviving tumour cells repopulate, so a prolonged course or interruptions can reduce tumour control. Delivered dose is not lost, and a gap does not stop normal tissue repairing. This is why missed fractions must be reported so the oncologist can decide how to compensate.
  113. The correct answer is D (Reassortment, where survivors move into sensitive cell cycle phases and are hit harder next time): Reassortment, also called redistribution, means cells in sensitive phases are killed first and survivors move into sensitive phases, so the next fraction affects more of them. Reoxygenation concerns oxygen, repair concerns sublethal damage, and repopulation concerns cell multiplication. Each is one of the four Rs, which makes the others tempting.
  114. The correct answer is C (Splitting the dose lets late responding tissue repair while tumour cells reoxygenate): Fractionation spares late responding normal tissue because it gets time to repair between fractions, and it also exposes tumour cells to reoxygenation and reassortment. Repopulation actually protects normal tissue rather than harming it, and hypoxic cells are not killed more easily by small doses alone. The benefit also reaches deep tissues, not just the skin.
  115. The correct answer is D (Fewer, larger daily fractions, for example 40 Gy in 15 fractions for many breast cancers): Hypofractionation means fewer, larger fractions, and the chapter's example is 40 Gy in 15 fractions for many breast cancers. Stereotactic treatment goes further, with one to a few very high dose fractions to small targets. Conventional fractionation is 1.8 to 2 Gy once a day, five days a week.
  116. The correct answer is B (1.8 Gy per fraction, since 45 divided by 25 gives 1.8): Dose per fraction is the total dose divided by the number of fractions, so 45 ÷ 25 = 1.8 Gy. Check: 1.8 × 25 = 45 Gy. Dividing by five treatment days uses the wrong divisor. Using 20 fractions misreads the prescription, and dividing 25 by 45 inverts the calculation.
  117. The correct answer is D (Remaining dose is 38 Gy over the 19 fractions still to come): Dose per fraction is 66 ÷ 33 = 2 Gy. Delivered so far is 14 × 2 = 28 Gy, so remaining is 66 minus 28 = 38 Gy over 33 minus 14 = 19 fractions. Check: 19 × 2 = 38 Gy. Giving 28 Gy reports what has already been delivered, and 52 comes from subtracting 14 fractions from 66 Gy.
  118. The correct answer is A (About 50.7 Gy, worked out as 40 Gy × (1 + 2.67 ÷ 10)): With n × d = 40 Gy and an alpha beta of 10 Gy, BED = 40 × (1 + 2.667 ÷ 10) = 40 × 1.2667 = 50.7 Gy. Using an alpha beta of 3 Gy gives 75.6 Gy, which is the late responding tissue figure rather than the tumour. Leaving out the number of fractions gives about 3.4 Gy. BED equals the physical dose only when dose per fraction is negligible, which is not so for 2.67 Gy fractions.
  119. The correct answer is C (Late tissue BED rises from about 83 to 92 Gy, so total dose needs lowering): For late tissue with α/β of 3 Gy, 25 fractions of 2 Gy give 50 × (1 + 2 ÷ 3) = 83.3 Gy, while 20 fractions of 2.5 Gy give 50 × (1 + 2.5 ÷ 3) = 91.7 Gy. That is about a 10 percent rise. The tumour with α/β of 10 Gy rises only from 60 to 62.5 Gy, about 4 percent, so a hypofractionated schedule needs a lower total dose.
  120. The correct answer is B (Treat as scheduled, then report the interruption to the radiation oncologist to decide compensation): Missed or interrupted fractions allow tumour cells to repopulate, so the therapist reports the gap to the radiation oncologist, who decides how to compensate. Quietly adding an extra treatment or ignoring the gap are the common wrong choices. His illness was not caused by radiation, so a toxicity referral does not fit.
  121. The correct answer is A (Wash gently in lukewarm water with mild soap, pat dry, and use approved moisturisers): Advice is to wash gently with lukewarm water and mild soap, pat dry, and use only moisturisers the department approves. Gentle washing with her perfumed cream is half right but the cream may irritate reacting skin. Applying any pharmacy cream before treatment is also unsafe because products on the skin can change the dose and irritate. Adhesive dressings pull on reacting skin.
  122. The correct answer is C (Report the moist peeling today so the nurse can assess it and approve a dressing): Moist peeling in a skin fold is a significant acute reaction that needs assessment and a department-approved dressing, so it is reported the same day. Waiting for a weekly review or recommending talc leaves the skin unprotected. Reassurance is kind and partly true, because it will heal, but it does not meet her needs now.
  123. The correct answer is D (Tell the nurse or radiation oncologist the same day and request dietitian review): A 3 kg loss in a week with painful swallowing suggests mucositis, which needs a same day report and dietitian review because he may need a feeding tube. Documenting it for next week's review delays care. Supplements and softer foods are sensible advice but do not replace escalation. His wish to hurry does not change what must be reported.
  124. The correct answer is D (Dry mouth from reduced saliva when the salivary glands lie within the treated volume): Xerostomia is dry mouth caused by reduced saliva after the salivary glands receive dose. Mucositis is the sore ulcerated lining, altered taste is a separate effect, and painful swallowing is a consequence of mucositis. They often occur together, which is why they are easy to confuse.
  125. The correct answer is B (It keeps bladder size and position reproducible, so the treated volume matches the plan each day): A set bladder filling routine keeps the size and position of the bladder, and so the prostate region, close to the planning scan, so the treated volume matches the plan. Bowel displacement can occur but is not the main purpose. Breathing has little effect on the pelvis, and the routine does not prevent infection. Explaining the reason often helps patients put up with the discomfort.
  126. The correct answer is A (Report the diarrhoea today, because chemotherapy can strengthen reactions and she may need review): Some diarrhoea is expected, but six stools with cramping while having concurrent chemotherapy is a strengthened reaction, so it is reported the same day. The nurse's earlier reassurance does not cover a change since then. Deciding to hold treatment belongs to the oncologist.
  127. The correct answer is B (Fatigue, which builds over the weeks and is helped by rest and pacing): Fatigue is the most common side effect overall, builds over the weeks, and is helped by rest, gentle activity and pacing. Skin reactions, nausea and mucositis are common but depend on the treated site. The question asks for the most common effect across all patients.
  128. The correct answer is C (Report the new symptoms to the radiation oncologist or nurse promptly before treating): Immunotherapy can cause rash, bowel inflammation and breathlessness, and radiation can produce similar effects, so new symptoms are reported promptly rather than assumed to be routine. Three different new symptoms together need team assessment. Waiting for weekly or monthly reviews leaves a potentially serious problem unseen.
  129. The correct answer is D (Acute reactions appear during treatment or within about three months and usually heal): Acute reactions appear during treatment or within about three months, arise in fast dividing tissue such as skin, mucosa and bowel lining, and usually heal. Late effects arise months to years later in slowly dividing tissue and are often permanent. Mixing the permanence of late effects with acute timing is tempting but wrong. Acute reactions are not limited to the first week or to skin.
  130. The correct answer is A (Late responding tissue is very sensitive to fraction size, so its BED rises more than tumour): With larger fractions, the late tissue BED (α/β about 3 Gy) rises proportionally more than the tumour BED (α/β about 10 Gy), as shown by 20 percent against 8 percent in the chapter's example. This is why a hypofractionated schedule needs a lower total dose. Late tissue damage is often permanent, so the protection matters most.
  131. The correct answer is B (Involve a play specialist and parents, and use anaesthetic as the team decides, for safe immobilisation): Young children who cannot stay still need comforting immobilisation, a play specialist, parents or carers, and sometimes a general anaesthetic decided by the team. Avoiding anaesthetic altogether may leave a child unable to hold still. A tighter mask without sedation is neither safe nor comforting. Sedation is not given by therapists at the machine, so that plan falls outside their scope.
  132. The correct answer is C (Treat the smallest necessary volume, using the lowest effective dose): Because children's tissues are still growing and late effects can appear over a long life, the principle is the smallest necessary volume with the lowest effective dose. Covering a whole growth region adds normal tissue to the field. Adult volumes and doses scaled by weight ignore growth. Fewer, larger fractions raise the risk of late effects.
  133. The correct answer is A (Stop, keep him safe, and report to the radiation oncologist immediately): Band-like pain with leg weakness in a patient with bone metastases is a warning sign of spinal cord compression, an emergency because paralysis can become permanent. The therapist stops, keeps him safe and escalates at once. Treating first, waiting until the list ends, or waiting for a review all delay care.
  134. The correct answer is B (A lung cancer patient with new numbness in both feet and trouble passing urine overnight): New numbness and bladder changes suggest spinal cord compression, which needs immediate escalation because delay can cause permanent paralysis. The skin and mouth reactions are real toxicities that need a same-day report. Unchanged back ache with normal walking is the least urgent of the four.
  135. The correct answer is C (Hold positioning, keep him comfortable, and ask the team before lying him flat): Facial and arm swelling, distended neck veins and breathlessness on lying flat suggest superior vena cava obstruction. Lying flat may worsen symptoms, so positioning is paused and the treating team is asked first. Plans that rely on the usual position cannot be followed automatically.
  136. The correct answer is D (Move him carefully using the department's safe method with help, and report the new leg symptom): With spinal metastases and a new leg symptom, the therapist moves the patient carefully using the department's safe method with help and reports the neurological change at once. Pulling the sheet quickly risks further injury. Using equipment is sensible but waiting for a routine review delays care. Handing the move to a porter and recording the symptom afterwards delays the report.
  137. The correct answer is C (A single high activity source is driven from a shielded safe into applicators, then returns): In HDR treatment, a remote afterloader drives one high activity source out of a shielded safe along guide tubes into the applicators for a few minutes, then returns it. Staff leave the room and watch by camera and intercom. Low dose rate treatment is the one with sources that stay for long periods.
  138. The correct answer is D (Dose rate falls with the square of distance, so doubling distance gives about a quarter): Radiation from a small source spreads over an area that grows with the square of distance, so doubling the distance leaves about a quarter of the dose rate. A linear relationship would halve it, which is the common first guess. A square root gives a mild fall of about 70 percent, and a cube gives one eighth, which treats spreading as a volume. Both slips give the wrong factor for a point source.
  139. The correct answer is B (Press the emergency stop, then enter with a survey meter and use emergency equipment): A source that does not retract calls for the local emergency procedure: press the emergency stop, enter with a survey meter, and use the equipment in the room, which normally includes long forceps and a shielded container. Entering without the stop or without a meter exposes the therapist. Restarting the treatment ignores the fault.
  140. The correct answer is A (Yes, the source is back in the safe, she is not radioactive, so contact is fine): With HDR brachytherapy the source returns to the shielded afterloader safe, so the patient carries no radioactive material and contact is safe. Precautions for permanent seed implants or unsealed radionuclide therapy are tempting, but they do not apply when no source stays in the body. The room survey confirming the source is in the safe is the evidence the partner needs, and a calm explanation is the right response to his fear.
  141. The correct answer is B (Check identity, applicator and source details against the prescription, plus lights and interlock): Practical rules include confirming identity and the applicator and source details against the prescription, wearing the issued personal monitor, and checking that warning lights and the door interlock work. Relying on others or on memory skips checks that are the therapist's responsibility. Leaving the personal monitor behind or trusting memory for source details are the tempting shortcuts that this question tests.
  142. The camera should be re-peaked and the flood repeated before any patient. A wider window lets in more scatter and lowers contrast, and fixing the data afterwards by software does not restore a mis-centred window.

The meal and imaging times were correct, so the study is valid and the physician decides how to interpret it. Reporting a diagnosis is outside scope, and an immediate repeat gives avoidable radiation with the same glucose. Sending the curve without the context hides the cause.
  186. The correct answer is A (2 m, worked out as 1 m × √(24 ÷ 6)): Dose rate follows the inverse square law, so the distance scales with the square root of the rate ratio: 1 m × √(24 ÷ 6) = 2 m. At 2 m the rate is 24 ÷ 4 = 6 µSv/h, which is where the plotted curve meets the target line. Treating the fall as inversely proportional to distance gives 4 m. Squaring the ratio gives 16 m, and inverting the ratio gives 0.5 m, a point closer to the patient where the rate is higher.
  187. The correct answer is B (Remove coat and gloves into waste, wash hand gently with lukewarm water and soap, resurvey): The hand reads 450 counts per second against an action level of 180, and the sleeve reads 1,900, so the person is contaminated and comes first. Contaminated clothing and gloves go into radioactive waste, then the skin is washed gently with lukewarm water and mild soap and resurveyed. Hard scrubbing can break the skin and drive activity in. The floor at 320 is also over the level but can follow, and waiting for decay leaves the skin exposed for hours.
  188. The correct answer is C (Report the failed linearity and stop using this calibrator for the high activity dose): At 22,000 MBq the instrument reads 13.8 percent low, outside the 10 percent tolerance, while the lower readings pass. Linearity shows whether the reading stays proportional across the range, so the high activity end cannot be trusted and a therapy dose lies there. The failed result goes to the person responsible for equipment and the calibrator is not used for that dose until corrected. A correction factor, acceptance of the other readings or a delayed repeat each rely on an unproven reading.
  189. The correct answer is D (About 13 days, worked out as (5 × 8) ÷ (8 − 5) days): The curve falls to 50 percent at 5 days, so the effective half-life is 5 days. From 1 ÷ Te = 1 ÷ Tp + 1 ÷ Tb, Tb = (Te × Tp) ÷ (Tp − Te) = (5 × 8) ÷ 3 = 13.3 days. Reading 5 days straight from the curve gives the effective value, which must be shorter than both other half-lives. Subtracting gives 3 days and multiplying gives 40 days, neither of which follows from the relation.
  190. The correct answer is C (About 5.8, worked out as 20 kBq/mL ÷ (253 000 kBq ÷ 74 000 g)): The injected activity must be decayed to the scan time: 370 × 2^(-60 ÷ 109.8) = 253 MBq, or 253 000 kBq. Dividing by 74 000 g gives 3.42 kBq/g, and SUV = 20 ÷ 3.42 = 5.8. Using the undecayed 370 MBq gives 4.0. Correcting upwards instead of downwards gives 2.7. Treating the 60 minutes as a full half-life leaves 185 MBq and gives 8.0.

Moving the isocentre or treating first both change or ignore an approved plan, and only the oncologist can approve a revised one. The waiting patient does not change this.
  192. The correct answer is B (The 12 MeV electron beam, because its dose stays high then falls steeply past the target.): The electron curve holds a high dose to about 3 cm and then falls to almost nothing by about 6 cm, so the lung behind the tumour receives little. The photon curve is still about 81 per cent at 6 cm and keeps falling slowly, which exposes that lung. Photon build-up also leaves the surface under-dosed, a further disadvantage for a skin target. Both curves peak within 3 cm, so reading only the peak hides the difference that decides the choice.

  196. The correct answer is A (Nineteen fractions and 38 Gy remain; the oncologist decides how the missed fraction is made up.): Dose per fraction is 66 ÷ 33 = 2 Gy. The record shows 5 + 5 + 4 = 14 fractions delivered, which is 28 Gy, so 33 − 14 = 19 fractions and 66 − 28 = 38 Gy remain. Counting the 15 scheduled fractions gives 18, which is the slip.   197. The correct answer is C (About 700 seconds, scaling the planned time by original activity over current activity.): Activity on day 90 is 370 × 0.5^(90 ÷ 73.8) = 159 GBq, which is about 43% of the original. Dose is proportional to activity × time, so time = 300 × 370 ÷ 159 = 699 seconds, about 700. Using the inverse ratio gives about 129 seconds. Doubling assumes exactly one half-life of 73.8 days, but 90 days is longer, so 600 seconds is too short.
  198. The correct answer is C (Dose fell to about 83% of planned, so record an incident and tell the oncologist.): Dose rate follows the inverse square law, so moving from 100 cm to 110 cm gives (100 ÷ 110)² = 0.826, about a 17% fall, as the curve shows at the second dashed line. Monitor units only count what the machine emits at a reference distance, so they do not correct for a wrong couch height. A dose error of this size is an incident that must be recorded and reported to the oncologist, not just noted.
  199. A target this shallow is therefore under-dosed by the planned beam. Higher energy increases skin sparing, so skin dose is lower, not higher. A photon beam does not stop abruptly, so the lung concern belongs to electrons. Bolus or a lower energy brings the build-up up to the scar.
